### [1] [RED: Adaptive Real-Time DAG Scheduling for Robotic Inference under Environmental Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24044)
*Zexin Li,Tao Ren,Johnathan Liu,Xiaoxi He,Cong Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RED is a real-time scheduling framework for multi-task DNN workloads on resource-constrained robots that adapts to environmental dynamics by using deadline-aware scheduling, sub-deadline assignment, and MIMONet graph reconstruction to preserve end-to-end timing guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots in dynamic environments face runtime changes (new tasks, shifting precedence, evolving workload structure) that degrade performance, especially under tight resource/real-time budgets for multi-task inference.

Method: RED proposes a deadline-aware scheduler assigning intermediate sub-deadlines to accommodate evolving computation graphs; integrates MIMONet weight-sharing via workload refinement and graph-reconstruction to align structure with schedulability requirements.

Result: Experiments on NVIDIA Jetson and Apple M-series MacBook show consistent gains over existing methods in throughput, deadline satisfaction, robustness to interference, adaptability, and runtime overhead.

Conclusion: RED effectively addresses real-time scheduling for multi-task DNN workloads under dynamic robotic environments while preserving timing guarantees and improving system efficiency.

Abstract: Robots deployed in dynamic environments must contend with environment-driven changes that reshape computation at runtime: new tasks may appear, precedence relations can shift, and overall workload structure evolves, all of which degrade performance, especially when multi-task inference is required under tight resource and real-time budgets. We present RED, a real-time scheduling framework for multi-task deep neural network workloads on resource-constrained robotic platforms that adapts to Robotic Environmental Dynamics (RED) while preserving end-to-end timing guarantees under modeling assumptions. The core of RED is a deadline-aware scheduler that assigns intermediate sub-deadlines, allowing it to accommodate evolving computation graphs and asynchronous inference induced by unpredictable conditions. The framework also supports flexible deployment of MIMONet (multi-input multi-output neural networks), commonly used in multi-tasking robots to alleviate memory pressure through weight sharing. RED explicitly leverages this shared-parameter property via a workload refinement and graph-reconstruction procedure that aligns MIMONet structure with schedulability requirements, improving compatibility and efficiency. We implement RED on NVIDIA Jetson family platforms and on an Apple M-series MacBook and evaluate it on navigation-oriented workloads representative of real robotic scenarios. Experiments show consistent gains over existing methods in throughput, deadline satisfaction, robustness to interference, adaptability, and runtime overhead.

</details>


### [2] [MASt3R-Nav: WayPixel Navigation in Relative 3D Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24111)
*Vansh Garg,Rohit Jayanti,Krish Pandya,Sarthak Chittawar,Siddharth Tourani,Muhammad Haris Khan,Sourav Garg,Madhava Krishna*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a novel map representation called pixel-relative connectivity for visual navigation, which balances geometric accuracy (via 3D grounded image matching and pixel correspondences) without requiring globally-consistent geometry. This enables a "WayPixel Costmap" for path planning and control, outperforming image- and object-level representations across four navigation tasks in simulation and real-world demos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing visual navigation representations either rely on globally-consistent 3D maps (geometrically accurate but brittle) or topological graphs (flexible but geometrically weak, limiting capability to teach-and-repeat). The authors aim to bridge this gap by creating a representation that is geometrically accurate yet does not require global geometric consistency.

Method: 1) Construct a map from an image sequence via inter-image connectivity using pixel correspondences in relative 3D coordinate systems (inspired by 3D grounded image matching). 2) Build a pixel-level graph, then approximate and sparsify intra-image pixel connectivity to derive a "WayPixel Costmap" representation. 3) Train a controller conditioned on this costmap to predict trajectory rollouts.

Result: The dense pixel-level costmap based on relative geometry is a more accurate conditioning variable for control prediction than image- or object-level counterparts. Validated on four navigation tasks in simulation and through real-world demonstrations.

Conclusion: Pixel-relative connectivity as a map representation effectively combines geometric accuracy with freedom from global consistency, enabling a highly capable navigation system that outperforms existing approaches.

Abstract: Visual navigation ability is strongly tied to its underlying representation of the world. Unlike classical 3D maps that require globally-consistent geometry, image- or object-relative topological graphs almost entirely do away with geometric understanding. But, this comes at the cost of navigation capability, often limiting it to merely teach-and-repeat. In this work, we propose a novel map representation in the form of pixel-relative connectivity, which is geometrically accurate but does not require global geometric consistency. Inspired by recent progress in 3D grounded image matching, we construct a map from an image sequence through inter-image connectivity based on pixel correspondences in the relative 3D coordinate systems of individual image pairs. We then use this pixel-level graph to perform global path planning by approximating and sparsifying intra-image pixel connectivity. Through this, we derive a ''WayPixel Costmap'' representation and train a controller conditioned on it to predict a trajectory rollout. We show that this dense pixel-level costmap based on relative geometry is a more accurate conditioning variable for control prediction than its image- and object-level counterparts. This enables a highly capable navigation system, as validated on four types of navigation tasks in the simulator and through real world demonstrations.

</details>


### [3] [Anisotropic Diffusion-Driven Ergodic Coverage in Multi-Robot Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24125)
*Thales C. Silva,Anoop Kiran,Nora Ayanian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces an anisotropic diffusion formulation for multi-robot ergodic search that generalizes previous isotropic heat equation and radial basis function approaches, using the Perona-Malik diffusion gradient to direct agent movement, with simulations demonstrating effectiveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional ergodic search algorithms (including heat equation-driven approaches) use isotropic diffusion that propagates error uniformly in all directions, regardless of changes in the desired distribution, lacking flexibility in directing multi-robot systems according to goal density variations.

Method: The authors propose a general class of anisotropic diffusion formulation for the ergodicity problem using the Perona-Malik diffusion, where agent movement is directed via the gradient of the solution. This formulation generalizes previous approaches including radial basis functions and the heat equation as a special case.

Result: Simulations in different scenarios demonstrate the methodology, showing that the anisotropic formulation outperforms isotropic approaches by better directing agents according to the goal density distribution.

Conclusion: The anisotropic diffusion formulation offers a more flexible and general framework for multi-robot ergodic search, with the heat equation as a special case, enabling more effective distribution-aware multi-agent coordination.

Abstract: We consider the problem of combining potential field and ergodic search on multi-robot systems. Traditional ergodic search algorithms use metrics for ergodicity that account for the desired distribution at different scales. Recently, a heat equation-driven ergodic approach was proposed, which adds flexibility to the smoothing of the ergodic metric. However, such an approach, as it is an isotropic diffusion, propagates the error uniformly in all directions, regardless of changes in the desired distribution. We introduce a general class of anisotropic diffusion formulation of the ergodicity problem, which generates a potential field for the ergodic search. We demonstrate that this approach generalizes previous results, which consider radial basis functions and the solution of the heat equation to represent the difference between the goal density distribution and the covered trajectories. In our solution, the agent movement is directed using the gradient of the solution of the Perona-Malik diffusion, and our formulation includes the heat equation as a special case. We demonstrate the methodology with a series of simulations in different scenarios.

</details>


### [4] [Investigating the Effect of a Series Elastic Actuation Retrofit to Black-Box Actuators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24127)
*Ivan Tregear,Ayhan Aktas,Ferdinando Rodriguez y Baena*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A custom Series Elastic Actuation module retrofitted to a black-box actuator improved force control bandwidth from 10.32 Hz to 30.32 Hz (2.93X), outperforming a commercial force sensor by 7.63% at a fraction of the cost (25 GBP).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional stiff actuators with minimal backlash limit compliance, causing poor disturbance rejection and force control in uncertain environments. SEA introduces compliance but typically reduces system bandwidth.

Method: A torsional SE element (stiffness 2155.4 Nm/rad) was designed via FE analysis and retrofitted to a black-box actuator. Open-loop and closed-loop force control bandwidth were measured using SEA feedback and a commercial force sensor.

Result: SEA increased bandwidth from 10.32 Hz to 30.32 Hz (2.93X improvement) and outperformed the commercial sensor by 7.63%, at a cost of only 25 GBP.

Conclusion: Retrofitting a low-cost custom SE element to a black-box actuator can significantly improve force control bandwidth and performance, surpassing expensive commercial sensors.

Abstract: In robotic applications, actuators are typically designed to be stiff with minimal backlash to ensure precision and repeatability. However, this limits compliance, leading to potential damage and poor force control in uncertain environments. Series Elastic Actuation (SEA) introduces compliance to enhance disturbance rejection and enable force measurement via Hooke's Law but reduces system bandwidth.
  A custom Series Elastic (SE) element was retrofitted to a black-box actuator to mitigate non-linearities like backlash and static friction. Integrating the SE element enabled high-fidelity force measurements, improving force control bandwidth and performance.
  A torsional SE element was designed through Finite Element (FE) analysis, yielding a stiffness of 2155.4 Nm/rad. Open-loop force control bandwidth was measured for the original motor and the SEA-integrated configuration, while closed-loop bandwidth was assessed using feedback from the SEA and a commercial force sensor. The SEA module increased bandwidth from 10.32 Hz to 30.32 Hz, a 2.93X improvement. Additionally, it outperformed the commercial sensor by 7.63% despite costing 25 GBP, a fraction of the price.

</details>


### [5] [Afford-VLA: Action-Aligned Visual Planning via Internalized Affordance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24203)
*Runze Wang,Yuqian Fu,Yu Li,Tao Lin,Tianwen Qian,Mohamed Elhoseiny,Bo Zhao,Yanwei Fu,Yu-Gang Jiang,Xiangyang Xue*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Afford-VLA internalizes task-conditioned affordance as a visual planning interface within VLA models using learnable <AFF> tokens, achieving state-of-the-art performance on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and SimplerEnv benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLA models suffer from insufficient spatial reasoning, especially for determining interaction regions; current visual planning approaches rely on global cues, symbolic representations, or externally generated signals that are weakly coupled with action prediction.

Method: Proposes Afford-VLA with learnable <AFF> tokens to query task-relevant interaction regions, decode affordance masks from multimodal features, convert them into compact embeddings for action generation, and jointly optimize affordance pathway with action prediction.

Result: Achieves consistent state-of-the-art performance on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and SimplerEnv simulation benchmarks, along with strong real-world results.

Conclusion: Internalizing affordance as action-aligned visual planning provides a powerful paradigm for improving VLA systems, with effective planning being local, visually grounded, internally generated, and directly aligned with action.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models have shown strong potential for generalist robot manipulation, yet they remain limited by insufficient spatial reasoning, particularly in determining where to interact in complex visual scenes. While recent efforts introduce various forms of visual planning to address this issue, existing approaches either rely on global geometric cues, symbolic intermediate representations, or externally generated visual signals, which are often weakly coupled with downstream action prediction. In this work, we revisit visual planning in VLA systems and argue that effective planning should be local, visually grounded, internally generated, and directly aligned with action. Based on this insight, we propose Afford-VLA, a unified framework that internalizes task-conditioned affordance as an explicit visual planning interface within VLA models. Concretely, we introduce learnable <AFF> tokens to query task-relevant interaction regions, decode affordance masks from multimodal features, and convert them into compact embeddings that directly condition action generation. This design enables affordance to be both generated and utilized within the VLA, forming a tightly coupled perception-action pathway. To further support this integration, we adopt a training strategy that allows the affordance pathway to be jointly optimized with action prediction, improving its effectiveness for downstream control. We evaluate our method on multiple simulation benchmarks, including LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and SimplerEnv, achieving consistent state-of-the-art performance, along with strong real-world results. These findings demonstrate that internalizing affordance as action-aligned visual planning provides a powerful paradigm for improving VLA systems.

</details>


### [6] [ECo-MoE: Embodiment-Conditioned Mixture of Experts Increases the Evolvability of Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24225)
*Yibin Wang,Muhan Li,Zihan Guo,Sam Kriegman*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a co-design framework for robots that jointly optimizes a distribution of latent design vectors (genotypes) and a mixture of control experts (neural modules) gated by latent coordinates of decoded phenotypes. The approach balances between per-robot policy training (inefficient) and monolithic universal controllers (overly conservative), enabling modular preservation of ancestral knowledge, selective activation of sensorimotor circuits, and integration of pretrained expert policies to guide evolution ("evo by demo").


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing co-design algorithms either train individual policies per robot (inefficient) or use a monolithic universal controller for all robots (overly conservative). The authors aim to find a middle ground that preserves ancestral knowledge in a unified yet modular framework.

Method: The method co-optimizes a distribution of latent design vectors (genotypes) and a mixture of control experts (neural modules) with gating based on latent coordinates of decoded phenotypes. This modular architecture allows different body plans to activate/deactivate different sensorimotor circuits, enabling partial controller overhaul without disrupting other expert modules.

Result: The approach enables scalable robot co-design where pretrained expert policies can be directly plugged into the mixture, which can steer evolution toward desired morphological traits in latent space.

Conclusion: The proposed "evo by demo" framework allows pretrained policies to guide freeform evolution toward canonical structures, providing a scalable and modular alternative to existing co-design approaches.

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce a model of evolution and learning in robots that co-optimizes a distribution of latent design vectors (genotypes) and a mixture of control experts (neural modules), which are gated by the latent coordinates of each decoded design (phenotype). This provides a scalable alternative to co-design algorithms that either train an individual policy for every robot, which is inefficient, or a monolithic universal controller for all robots, which results in overly conservative structures and behaviors. Our approach lies somewhere between these two extremes, preserving ancestral knowledge in a unified yet modular framework in which different body plans activate and deactivate different combinations of learned sensorimotor circuits for goal-directed behavior. This allows one part of the controller to be overhauled to better suit new species of designs as they emerge without disrupting the hard-earned knowledge contained within other expert modules. It also allows pretrained expert policies to be directly plugged into the mixture, which can steer evolution into otherwise unexplored areas of latent space containing desired morphological traits. We refer to this process as "evo by demo" and explore how it may be used to guide freeform evolution toward canonical structures defined by the pretrained model. Videos and code can be found at: https://eco-moe.github.io.

</details>


### [7] [AcroRL: Learning Aggressive Quadrotor Inversion using Bidirectional Thrust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24301)
*Gabriel Rodriguez,Henri Sayag,Abhishek Rathod,John Stecklein,Siddharth Saha,Christopher Barngrover,Wennie Tabib*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A learning-based framework using reinforcement learning policies enables compact, position-constrained quadrotor inversions with bidirectional thrust, reducing position RMSE by 32% and settling time by 57% over the best optimization baseline, demonstrated in simulation and hardware experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior geometric control approaches for bidirectional thrust quadrotors struggle with actuator saturation and motor reversal delay during inversions, requiring heuristic thrust posture scheduling and waypoint tuning, motivating a learning-based solution.

Method: Separate RL policies are trained via reinforcement learning for nominal-to-inverted and inverted-to-nominal transitions, modulating a constant reference trajectory to perform compact, position-constrained inversions while remaining compatible with traditional trajectory generation and tracking.

Result: In simulation, the method achieves the lowest position deviation and settling time, reducing position RMSE by 32% and settling time by 57% relative to the strongest optimization baseline. Hardware experiments show successful inversion across multiple yaw configurations with position RMSE below 0.35m and compatibility with circular flight in both regimes.

Conclusion: The proposed learning-based framework effectively addresses the limitations of prior geometric control approaches for bidirectional thrust quadrotors, achieving superior performance in compact inversions while maintaining compatibility with existing trajectory generation and tracking methods.

Abstract: Bidirectional thrust grants quadrotors a second equilibrium condition and increased control authority, expanding the envelope of possible aggressive maneuvers and enabling inverted flight, perching, and sensing. Prior geometric control approaches extend differential flatness through Hopf fibration-based attitude representations to support bidirectional thrust, but struggle with actuator saturation and motor reversal delay during inversions, requiring heuristic thrust posture scheduling and waypoint tuning. We propose a learning-based framework that modulates a constant reference trajectory to perform compact, position-constrained quadrotor inversions while remaining compatible with traditional trajectory generation and tracking across flight regimes. Separate policies are trained via reinforcement learning for nominal-to-inverted and inverted-to-nominal transitions. In JAX-based simulation, the proposed method achieves the lowest position deviation and settling time across all evaluated baselines, reducing position root mean square error (RMSE) by 32% and settling time by 57% relative to the strongest optimization-based baseline. Hardware experiments demonstrate successful inversion across multiple yaw configurations with position RMSE below 0.35m, and compatibility with downstream trajectory generation and control through circular flight in both regimes. Additionally, we provide an open-source implementation of the proposed framework.

</details>


### [8] [Terrain-Adaptive Grouser Wheel for Optimal Planetary Exploration: Design and Experimental Investigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24311)
*Vincent Griffo,Yashwanth Kumar Nakka*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces [Anonymized Robot Name], a planetary rover wheel with continuously adjustable grouser height. Evaluated across 750 trials on four granular terrains, adaptive deployment reduced slip by 30–58% and improved travel time and energy consumption by up to 77.4% compared to fixed configurations. A scaling analysis confirmed terrain granularity predicts optimal grouser height, highlighting the limitations of fixed-wheel systems for planetary exploration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Planetary rovers struggle with mobility across diverse extraterrestrial terrains (gradients, granularity). While multimodal wheel designs have explored stiffness, compliance, and diameter adjustments, full wheel grouser-adjustable designs remain largely unexplored. Grousers are particularly compelling because granular terrains require increased grouser height for better performance, motivating the need for an adaptive grouser mechanism.

Method: The authors designed [Anonymized Robot Name], a multimodal wheel capable of continuously adjusting grouser height. They conducted 750 experimental trials across four representative surfaces (vinyl flooring, coarse rock, pea gravel, and sand under two packing states) covering a range of granular conditions. A simplified scaling analysis was developed and validated using the trial data to relate terrain granularity to optimal grouser height.

Result: Adaptive deployment reduced slip by 30.0–58.0% and improved travel time and energy consumption by up to 77.4% in granular regimes compared to fixed configurations. No single grouser height minimized slip across all terrains. The scaling analysis suggested a relationship between terrain granularity and optimal grouser height.

Conclusion: Fixed-wheel systems commonly used in planetary exploration are fundamentally limited, as no single grouser height is optimal across diverse terrains. Grouser-adaptive morphology (exemplified by [Anonymized Robot Name]) is an effective solution for enhancing rover mobility across challenging extraterrestrial environments.

Abstract: Planetary rovers operating in extraterrestrial environments often encounter significant mobility challenges due to varying terrain features such as gradients and granularity. While recent works in multimodal wheel design have explored adjustments in stiffness, compliance, and diameter as a means to improve terrain adaptability, full wheel grouser-adjustable designs remain largely unexplored. Grousers are a compelling feature to actuate, as granular terrains tend to require increased grouser height for improved wheel performance. As a result, we introduce [Anonymized Robot Name], a multimodal wheel capable of continuously adjusting its grouser height for terrain adaptation. The platform was evaluated across four representative surfaces, including vinyl flooring, coarse rock, pea gravel, and sand under two packing states, spanning a range of granular conditions. Results from 750 experimental trials demonstrate that adaptive deployment reduces slip by 30.0--58.0\% and improves travel time and energy consumption by up to 77.4\% in granular regimes relative to fixed configurations. Using the terrain trial data, a simplified scaling analysis was developed and validated, suggesting a relationship between terrain granularity and optimal grouser height for the tested configuration. No single grouser height minimized slip across all terrains, underscoring the limitations of fixed-wheel systems commonly used for planetary exploration. This observation reinforces the potential of grouser-adaptive morphology, such as [Anonymized Robot Name], as an effective solution for enhancing rover mobility across diverse and mobility-challenging extraterrestrial environments.

</details>


### [9] [IsaacIPC: Coupling High-Fidelity Simulation and Realistic Rendering for Contact-Rich Robotic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24339)
*Qixin Liang,Zhongqing Han*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IsaacIPC is a GPU-accelerated robotic simulation framework coupling incremental potential contact (IPC) with IsaacSim/Lab, enabling real-time realistic rendering and tactile sensing via the geometric mortar contact potential (GMCP) for improved contact-pressure distribution resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable high-fidelity rigid-deformable robotic simulations with realistic rendering and accurate tactile contact-pressure distributions for data collection and policy evaluation.

Method: Couples GPU-accelerated incremental potential contact (IPC) with IsaacSim/Lab, maps deformation between simulation and visual meshes for rendering, and introduces geometric mortar contact potential (GMCP) as a barrier potential over contact samples on tactile surfaces.

Result: GMCP evaluated on contact benchmarks; IsaacIPC demonstrated on quadruped robot, dexterous hand, and UMI gripper simulations.

Conclusion: IsaacIPC and GMCP enable real-time realistic rendering and improved contact-pressure distribution resolution for rigid-deformable robotic simulations.

Abstract: We present IsaacIPC, a robotic simulation framework that couples GPU accelerated incremental potential contact (IPC) with IsaacSim/Lab. IsaacIPC maps simulated deformation between simulation and visual meshes, enabling real-time realistic rendering with applications to data collection and policy evaluation. For tactile sensing, we introduce the geometric mortar contact potential (GMCP), which defines a barrier potential over contact samples on tactile surfaces to better resolve contact-pressure distributions. We evaluate GMCP on contact benchmarks and demonstrate IsaacIPC on rigid-deformable robotic simulations including a quadruped robot, a dexterous hand, and a universal manipulation interface (UMI) gripper.

</details>


### [10] [PACT: Proactive Asking for Continual Task Assistance in Human-Robot Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24350)
*Chengbo He,Sheng Li,Chenyang Ma,Bochao Zou,Li Sun,Jiansheng Chen,Junliang Xing,Yuanchun Shi,Huimin Ma*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PACT is a proactive asking framework for continual human-robot collaboration that determines when to ask for clarification versus take action under partial observations, using cross-day interaction history to improve assistance accuracy and adapt to users over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic assistants in long-term HRI face challenges of partial observations and unknown human traits/routines at the start of collaboration, making passive infer-then-act approaches ineffective and inefficient.

Method: Proposes PACT (Proactive Asking for Continual Task Assistance), an ask-or-act framework that evaluates contextual sufficiency using current observations and accumulated interaction history, with a primary RL-based instantiation; introduces a clarification utility metric to trade off assistance accuracy vs. clarification frequency.

Result: Experiments in multi-day embodied collaboration scenarios show PACT consistently improves both assistance accuracy and clarification utility over passive inference baselines.

Conclusion: Proactive asking is crucial in continual human-robot collaboration; PACT enables robots to progressively adapt to users over time and provide more reliable assistance.

Abstract: Robotic assistants in long-term human-robot collaboration need to assist users under partial observations while leveraging cross-day interaction history. However, human traits and routines are often unknown at the beginning of collaboration, making passive infer-then-act assistance ineffective and inefficient. To address this challenge, we study a cross-day proactive asking setting for continual task assistance and propose PACT (Proactive Asking for Continual Task Assistance), an ask-or-act framework that determines whether clarification should be sought before taking action. PACT leverages current observations together with accumulated interaction history to evaluate contextual sufficiency, enabling the robot to provide more reliable assistance and progressively adapt to the user over time. We implement its primary learned instantiation using reinforcement learning and evaluate alternative instantiations under the same framework. To assess such behavior, we further introduce a clarification utility metric that quantifies the trade-off between assistance accuracy and the frequency of clarification requests. Experiments in multi-day embodied collaboration scenarios demonstrate that, compared with passive inference baselines, PACT consistently improves both assistance accuracy and clarification utility, highlighting the importance of proactive asking in continual human-robot collaboration.

</details>


### [11] [RoboHitch: Learning Visual Affordance from Disordered Keypoints for Hitch Knots Tying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24394)
*Jiahui Zuo,Boyang Zhang,Fumin Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RoboHitch is a framework for robotic hitch knot tying that uses only disordered 3D keypoints and RGB images, avoiding the need for explicit topological tracking. It employs a dynamic Graph Autoencoder and Convolutional Autoencoder with bidirectional cross-attention to predict pick and place affordances, demonstrating success under self-occlusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robotic knot tying methods rely on precise topological state tracking with ordered keypoints and explicit edge connectivity, which are prone to failures due to tracking drift and topology mismatch from repeated bending and crossings during knot formation.

Method: RoboHitch uses a dynamic Graph Autoencoder to extract geometric features from untracked (disordered) 3D keypoints, a Convolutional Autoencoder for visual context from RGB images, and a bidirectional cross-attention mechanism to fuse modalities for joint pick and place affordance prediction.

Result: Real-world experiments demonstrate effectiveness and generalizability, successfully completing hitch knots under self-occlusion scenarios.

Conclusion: The framework eliminates the need for explicit topological ordering and tracking, enabling more flexible and robust deformable linear object manipulation under occlusion.

Abstract: Robotic manipulation of deformable linear objects (DLOs) presents significant challenges due to complex dynamics and frequent self-occlusions. Existing robotic knot tying methods typically rely on precise topological state tracking with ordered keypoints and explicit edge connectivity. This reliance makes them prone to failures due to tracking drift and topology mismatch caused by repeated bending and crossings during knot formation.To address these limitations, we introduce RoboHitch, a novel framework that learns to perform hitch knot tying from human demonstrations using only disordered 3D keypoints and RGB images. This eliminates the need for explicit topological order, allowing for more flexible manipulation. Our method employs a dynamic Graph Autoencoder to extract geometric features from untracked keypoints, complemented by a Convolutional Autoencoder that captures essential visual context. A bidirectional cross-attention mechanism then fuses these modalities to jointly predict pick and place affordances, facilitating implicit reasoning about the rope's state and enabling knot tying under occlusion.Real-world experiments demonstrate the effectiveness and generalizability of our approach, successfully completing hitch knots in scenarios with self-occlusions.

</details>


### [12] [Smoother Action Chunking Flow Policy via Prior-Corrected Orthogonal Trust-Region Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24433)
*Kai Fang,Hailong Pei,Xuemin Chi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: POTR is a prior-corrected orthogonal trust-region guidance method for flow-matching robot policies that improves action continuity and success rate over existing RTC guidance by incorporating a data-prior scale into the guidance weight and constraining the perpendicular component of the correction vector within a trust region.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow-matching robot policies with action-chunking inference suffer from discontinuous action transitions at chunk boundaries. Existing RTC guidance has weak weight schedules at intermediate timesteps and its unconstrained correction direction can introduce transverse perturbations.

Method: POTR incorporates a data-prior scale σ_d into the RTC guidance weight for stronger intermediate-time correction, and decomposes the guidance vector into parallel/perpendicular components relative to denoising velocity, constraining the perpendicular component within a trust region.

Result: On LIBERO with π_{0.5}, POTR improves success rate and consistently reduces chunk-boundary discontinuity, acceleration, and jerk compared to RTC. The prior-corrected weight provides the main correction gain, while orthogonal trust region further improves stability.

Conclusion: POTR effectively addresses action discontinuity in flow-matching robot policies through a principled combination of prior-corrected weighting and orthogonal trust-region constraints, outperforming RTC on both success rate and motion smoothness metrics.

Abstract: Flow-matching robot policies commonly use action-chunking inference for efficient closed-loop control, but chunk boundaries can introduce discontinuous action transitions. Existing RTC guidance improves continuity by injecting correction signals during denoising, yet its weight schedule is weak at intermediate timesteps and its unconstrained correction direction may introduce transverse perturbations. We propose POTR, a **p**rior-corrected **o**rthogonal **t**rust-**r**egion guidance method. First, we incorporate a data-prior scale $σ_d$ into the RTC guidance weight, yielding stronger intermediate-time correction. Second, we decompose the guidance vector into components parallel and perpendicular to the denoising velocity, and constrain the perpendicular component within a trust region. On LIBERO with $π_{0.5}$, POTR improves success rate and consistently reduces chunk-boundary discontinuity, acceleration, and jerk compared with RTC. Ablations show that the prior-corrected weight provides the main correction gain, while the orthogonal trust region further improves stability.

</details>


### [13] [Vision-Guided Outdoor Flight and Obstacle Evasion via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24449)
*Shiladitya Dutta,Aayush Gupta,Varun Saran,Avideh Zakhor*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A novel sensorimotor policy using stereo-vision depth and VIO enables autonomous quadcopter navigation through unknown obstacles via reinforcement and privileged learning, achieving zero-shot sim-to-real transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadcopters require continuous pilot control in complex GNSS and telemetry-denied environments, limiting their autonomous deployment.

Method: A pre-trained autoencoder perception head + LSTM planning/control network outputs velocity commands; trained via two-stage process (supervised by optimal trajectories + curriculum fine-tuning) using reinforcement and privileged learning; sim-to-real gap addressed via domain randomization and reward shaping.

Result: Successful zero-shot transfer to unseen obstacle environments and drone platforms in outdoor experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed policy enables autonomous obstacle navigation in GNSS-denied settings without real-world training, demonstrating robustness to domain shift.

Abstract: Although quadcopters boast impressive traversal capabilities enabled by their omnidirectional maneuverability, the need for continuous pilot control in complex environments impedes their application in GNSS and telemetry-denied scenarios. To this end, we propose a novel sensorimotor policy that uses stereo-vision depth and visual-inertial odometry (VIO) to autonomously navigate through obstacles in an unknown environment to reach a goal point. The policy is comprised of a pre-trained autoencoder as the perception head followed by a planning and control LSTM network which outputs velocity commands that can be followed by an off-the-shelf commercial drone. We leverage reinforcement and privileged learning paradigms to train the policy in simulation through a two-stage process: 1) initial training with optimal trajectories generated by a global motion planner acting as a supervisory backbone, 2) further fine-tuning in a curriculum environment. To bridge the sim-to-real gap, we employ domain randomization and reward shaping to create a policy that is both robust to noise and domain shift. In outdoor experiments, our approach achieves successful zero-shot transfer to both obstacle environments and a drone platform that were never encountered during training.

</details>


### [14] [Polymander II: an amphibious salamander-inspired robot with contact and flow sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24465)
*Qiyuan Fu,Sudong Lee,Andrea Grillo,Jonathan Arreguit,Louis Gevers,Josie Hughes,Auke J. Ijspeert*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A salamander-inspired amphibious robot uses Hall-effect sensors to sense foot contact and lateral hydrodynamic forces at over 500 Hz, enabling robust amphibious locomotion and feedback control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Few amphibious robots can sense interactions with both terrestrial and aquatic environments; the authors aim to address this gap using compact, easily waterproofed Hall-effect sensors.

Method: Hall-effect sensors are embedded in a salamander-inspired robot to measure foot contact forces and lateral hydrodynamic forces; the system uses two bus lines for simultaneous exteroceptive (>500 Hz) and proprioceptive (100 Hz) sensing.

Result: The sensor system achieves high-frequency sensing (>500 Hz for exteroception, 100 Hz for proprioception) with compact, force-sensitive, and easily waterproofed Hall-effect sensors; the robot successfully traverses amphibious environments.

Conclusion: Hall-effect-based sensing enables amphibious robots to perceive both terrestrial and aquatic interactions, supporting feedback control for complex locomotion tasks.

Abstract: Robots benefit from sensory information to coordinate body movement, gain robustness against perturbations, and transit between different modes to adapt to various terrains. However, few amphibious robots can sense interactions with both terrestrial and aquatic environments. In this paper, we present a solution that uses Hall-effect sensors to sense foot contact forces and lateral hydrodynamic forces on a salamander-inspired amphibious robot. With two bus lines, the robot can simultaneously acquire this exteroceptive information at more than 500 Hz and proprioceptive information, such as joint positions and loads, at 100 Hz. The Hall-effect sensors used are compact, making them suitable for embedding in multiple positions within a robot, and exhibit high sensitivity to small forces. Moreover, because the sensor can be positioned separately from the measured object, waterproofing can be implemented with relative ease. Our tests demonstrate the robot's capabilities in traversing amphibious environments and its potential in using feedback control for more complex locomotion tasks.

</details>


### [15] [Elevator-LIO: Robust LiDAR-Inertial Odometry for Multi-Floor Navigation under Elevator-Induced Non-Inertial Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24495)
*Yifan Zhang,Yudong Huang,Yuchong Zhang,Changze Li,Haoran Liu,Ming Yang,Tong Qin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Elevator-LIO is a LiDAR-inertial odometry framework that enables continuous robot localization during elevator travel using a decoupled state-estimation model, mode-dependent Kalman filter, elevator mode manager, and adaptive voxel downsampling, achieving sub-1cm terminal height error in 17/79 elevator rides across 20 sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LIO systems fail to maintain continuous and stable localization during elevator travel because the elevator cabin is a non-inertial reference frame where conventional state-estimation assumptions break down, preventing cross-floor robotic tasks.

Method: (1) A decoupled state-estimation model separating robot-relative-to-elevator motion and elevator motion itself; (2) A mode-dependent iterated error-state Kalman filter that degrades to conventional LIO in ordinary environments and handles elevator states in non-inertial frames; (3) An elevator mode manager using LiDAR ranging statistics and estimated states for entry/exit detection, with event-triggered zero-velocity/zero-acceleration updates during stops; (4) Adaptive voxel downsampling for stable point counts under environmental scale changes.

Result: Tested on 20 real-world sequences with 79 elevator rides; terminal height error <1cm in 17 sequences; outperforms existing systems on elevator sequences; competitive on Hilti 2022/2023 standard indoor benchmarks.

Conclusion: Elevator-LIO is the first dedicated LIO framework for continuous robot localization during elevator travel, effectively handling challenges like large spaces, long vertical travel, pedestrian interference, and mirror reflections.

Abstract: This paper presents Elevator-LIO, a LiDAR-inertial odometry framework designed to achieve continuous robot localization during elevator travel, thereby supporting cross-floor robotic tasks. To address the state-estimation problem in non-inertial frames, Elevator-LIO establishes a decoupled state-estimation model that separately models the robot motion relative to the elevator and the elevator motion itself, and embeds it into a mode-dependent iterated error-state Kalman filter framework. This framework degenerates to conventional LIO estimation in ordinary indoor environments, while enabling the propagation and constrained update of elevator-related states in elevator non-inertial environments, thereby achieving continuous and stable localization. An elevator mode manager detects elevator entry and exit events using LiDAR ranging statistics and estimated states, and introduces event-triggered zero-velocity and zero-acceleration updates when the elevator stops to suppress accumulated vertical drift. In addition, this paper adopts an adaptive voxel downsampling strategy to maintain a stable number of effective points under significant environmental scale changes. We conduct extensive experiments on 20 real-world sequences containing 79 elevator rides, including practical challenges such as large-scale spaces, long vertical travel, dynamic pedestrian interference, and mirror reflections. The results show that Elevator-LIO maintains continuous localization accuracy in all sequences, with terminal height error below 1 cm in 17 sequences. In contrast, existing representative localization systems perform poorly on these elevator sequences. Tests on the Hilti 2022/2023 datasets further show that the proposed method remains competitive in standard indoor scenarios. The project page is available at https://xiaofan4122.github.io/Elevator_LIO_Page/.

</details>


### [16] [MuGen: Multi-Skill Generative Locomotion Controller for Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24592)
*Yusen Feng,Xiang Wang,Heyuan Yao,Zixi Kang,Xinyu Huo,Boyang Yu,Pengyun Qiu,Ruijie Zhao,Baoquan Chen,Libin Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MuGen is a data-driven framework that uses VQ-VAEs with model-based RL and teacher-student distillation to enable humanoid robots to learn and deploy multi-skill locomotion, tracking and mimicking unseen human motions from example sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable humanoid robots to perform expressive, human-like multi-skill locomotion guided by example motion sequences, capturing key patterns from heterogeneous human performance data.

Method: Vector-quantized autoencoders (VQ-VAEs) trained with model-based reinforcement learning to generate a latent representation of locomotion; teacher-student learning framework with a new policy distillation strategy for deployable student policy.

Result: The robot can track and mimic unseen human motions, reuse the learned latent space for other tasks, and demonstrates diverse motions with accurate execution.

Conclusion: MuGen effectively enables multi-skill locomotion on humanoid robots by learning a generative latent representation from human data, with successful transfer to deployment via policy distillation.

Abstract: This paper presents MuGen, a data-driven framework for learning and deploying multi-skill locomotion on humanoid robots. MuGen enables a robot to perform expressive motions like humans under the guidance of example motion sequences. To achieve this, we employ vector-quantized autoencoders (VQ-VAEs) trained with model-based reinforcement learning, resulting in a generative representation of locomotion that captures key patterns of human motion from hours of heterogeneous human performance data. We employ a teacher-student learning framework and develop a new policy distillation strategy to enable a deployable student policy learning this efficient latent representation. This policy allows the robot to track and mimic unseen human motions and further enables the robot to reuse the learned latent space for other tasks. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our framework through a diverse set of motions and accurate execution.

</details>


### [17] [PoseRefer: Pathway-Local Parameters for Semantically Grounded Reference Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24622)
*Anna Deichler*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MM-Conv captures natural co-speech gesture from dyadic VR interaction with full-body motion capture and 3D scene graphs. A decoupled late-fusion architecture (no shared parameters between pose and text pathways) is used for pose-language fusion. Frozen MiniLM category embeddings with fusion achieve 31.9% top-1, outperforming pose-only and text-only baselines. The learned scalar gate reveals opposing policies depending on text pathway category access, serving as a diagnostic for fusion-accuracy claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D grounding benchmarks fail to capture natural co-speech gesture in realistic settings: descriptions are written post-hoc, gestures are templated, or pointing is staged for the camera. MM-Conv addresses this by capturing natural dyadic VR interaction with full-body motion capture and 3D scene graphs.

Method: A decoupled late-fusion architecture is used where pose and text pathways share no learned parameters. The approach fuses pose input with frozen MiniLM category embeddings. A learned scalar gate controls modality fusion. Controlled ablations isolate contributions from category, pose, and text.

Result: Fusion with frozen MiniLM category embeddings achieves 31.9% top-1 accuracy, exceeding both pose-only and the best text-only pathway on every reference type. The learned scalar gate flips between opposing policies depending on whether the text pathway has category access.

Conclusion: Fusion-accuracy claims for semantic grounding systems are indistinguishable from category-representation artifacts unless pathways are architecturally decoupled. The decoupled design enables reliability diagnostics for grounding systems.

Abstract: A robot resolving ``put the cup on that one'' must fuse gesture, language, and scene geometry, yet 3D grounding benchmarks only partially capture this regime: descriptions are written post-hoc, gestures are templated, or pointing is staged for the camera. MM-Conv captures natural co-speech gesture from dyadic VR interaction alongside full-body motion capture and 3D scene graphs. We use it to evaluate pose-language fusion with a decoupled late-fusion architecture in which pose and text pathways share no learned parameters. The two choices together make category, pose, and text contributions easier to isolate through controlled ablations. Fusion with frozen MiniLM category embeddings exceeds pose alone and the best text-only pathway on every reference type, reaching 31.9% top-1. The learned scalar gate flips between opposing policies depending on whether the text pathway has category access. This is a reliability diagnostic: fusion-accuracy claims for semantic grounding systems are indistinguishable from category-representation artifacts unless pathways are architecturally decoupled.

</details>


### [18] [Sum of Costs Diffusion with Dynamic Guidance for Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24690)
*Aysu Aylin Kaplan,Özgür Erkent*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A diffusion model for robotic motion planning that generates collision-free trajectories by dynamically guiding the denoising process with gradient of collision costs, achieving robust generalization across diverse settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing motion planning methods (both classical and deep learning-based) struggle to generalize across diverse robotic manipulation settings.

Method: Uses diffusion models to generate collision-free trajectories, where denoising is guided by the gradient of total collision cost, with a dynamic approach for selecting the starting step of gradient guidance.

Result: Achieves highest performance on diverse test settings in the Mπnets dataset compared to competing methods, demonstrating robust generalization.

Conclusion: Dynamically guiding diffusion models with collision cost gradients overcomes generalization issues of existing methods and enables effective collision-free motion planning.

Abstract: The motion planning problem for robotic manipulation can be addressed through classical or deep learning approaches. Existing methods face significant challenges in generalizing to diverse settings. In this study, we present a method with high generalization capability that generates collision-free trajectories using diffusion models where the denoising process is guided by the gradient of the total collision cost. We are also presenting a dynamic approach for choosing start step of the gradient guidance. Experimental results demonstrate that guiding the diffusion model dynamically with the sum of collision costs offers more robust performance by overcoming the generalization issues faced by competing methods. The proposed model demonstrates its effectiveness by achieving the highest performance on diverse test settings in M$π$nets\ dataset among the compared methods.

</details>


### [19] [Geometric Workspace Analysis and Transmission-Aware Dynamics of a Serial Spherical Tool for Microsurgery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24760)
*Anestis Mablekos-Alexiou,Lyndon da Cruz,Christos Bergeles*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A kinematic and transmission-aware design framework for a serial spherical mechanism with an additional translational DOF for microsurgery, featuring analytical workspace formulation, dynamics-informed methodology for self-locking transmissions, and open-source software, validated on a vitreoretinal surgical robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable rapid, optimization-free design of spherical mechanisms for microsurgery by providing geometric workspace insights and torque requirement evaluation without relying on numerical methods.

Method: Analytical workspace formulation for rapid rotation axis selection; dynamics-informed methodology for self-locking transmission torque evaluation; open-source software package for friction identification and inverse dynamics analysis.

Result: Experimental validation on a purpose-built vitreoretinal surgical robotic tool confirmed the predictive capability of the models and demonstrated practical engineering design utility.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively supports design of spherical mechanisms for microsurgery by combining analytical workspace analysis with transmission-aware dynamics and open-source tooling.

Abstract: We present a kinematic and transmission-aware design framework for a serial spherical mechanism with an additional translational degree of freedom for microsurgery. The first contribution is an analytical workspace formulation that provides geometric insight into reachable motion and enables rapid selection of rotation axis orientations without numerical optimization. The second contribution is a dynamics-informed methodology for mechanisms driven by self-locking transmissions, supporting evaluation of torque requirements for a prescribed workspace geometry. The framework is accompanied by an open-source software package for friction identification and inverse dynamics analysis. Experiments on a purpose-built robotic tool for vitreoretinal surgery validate the predictive capability of the models and demonstrate their practical utility for engineering design.

</details>


### [20] [Enhanced INS/GNSS State Estimation using GNSS-Based Acceleration Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24767)
*Gal Versano,Itzik Klein*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes using past GNSS measurements with a motion model to derive vehicle acceleration, then integrating this into INS/GNSS fusion filters to improve navigation accuracy, achieving 11-20% RMSE improvements on real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard INS/GNSS fusion relies on GNSS position updates, which provide limited observability of orientation and inertial sensor error states, especially during low-dynamic motion.

Method: Utilizing past GNSS measurements alongside a motion model to extract meaningful vehicle acceleration information, then integrating this acceleration measurement into the INS/GNSS filter.

Result: Consistent positioning accuracy improvements relative to the standard position-aided filter, with mean position RMSE improvements of 11.40% and 20.74% on two real-world UGV datasets.

Conclusion: The proposed acceleration-aided INS/GNSS fusion approach improves robustness and accuracy, particularly during low-dynamic motion where standard methods have limited observability.

Abstract: Accurate and reliable navigation is essential for autonomous ground vehicle operations. Standard INS/GNSS fusion relies on GNSS position updates, which provide limited observability of orientation and inertial sensor error states, particularly during low-dynamic motion. In this work, we propose utilizing past GNSS measurements alongside a motion model to extract meaningful vehicle acceleration information. This acceleration measurement is then integrated into the INS/GNSS filter to improve its robustness and accuracy. The proposed approach is evaluated on two real-world unmanned ground vehicle datasets collected from different mobile platforms and inertial sensor grades. Results demonstrate consistent positioning accuracy improvements relative to the standard position-aided filter, with mean position root mean square error improvements of 11.40 % and 20.74 % on the two datasets, respectively.

</details>


### [21] [MR-LiDAR: A Multi-Resolution Roadside LiDAR Benchmark for Perception Diagnostics and Deployment Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24777)
*Shunlai Cui,Peng Cao,Yuan Zhu,Yongjiang He,Jiacheng Yin,Xiao Huo,Gang Cao,Xiaobo Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MR-LiDAR is a controlled multi-resolution LiDAR benchmark for roadside perception diagnostics. Using 16-, 32-, 80-, and 128-beam LiDARs in identical roadside scenarios, the study reveals that beam count, beam distribution, target distance, object category, and vehicle occlusion significantly impact perception performance. Notably, an 80-beam LiDAR with optimized beam distribution can match or outperform a 128-beam LiDAR with uniform distribution. The work provides empirical benchmarks and practical guidance for cost-effective LiDAR selection in roadside sensing systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LiDAR model selection critically affects both perception capability and deployment cost in roadside sensing systems, yet no empirical benchmarks exist for comparing perception performance across different LiDAR configurations, constraining scientific sensor selection and deployment planning.

Method: The authors constructed MR-LiDAR, a controlled benchmark using 16-, 32-, 80-, and 128-beam LiDARs in identical roadside scenarios, collecting point clouds and ground-truth annotations for vehicles and VRUs across varying distances. They performed systematic empirical analyses examining how beam count, beam distribution, target distance, object category, and vehicle occlusion affect perception performance using two widely used detection algorithms.

Result: All examined factors (beam count, beam distribution, target distance, object category, vehicle occlusion) substantially impact perception performance. Critically, an 80-beam LiDAR with optimized beam distribution can match or outperform a 128-beam LiDAR with uniform beam distribution, challenging the common assumption that higher beam counts always yield better perception. The paper also provides target point-count statistics and detection performance comparisons.

Conclusion: This work delivers a diagnostic benchmark and practical reference guide for determining cost-effective LiDAR configurations in roadside perception applications, enabling informed sensor selection and deployment planning.

Abstract: LiDAR model selection is a critical issue in roadside sensing systems, as it directly determines both perception capability and deployment cost. However, the lack of empirical benchmarks for comparing perception performance across different LiDAR configurations has greatly constrained scientific sensor selection and deployment planning. To address this gap, we present MR-LiDAR, a controlled multi-resolution LiDAR benchmark for roadside perception diagnostics. Using 16-, 32-, 80-, and 128-beam LiDARs in identical roadside scenarios, we collect point clouds and ground-truth annotations for diverse traffic participants, including vehicles and vulnerable road users (VRUs), across varying distances. This controlled design isolates intrinsic LiDAR specifications, particularly beam count and beam distribution, as the key variables for precise performance diagnostics. Based on MR-LiDAR, we conduct systematic empirical analyses to examine how beam count, beam distribution, target distance, object category, and vehicle occlusion affect LiDAR perception performance. The results reveal that all of these factors have substantial impacts. In particular, contrary to the common assumption that higher beam counts always yield better perception, we show that an 80-beam LiDAR with optimized beam distribution can match or even outperform a 128-beam LiDAR with uniform beam distribution. In addition, we provide a practical reference guide for LiDAR selection, including target point-count statistics and detection performance comparisons based on two widely used detection algorithms. This work offers a diagnostic benchmark and practical guidance for determining cost-effective LiDAR configurations in roadside perception applications.

</details>


### [22] [Manifold-Constrained MPPI: Real-Time Sampling-Based Control Under Hard Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24813)
*Seulchan Lee,Sanghyun Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MC-MPPI combines Variational Autoencoders with MPPI to enforce manifold-based equality constraints in real-time sampling-based control, demonstrated on a 14-DoF dual-arm system at 100 Hz.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MPPI uses cost-based soft penalties that cannot guarantee hard-constraint satisfaction, limiting its applicability to highly constrained tasks like closed-chain manipulation.

Method: Decouples constrained optimal control into latent-space planning (VAE learns low-dimensional manifold representation for MPPI trajectory generation) and execution-level correction (QP controller resolves residual manifold mismatch in a single solve).

Result: Operates stably at 100 Hz on a 14-DoF closed-chain dual-arm system in both simulation and real-world settings, reliably maintains hard equality constraints in dynamic environments, and significantly outperforms baselines in tracking accuracy.

Conclusion: MC-MPPI effectively enforces hard equality constraints in real-time sampling-based control while preserving MPPI's computational advantages, enabling application to highly constrained robotic tasks.

Abstract: Sampling-based model predictive control methods, such as Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI), offer derivative-free optimization and robustness in complex robotic systems. However, standard MPPI relies on cost-based soft penalties that cannot guarantee hard-constraint satisfaction, severely limiting its applicability to highly constrained tasks such as closed-chain manipulation. To address this, we propose Manifold-Constrained MPPI (MC-MPPI), a real-time sampling-based control framework that enforces manifold-based equality constraints while preserving the computational advantages of MPPI. The key idea is to decouple the constrained optimal control problem into latent-space planning and execution-level correction. At the planning stage, a Variational Autoencoder (VAE) learns a low-dimensional latent representation of the constraint manifold, enabling MPPI to efficiently generate near-feasible candidate trajectories without per-sample modification. Since this reference enables accurate linearization of the equality constraints, an execution-level Quadratic Programming (QP) controller resolves the residual manifold mismatch in a single solve rather than through iterative projection. Experiments on a 14-DoF closed-chain dual-arm system in both simulation and real-world settings demonstrate that MC-MPPI operates stably at 100 Hz, reliably navigates dynamic environments while effectively maintaining hard equality constraints, and significantly outperforms baseline methods in tracking accuracy. Supplementary videos and implementation details are available at https://rcilab.github.io/mcmppi.

</details>


### [23] [Learning Transferable Motor Skills for Geometry-Aware Robotic Surface Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24881)
*Miroslav David,Karla Stepanova,Robert Babuska*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A modular framework decouples geometric motion planning from execution expertise by using interpretable atomic motor rules (e.g., velocity scaling, orientation offsets) inferred via a multimodal neural network from kinematic and CAD data, improving robotic surface-interaction tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic surface-interaction tasks (e.g., spray painting, welding) require both accurate geometric planning and precise motor execution, but existing methods either lack expert motor patterns (pure geometric planners) or tightly couple execution to specific training geometries (LfD), limiting transferability.

Method: A modular framework that decouples geometric motion planning from execution-level expertise. Expert behavior is encoded as a vocabulary of interpretable atomic motor rules (e.g., velocity scaling, orientation offsets) that systematically modify a geometrically planned reference path. A multimodal neural network is trained to infer rule parameters jointly from kinematic trajectory data and CAD model geometry.

Result: Evaluated via dynamic simulation on L-shaped and window-shaped objects, the model successfully extracts velocity and orientation rules across both topologies.

Conclusion: The proposed decoupling of geometric planning and execution expertise via interpretable motor rules shows promise for transferable skill learning in robotic surface-interaction tasks.

Abstract: Robotic surface-interaction tasks, such as spray painting or welding, require both accurate geometric planning and precise motion execution. While modern motion planners generate valid geometric paths, they often lack the expert motor patterns observed in human operators. Conversely, learning from demonstration often tightly couples task execution to the specific training geometry, limiting transferability. We propose a modular framework that decouples geometric motion planning from execution-level expertise. Expert behavior is represented as a vocabulary of interpretable, atomic motor rules, such as velocity scaling and orientation offsets, that systematically modify a geometrically planned reference path. We train a multimodal neural network to infer rule parameters jointly from kinematic trajectory data and CAD model geometry. We evaluate our approach through dynamic simulation on L-shaped and window-shaped objects, demonstrating on simulated data that the model successfully extracts velocity and orientation rules across both topologies.

</details>


### [24] [MuJoCoUni:Persistent Batched Runtime Primitives for MuJoCo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24922)
*Yufei Jia,Junzhe Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MuJoCoUni extends MuJoCo with a high-throughput BatchEnvPool executor for online robot learning, providing stateful batched environment stepping, sparse resets, domain randomization, and batched sensor/Jacobian queries via C++/pybind11, all while preserving upstream CPU semantics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable high-throughput parallel online robot learning and batched physics evaluation while retaining upstream MuJoCo CPU semantics, filling the gap beyond open-loop batched trajectory generation already provided by mujoco.rollout.

Method: BatchEnvPool — a C++/pybind11 executor with per-environment mjModel copies, per-thread mjData workers, and an internal thread pool. Provides final-state-only short stepping, sparse reset, reset-lifecycle domain randomization, batched sensor forward evaluation, and batched Jacobian/height-field queries, all confined to the Python binding layer without altering MuJoCo's core solver, contact model, integrator, or source tree.

Result: A validated open-source package (pip install mujoco-uni) shipped with benchmark scripts, enabling stateful batched environment execution for online learning workloads.

Conclusion: MuJoCoUni successfully extends MuJoCo's capabilities for online robot learning with high-throughput parallel execution, preserving upstream semantics while adding runtime primitives for stateful environment execution and batched queries.

Abstract: We present MuJoCoUni, a downstream MuJoCo distribution for online robot learning and batched physics evaluation. Alongside the open-loop batched trajectory generation already provided by upstream mujoco.rollout, MuJoCoUni supplies runtime primitives for stateful environment execution. The target workloads need high-throughput parallel execution while retaining upstream CPU MuJoCo semantics for models, sensors, contact, and constraints. Its core object, BatchEnvPool, is a C++/pybind11 executor that owns per-environment mjModel copies, per-thread mjData workers, and an internal thread pool. It provides final-state-only short stepping, sparse reset, reset-lifecycle domain randomization, batched sensor forward evaluation without advancing dynamics, and batched Jacobian and height-field queries. The implementation is confined to the Python binding layer; MuJoCo's solver, contact model, integrator, and core source tree retain upstream semantics. This report describes the BatchEnvPool API, implementation boundary, relationship to rollout, and the validation and benchmark scripts shipped with the open-source mujoco-uni package, which is installed with \texttt{pip install mujoco-uni}.

</details>


### [25] [Dynamic Neural Koopman Distillation for Real-Time Robot Control Using Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24924)
*Lei Zheng,Peiqi Yu,Zengqi Peng,Changliu Liu,Armin Lederer*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Distills multistep diffusion model inference into a single forward pass using a Factorized Dynamic Koopman layer for fast, multimodal robotic planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models generate high-quality diverse trajectories but their iterative denoising is too slow for high-frequency closed-loop control.

Method: Proposes Dynamic Neural Koopman Distillation with a Factorized Dynamic Koopman layer that learns a factorized latent transition with state-dependent modal gains, compressing multistep diffusion into one forward pass.

Result: Outperforms existing one-step distillation methods on D4RL MuJoCo locomotion; reduces inference latency to milliseconds; enables smooth closed-loop execution on a physical Kinova manipulator.

Conclusion: The proposed distillation framework effectively bridges the speed-expressivity gap, enabling diffusion-based planning for real-time closed-loop control.

Abstract: Diffusion models excel at generating diverse and multimodal trajectories for robotic planning, yet their iterative denoising process introduces latency that is incompatible with high-frequency closed-loop control. To address this problem, we propose Dynamic Neural Koopman Distillation, a framework that distills multistep diffusion inference into a single forward pass while retaining the multimodal expressivity of the teacher model. Specifically, we introduce a Factorized Dynamic Koopman layer that models the denoising process through a factorized latent transition with state-dependent modal gains. We evaluate the proposed method on standard D4RL MuJoCo locomotion benchmarks and a physical Kinova manipulator, comparing against one-step baselines. The results show that our method significantly outperforms existing one-step distillation approaches on the reported locomotion tasks, and reduces the inference latency to the millisecond regime compared with the teacher policy. Hardware experiments further demonstrate that our method enables smooth and fast closed-loop execution while maintaining task success and comparable accuracy. A project page is available at https://fdkoopman.github.io/.

</details>


### [26] [Learning High-Frequency Continuous Action Chunks in Latent Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24931)
*Kunyun Wang,Yuhang Zheng,Yupeng Zheng,Jieru Zhao,Wenchao Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes shifting high-frequency action learning from action space to a latent VAE space with a Reuse-then-Refine chunk-level strategy, enabling smooth, continuous, and consistent high-frequency (60 Hz) control for contact-rich robotic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing action chunking policies work well at moderate frequencies but fail to maintain temporal smoothness and spatial consistency at high action frequencies (e.g., 60 Hz), leading to jerky motions and execution pauses.

Method: (1) Shifts high-frequency action learning from action space to a latent space using variational autoencoder (VAE); (2) Introduces Reuse-then-Refine, a chunk-level refinement strategy to improve continuity between adjacent action chunks under asynchronous inference.

Result: On three real-world contact-rich robotic tasks, the approach consistently completes tasks with smooth motions, fewer pauses, and less jerky behavior compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Learning high-frequency actions in a VAE latent space combined with chunk-level refinement enables robots to execute complex contact-rich tasks continuously and smoothly, overcoming the limitations of action-space chunking at high frequencies.

Abstract: Modern robotic policies increasingly rely on action chunking to execute complex tasks in the physical world. While action chunking improves temporal consistency at moderate action frequencies, it becomes insufficient when the action frequency is further increased (e.g., to 60~Hz). At such high frequencies, policies often fail to generate actions that are both temporally smooth and spatially consistent. We address this challenge by shifting high-frequency action learning from the action space to a latent space with variational autoencoder (VAE). This formulation significantly improves both temporal and spatial consistency of high-frequency control. To enable smooth real-time execution, we further introduce Reuse-then-Refine, a chunk-level refine strategy that improves continuity between adjacent action chunks under asynchronous inference. As a result, robots controlled by our policy can execute complex contact-rich tasks continuously, with less pauses and jerky motions. Experiments on three real-world contact-rich robotic tasks show that our approach consistently completes tasks with smooth motions. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/tars-robotics/RTR.

</details>


### [27] [HumanEgo: Zero-Shot Robot Learning from Minutes of Human Egocentric Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24934)
*Zhi,Wang,Botao He,Kelin Yu,Seungjae Lee,Ruohan Gao,Furong Huang,Yiannis Aloimonos*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HumanEgo is a robot-data-free framework that bridges the human-to-robot embodiment gap by lifting human egocentric videos to entity-level hand-object interaction representations and training a flow matching policy with dense auxiliary supervision, achieving 92.5% success across four real-world tasks with only 30 minutes of human video data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human egocentric video captures rich manipulation demonstrations without robot hardware, but transferring these skills to robots is challenging due to the embodiment gap between human and robot in visual appearance and kinematics. Existing methods require robot-specific data collection.

Method: HumanEgo lifts each human demonstration to an entity-level representation of hand-object interaction, and trains a flow matching policy with dense auxiliary objectives that amplify supervision from every trajectory.

Result: With 30 minutes of human videos per task, achieves 92.5% average success (75% with 15 minutes), outperforms matched-time robot teleoperation by 41%, and robustly transfers zero-shot across novel robots, cameras, and environments.

Conclusion: HumanEgo is robot-data-free, hardware-agnostic, data-efficient, and zero-shot human-to-robot transferable, effectively bridging the embodiment gap without requiring any robot-specific data.

Abstract: Human egocentric video captures rich manipulation demonstrations without any robot hardware, yet transferring these skills to robots remains challenging due to the embodiment gap between human and robot in both visual appearance and kinematics. We present HumanEgo, a framework that bridges the embodiment gap by lifting each human demonstration to an entity-level representation of hand-object interaction, and training a flow matching policy with dense auxiliary objectives that amplify supervision from every trajectory. HumanEgo is robot-data-free, hardware-agnostic, data-efficient, and zero-shot human-to-robot transferable. With only 30 minutes of human videos per task, HumanEgo achieves 92.5% average success across four real-world tasks (75% with just 15 minutes), outperforms matched-time robot teleoperation by 41%, and robustly transfers zero-shot across novel robots, cameras, and environments.

</details>


### [28] [Bridging the Gap: Enabling Soft Actor Critic for High Performance Legged Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24975)
*Gianluca Sabatini,Chenhao Li,Marco Hutter*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper identifies why SAC underperforms PPO in massively parallel robot training, proposes targeted modifications (policy initialization, timeout-aware critic targets, multi-step returns), and shows SAC can match PPO's performance across multiple legged robot platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PPO is sample-inefficient due to its on-policy nature, limiting real-world adaptation and fine-tuning. SAC is off-policy and more sample-efficient but fails to match PPO in massively parallel simulation settings. The paper aims to bridge this performance gap.

Method: The authors identify root causes of SAC's poor performance at scale and introduce three targeted modifications: (1) improved policy initialization, (2) timeout-aware critic targets, and (3) multi-step return estimation.

Result: The modified SAC closes the performance gap with PPO entirely, evaluated across multiple legged robot platforms and diverse locomotion tasks.

Conclusion: With proper modifications, SAC can be as effective as PPO in massively parallel training while retaining its off-policy sample efficiency advantages for sim-to-real transfer.

Abstract: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) has become the de facto standard for training legged robots, thanks to its robustness and scalability in massively parallel simulation environments like IsaacLab. However, its on-policy nature makes it inherently sample-inefficient, preventing its use for continuous adaptation and fine-tuning on real hardware. Soft Actor-Critic (SAC), by contrast, is an off-policy algorithm that can reuse past experience, making it a natural candidate for sim-to-real transfer workflows where the same algorithm can be used both in simulation and for online learning on the real robot. Despite these advantages, SAC has consistently failed to match PPO's empirical performance in massively parallel training settings. This work identifies the root causes of this gap and introduces targeted modifications, covering policy initialization, timeout-aware critic targets, and multi-step return estimation, that enable SAC to train stably at scale. Evaluated across multiple legged robot platforms and diverse locomotion tasks, our approach closes the performance gap with PPO entirely.

</details>


### [29] [Loosely Coupled Factor Graph Optimization for Pseudolite-Augmented Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24980)
*Chih-Chun Chen,Lipeng Tan,Shiyu Bai,Heike Vallery*

Main category: cs.RO
