We study the opposite extreme, a regime common in practice but overlooked in theory, which we name Zero-Knowledge MRTA (ZK-MRTA): a robot team with no prior knowledge (no task models, not even the latent rank), no communication (no messages, no parameter sharing, no coordinator), and only a partial and privately-noisy view of a public stream of teammates' outcomes. A hidden low-rank structure governs which robot suits which task, and there are far more tasks than rounds, so most (robot, task) pairs are never attempted. Yet each robot can act well on tasks it never attempted, and onboard new tasks, by running online low-rank collaborative filtering over the broadcast (SwarmCF). The advantage over any structure-free learner is categorical, not a constant factor: a structure-free learner is provably at the prior-mean error floor on unseen pairs. We prove a matching per-robot sample complexity (Θ(d) versus Θ(n), in the rank d and the task count n), an anytime (cumulative-reward) separation under task scarcity, and a deterministic condition under which decentralized recovery from the masked broadcast is exact (validated empirically). Experiments quantify the value of the broadcast, a positive scaling law (per-robot unseen-pair skill rises with team size), and the strongest masking-robustness and anytime profile among low-rank methods, recovering most (about 80% on earned skill) of a centralized full-communication ceiling, and holding under capacity-1 contention and in a robotics-grounded sensing instance.

</details>


### [56] [Compliant Non-Prehensile Pushing Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25672)
*Francesco Cufino,Mario Selvaggio,Fabio Amadio,Fabio Ruggiero*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper extends a pushing model for impedance-controlled robots using MPC with an energy tank passivity filter, enabling compliant, non-prehensile pushing operations that are safe for human-robot interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable safe non-prehensile pushing operations in human-populated environments by ensuring robots comply with external interactions and exhibit passive behavior, preventing unsafe force buildup.

Method: Extends a state-of-the-art pushing model for impedance-controlled robots; develops an MPC framework that modulates robot position/velocity set-points to realize pushing force and contact point adaptation; integrates an energy tank passivity filter to modulate velocity and prevent unbounded energy accumulation.

Result: Rigorously tested in simulation and experimentally validated on two different robotic systems, demonstrating passive compliance during human-robot interactions, trajectory tracking performance, and robustness to variations in object physical parameters.

Conclusion: The proposed compliant pushing framework with passivity guarantees enables safe and robust non-prehensile manipulation in human-populated environments.

Abstract: In this paper, we address the challenge of performing non-prehensile pushing operations with a compliant robotic manipulation system. To ensure safe operations in human-populated environments, robots must comply with external physical interactions and exhibit passive behavior. To achieve this, we extend a state-of-the-art pushing model to integrate it with impedance-controlled robots. We develop a model predictive control framework built upon this model that enables compliant pushing through optimal modulation of the robot's position/velocity set-point, jointly realizing the required pushing force and contact point adaptation to obtain desired object motion. However, external interactions may induce tracking errors, causing a consequent potentially indefinite increase of the pushing force. To prevent this, we integrate an energy tank passivity filter that further modulates the robot velocity set-point to guarantee passivity and avoid uncontrolled energy buildup. The proposed method has been rigorously tested in simulation and validated through experiments on two different robotic systems, demonstrating passive compliance during human-robot interactions and assessing trajectory tracking performance and robustness to variations in the object's physical parameters.

</details>


### [57] [HumanFlow -- Diffusion-Driven MAV Navigation Among Humans via Tightly-Coupled Motion Tracking, Forecasting, and Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25685)
*Simon Schaefer,Joshua Näf,Stefan Leutenegger*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HumanFlow is a latent diffusion model for unified human motion tracking and forecasting conditioned on 3D scene context, which produces smooth/accurate predictions under occlusions and outperforms SOTA in tracking efficiency. Its latent space is coupled with a flow-matching-based MPC policy for MAV social navigation, achieving collision-free navigation under partial observability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots need robust and accurate human perception in 3D scenes for safe integration, but existing approaches fail under occlusions or partial visibility, producing implausible motion estimates inconsistent with the scene.

Method: HumanFlow uses a latent diffusion model that unifies motion tracking and forecasting conditioned on 3D scene context. Its latent representation is coupled with a flow-matching-based approximate MPC policy for control.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art in tracking accuracy while being significantly more efficient; produces smooth/accurate predictions under heavy occlusions; achieves superior MAV social navigation and remains collision-free under partial observability.

Conclusion: HumanFlow provides a robust, efficient framework for human-aware robot navigation by tightly coupling scene-conditioned motion prediction with MPC-based control, improving both safety and performance under challenging perceptual conditions.

Abstract: Robust and accurate perception of humans in their 3D scene context is essential for integrating robots into everyday environments. Existing approaches, however, often fail to predict plausible and accurate human motion estimates that are consistent with the surrounding scene, especially in the presence of heavy occlusions or partial visibility. This can limit both safety and efficiency for robotic operations. We introduce HumanFlow, a latent diffusion model that unifies human motion tracking and forecasting, conditioned on the 3D scene context. We show that our human motion model produces smooth and accurate predictions under challenging conditions, including heavy occlusions, and outperforms state-of-the-art methods in tracking accuracy while being significantly more efficient. Furthermore, we show how HumanFlow's latent space can be tightly coupled with control by conditioning a flow-matching-based, approximate MPC policy on these representations. We validate our policy in simulation with real human trajectories for MAV social navigation, demonstrating superior navigation performance and remaining collision-free, even under partial observability of the human.

</details>


### [58] [Implicit Null-space Manifold Generation for Redundant Robotic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25770)
*Taiki Ishigaki,Teresa Vidal-Calleja,Ko Ayusawa,Eiichi Yoshida*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes an implicit scalar field representation to model solution manifolds for redundant robotic systems, using Jacobian-guided sampling to capture the geometry of configuration spaces that achieve the same task outcome, demonstrated on planar and 7-DOF manipulators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Jacobian-based redundancy resolution methods compute individual solutions or trajectories but fail to retain the geometric structure of the entire solution set (manifold) in configuration space, motivating a representation-centric approach.

Method: Construct an implicit scalar field over configuration space whose zero-level set corresponds to the solution manifold; use Jacobian-guided exploration to generate samples near the manifold, capturing both local and global structure efficiently.

Result: Demonstrated effectiveness on a planar three-link robot and a 7-DOF Franka manipulator; the framework also enables consistent modeling of solution spaces across continuously varying task families.

Conclusion: The implicit representation provides a continuous distance field encoding proximity to the solution manifold, preserving the geometric structure of solution sets for redundant robots and extending to task families.

Abstract: Robotic systems with redundant degrees of freedom can achieve the same task outcome using multiple configurations, resulting in solution sets that form manifolds in the configuration space. Existing approaches typically exploit such redundancy locally through Jacobian-based techniques to compute individual solutions or trajectories. While effective for solution computation, these methods do not retain a representation of the geometry of the solution set itself. In this work, we adopt a representation-centric approach to estimate the geometric structure of the solution space. We consider solution manifolds induced by general task-defining maps and construct an implicit scalar field over the configuration space, whose zero-level set corresponds to the solution manifold. To this end, we generate samples in the neighborhood of the solution manifold using a Jacobian-guided exploration strategy, which efficiently captures its local and global structure. The resulting implicit representation is defined over the configuration space and naturally induces a continuous, distance field that encodes proximity to the solution manifold. Experiments on a planar three-link robot and a seven-degree-of-freedom Franka manipulator demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed representation. Furthermore, the framework enables consistent modeling of solution spaces across families of tasks with continuous variation.

</details>


### [59] [ParkourFormer: Integrating Predictive Supervision and Sequence Modeling into Parkour Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25782)
*Yanheng Mai,Wenhao Xu,Zirui Huang,Yifei Fu,Shengwei Dong,Xinjue Wang,Kailun Huang,Yanzhe Xie,Renjing Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ParkourFormer uses Transformer-based sequence modeling with explicit future-state prediction to achieve 93.85% traversal success rate in humanoid parkour, outperforming baselines by up to 42.73% across diverse terrains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL policies for humanoid parkour are reactive (mapping observations directly to actions) without explicitly modeling future body states, which is critical for anticipating contact transitions and body dynamics in agile locomotion tasks.

Method: ParkourFormer is a Transformer-based sequence modeling framework: current robot state queries historical sensorimotor trajectories via cross-attention; a lightweight prediction head forecasts short-horizon future proprioceptive states; predicted future states (trained with supervised signals) are fused with temporal features to generate actions, enabling joint reasoning over motion history and anticipated future dynamics.

Result: Achieves 93.85% average traversal success rate on challenging terrains (stairs, gaps, slopes, rough terrain, obstacles); improvements of up to 42.73% over MLP, MoE-MLP, and vanilla Transformer baselines; maintains a single unified policy across all terrain types.

Conclusion: Explicit future-state modeling significantly improves robustness and generalization for agile whole-body locomotion in humanoid parkour.

Abstract: Humanoid parkour requires locomotion policies to coordinate whole-body dynamics across rapidly changing terrains such as stairs, gaps, slopes, and obstacles. Existing reinforcement learning policies are largely reactive, mapping observations directly to actions without explicitly modeling future body states. Such modeling becomes critical in agile locomotion tasks where successful motion execution depends strongly on anticipating upcoming contact transitions and body dynamics.We present ParkourFormer, a Transformer-based sequence modeling framework that reformulates humanoid locomotion as a future-conditioned decision-making problem. The current robot state queries historical sensorimotor trajectories through cross-attention, while a lightweight prediction head forecasts short-horizon future proprioceptive states. The predicted future states, trained with supervised signals, are fused with temporal features to generate actions, enabling the policy to jointly reason over motion history and anticipated future dynamics. We evaluate ParkourFormer on a diverse multi-terrain humanoid parkour benchmark including stairs, gaps, slopes, rough terrain, and obstacle traversal. Experiments in simulation and on a real humanoid robot show that ParkourFormer achieves a 93.85% average traversal success rate on highly challenging terrains, with improvements of up to 42.73% over strong MLP, MoE-based MLP, and vanilla Transformer baselines, while maintaining a single unified policy across all terrain types. These results demonstrate that explicit future-state modeling significantly improves robustness and generalization for agile whole-body locomotion.

</details>


### [60] [HoLoArm: Deformable Arms for Collision-Tolerant Quadrotor Flight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25790)
*Quang Ngoc Pham,Jonas Eschmann,Yang Zhou,Alejandro Ojeda Olarte,Giuseppe Loianno,Van Anh Ho*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HoLoArm is a quadrotor with dragonfly-inspired compliant arms and RL-based control that enables collision survival up to 7.6 m/s, rapid recovery (0.3-0.6 s), and stable flight with payloads up to 540 g.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Drones increasingly operate in human-centric environments, requiring designs that can survive collisions and recover quickly to minimize risks to humans and surroundings.

Method: Inspired by the nodus structure of dragonfly wings, compliant arms provide passive multi-directional deformation; a Reinforcement Learning (RL) control policy enhances recovery and hovering stability.

Result: HoLoArm passively deforms in any direction (including axial), recovers within 0.3-0.6 s, survives collisions at speeds up to 7.6 m/s, and carries a 540 g payload with stable flight.

Conclusion: This work advances soft aerial robot morphology with high agility and safety for cluttered/human-shared spaces, and establishes a foundation for future fully soft drones integrating compliant structures with intelligent control.

Abstract: The increasing use of drones in human-centric applications highlights the need for designs that can survive collisions and recover rapidly, minimizing risks to both humans and the environment. We present HoLoArm, a quadrotor with compliant arms inspired by the nodus structure of dragonfly wings. This design provides natural flexibility and resilience while preserving flight stability, which is further reinforced by the integration of a Reinforcement Learning (RL) control policy that enhances both recovery and hovering performance. Experimental results demonstrate that HoLoArm can passively deform in any direction, including axial one, and recover within 0.3-0.6 s depending on the direction and level of the impact. The drone can survive collisions at speeds up to 7.6 m/s and carry a 540 g payload while maintaining stable flight. This work contributes to the morphological design of soft aerial robots with high agility and reliable safety, enabling operation in cluttered and human shared environments, and lays the groundwork for future fully soft drones that integrate compliant structures with intelligent control.

</details>


### [61] [Extending Embodied Question Answering from Perception to Decision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25813)
*Xicheng Gong,Qiwei Li,Peiran Xu,Yadong Mu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EQA-Decision is a large-scale embodied QA dataset with 4M+ QA pairs covering four reasoning dimensions (static scene, spatial understanding, task dynamics, instant decision), paired with the RoboDecision baseline model for unified evaluation of perception, reasoning, and action-level decision-making in embodied environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EQA datasets are fragmented and each focuses on a limited subset of reasoning skills (e.g., spatial or procedural reasoning), lacking a unified large-scale framework for comprehensive evaluation of embodied reasoning.

Method: Constructed EQA-Decision, a large-scale dataset with 4M+ QA pairs covering four embodied reasoning dimensions: static scene construction, spatial understanding, task dynamics reasoning, and instant decision. Developed RoboDecision, a baseline model aligned with the benchmark for unified evaluation of perception, reasoning, and action-level decision-making.

Result: EQA-Decision effectively benchmarks and enhances VLM capabilities in spatial and interaction reasoning, providing a solid foundation for advancing embodied intelligence research.

Conclusion: The proposed dataset and baseline model address fragmentation in EQA benchmarks and provide a unified framework for evaluating embodied reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Embodied Question Answering (EQA) connects perception, reasoning, and interaction within embodied environments. However, existing datasets and benchmarks remain fragmented, each focusing on a limited subset of reasoning skills such as spatial understanding or procedural reasoning, without offering a unified large-scale framework for comprehensive evaluation. We present EQA-Decision, a large-scale embodied QA dataset that systematically covers four complementary dimensions of embodied reasoning: static scene construction, spatial understanding, task dynamics reasoning, and instant decision. The dataset contains over four million question-answer pairs with hierarchical annotations across diverse embodied scenarios. In addition, we develop RoboDecision, a strong baseline model aligned with the EQA-Decision Benchmark, providing a unified framework that jointly evaluates perception, reasoning, and action-level decision-making in embodied environments. Results demonstrate that EQA-Decision effectively benchmarks and enhances VLM capabilities in spatial and interaction reasoning, providing a solid foundation for advancing embodied intelligence research.

</details>


### [62] [OASIS: Observation-Action Space Alignment via SE(3) Trajectory Prediction for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25829)
*Xinzhe Chen,Sihua Ren,Liqi Huang,Haowen Sun,Mingyang Li,Xingyu Chen,Zeyang Liu,Xuguang Lan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: OASIS is a visuomotor policy that aligns intermediate representations with action space via SE(3) end-effector trajectory prediction, achieving superior robotic manipulation performance over VLA and WAM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLA models and WAMs use auxiliary spatial features or future visual-state prediction, but these representations remain in observation space and lack rigid-body geometry of the action space, forcing action decoders to implicitly recover this geometry.

Method: OASIS couples a 3D-aware feature encoder (fusing vision-language and metric-depth features) with an SE(3) trajectory predictor that produces camera-frame end-effector trajectories. The action decoder is conditioned on the predictor's pose-supervised hidden states to generate action chunks consistent with rigid-body motion.

Result: OASIS outperforms VLA and WAM baselines in both success rate and out-of-distribution generalization across simulation and real-world experiments.

Conclusion: Aligning intermediate representations with the action space via explicit SE(3) trajectory prediction improves robotic manipulation performance and generalization.

Abstract: Recent vision-language-action (VLA) models and world action models (WAMs) advance robotic manipulation by enriching intermediate representations with auxiliary spatial features or future visual-state prediction. However, these representations largely remain within the observation space and do not share the rigid-body geometry of the action space, forcing the action decoder to implicitly recover this geometry. We propose OASIS, a visuomotor policy that aligns the intermediate representation with the action space via $SE(3)$ end-effector trajectory prediction. OASIS couples a 3D-aware feature encoder that fuses vision-language and metric-depth features with an $SE(3)$ trajectory predictor that produces a camera-frame end-effector trajectory. Conditioned on the predictor's pose-supervised hidden states, the action decoder generates action chunks consistent with rigid-body motion. Across simulation and real-world experiments, OASIS outperforms VLA and WAM baselines in success rate and out-of-distribution generalization. Our project page is available at https://npuhandsome.github.io/OASIS_web.

</details>


### [63] [When Search Becomes Memory: Turning Robot Design Trials into Transferable Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25832)
*Yunfei Wang,Xiaohao Xu,Yang Li,Xiaonan Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Auto-Robotist is a self-evolving LLM agent that distills robot morphology search traces into an explicit, inspectable skill library, improving search efficiency and enabling transfer across design spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-driven evolutionary robot design loops are memoryless—simulator results are used only for the next population but not preserved as reusable design knowledge.

Method: Auto-Robotist uses an LLM agent that retrieves explicit skill entries (structural archetypes, positive/negative rules, supporting designs) to condition elite body edits, while retaining a GA mutation path. The skill library is updated via Add, Diagnose, and Merge operations after each evaluation.

Result: On seven EvoGym tasks, Auto-Robotist improves cold-start 5x5 search and transfers learned skills to 10x10 design spaces, where reference-conditioned transfer outperforms GA on every task.

Conclusion: LLM agents can convert expensive physical evaluations into reusable, auditable design principles for evolutionary robot design.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as proposal generators for evolutionary robot design, yet most loops remain memoryless: simulator results shape the next population but are not preserved as reusable design knowledge. We present Auto-Robotist, a self-evolving LLM agent that distills morphology-search traces into an explicit natural-language skill library. Each skill stores a structural archetype, evidence-grounded positive and negative rules, and the evaluated designs that support them, making design memory inspectable rather than implicit in a population. During search, the agent retrieves skills to condition LLM edits of elite bodies while retaining a Genetic Algorithm (GA) mutation path for exploration; after evaluation, it updates the library through Add, Diagnose, and Merge. Across seven EvoGym tasks spanning locomotion, traversal, and object interaction, Auto-Robotist improves cold-start 5x5 search and transfers learned skills to 10x10 design spaces, where reference-conditioned transfer outperforms GA on every task. These results suggest that LLM agents can convert expensive physical evaluations into reusable, auditable design principles. Our code will be released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [64] [RePlan-Bot: Multi-Level Replanning for Embodied Instruction Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25851)
*Xicheng Gong,Guozheng Sun,Peiran Xu,Yadong Mu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RePlan-Bot is a novel embodied instruction following agent that achieves state-of-the-art performance on ALFRED by integrating multi-level continuous replanning: an LLM-based auditor for dynamic sub-goal adjustment, a commonsense-guided search mechanism for object localization, and a ViT-based corrector for fixing risky low-level actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EIF methods struggle with long-horizon planning and handling irreversible state changes in interactive 3D environments, resulting in low task success rates.

Method: RePlan-Bot performs multi-level, continuous replanning via three components: (1) a high-level LLM-based auditor for dynamic sub-goal adjustment guided by environmental feedback, (2) a commonsense-guided search mechanism based on a multi-layered instance map for structured object localization, and (3) a lightweight ViT-based corrector to preemptively fix risky low-level actions.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on the ALFRED benchmark in both seen and unseen environments.

Conclusion: Demonstrates superior adaptability and reliability for embodied instruction following through continuous replanning at multiple levels.

Abstract: Embodied instruction following (EIF) requires agents to understand and execute complex natural language commands within interactive 3D environments. Despite recent advances, existing methods often fail in long-horizon planning and handling irreversible state changes, resulting in low task success rates. To address these challenges, we introduce RePlan-Bot, a novel EIF agent that performs multi-level, continuous replanning throughout task execution. RePlan-Bot integrates a high-level LLM-based auditor for dynamic sub-goal adjustments guided by environmental feedback, a commonsense-guided search mechanism based on a multi-layered instance map for precise and structured object localization, and a lightweight ViT-based corrector to preemptively fix risky low-level actions. Evaluated on the ALFRED benchmark, RePlan-Bot achieves state-of-the-art performance in both seen and unseen environments, demonstrating superior adaptability and reliability.

</details>


### [65] [AnyScene: Towards Highly Controllable Driving Scene Generation at Anywhere and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26113)
*Haiming Zhang,Junfei Zhou,Feng Jiang,Jingzhong Li,Zhenglong Guo,Penglin Dai,Jifeng Dai,Yan Xie,Benjin Zhu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AnyScene is a unified occupancy-centric framework for driving scene generation that uses a Spatial-Temporal Occupancy Diffusion Transformer to generate semantic occupancy sequences from BEV layouts, then synthesizes temporally consistent multi-view videos via a Geometry-Grounded View Expansion module, achieving state-of-the-art performance in occupancy and video generation with strong generalization to unseen layouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing occupancy-guided methods rely on shallow conditioning mechanisms and reference-frame-dependent video synthesis, limiting fine-grained controllability from arbitrary BEV layouts and restricting scalable simulation for addressing rare safety-critical scenarios in autonomous driving.

Method: Proposes (1) a Spatial-Temporal Occupancy Diffusion Transformer that jointly tokenizes BEV and occupancy features in an autoregressive manner for controllable occupancy sequence generation, and (2) a Geometry-Grounded View Expansion module that treats occupancy as canonical spatial representation for reference-free, autoregressive, temporally consistent multi-view video synthesis with flexible camera configurations.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance in both occupancy and video generation tasks; demonstrates strong generalization to unseen and customized BEV layouts; provides measurable benefits for downstream tasks like sparse-view 3D reconstruction.

Conclusion: The unified occupancy-centric framework enables precise controllability from cross-dataset and user-defined BEV inputs, supports long-horizon generation, and offers flexible camera configurations at inference time, advancing scalable simulation for autonomous driving.

Abstract: Generating high-fidelity and controllable synthetic data is critical for advancing end-to-end autonomous driving, particularly for addressing the long tail of rare safety-critical scenarios. Existing occupancy-guided methods typically rely on shallow conditioning mechanisms and reference-frame-dependent video synthesis, which limits fine-grained controllability from arbitrary BEV layouts and restricts their applicability for scalable simulation. In this paper, we propose AnyScene, a unified occupancy-centric framework for driving scene generation. AnyScene generates semantic occupancy sequences from BEV layouts through a Spatial-Temporal Occupancy Diffusion Transformer that jointly tokenizes BEV and occupancy features in an autoregressive manner. This design enables precise controllability from cross-dataset and user-defined BEV inputs while naturally supporting long-horizon generation. Building upon the generated occupancy, a Geometry-Grounded View Expansion module treats occupancy as the canonical spatial representation and synthesizes temporally consistent multi-view driving videos in a reference-free and autoregressive fashion, supporting flexible camera configurations at inference time. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AnyScene achieves state-of-the-art performance in both occupancy and video generation. It exhibits strong generalization to unseen and customized layouts, and provides measurable benefits for downstream tasks such as sparse-view 3D reconstruction.

</details>

### [66] [In Search of the Ingredients of Open-Endedness: Replicating Picbreeder with Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23908)
*Sam Earle,Kay Arulkumaran,Andrew Dai,Akarsh Kumar,Julian Togelius,Sebastian Risi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether AI agents (specifically Vision Language Models) can replicate the open-ended creative discovery found in human-driven systems like Picbreeder, finding qualitative differences and testing factors like exploratory noise, behavioral diversity, and narrative memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine if artificial agents have the capacity for fruitful unguided discovery—the open-ended generation of novel and meaningful forms—which is a fundamental property of human creative and scientific processes.

Method: The authors replicate Picbreeder (a human-driven interactive evolutionary system for generating images) by replacing human users with frontier Vision Language Models (VLMs). They compare outputs using metrics of phylogenetic complexity, visual and semantic salience, and novelty. They also study the effects of adding exploratory noise, behavioral diversity between agents, and narrative momentum through memory of past actions.

Result: Clear qualitative differences were observed between the AI-generated output and the historical human baseline. The paper characterizes these differences using the proposed metrics.

Conclusion: Adding exploratory noise, behavioral diversity, and narrative memory may help address some of the observed differences, but the study highlights fundamental gaps between human-driven and AI-driven open-ended discovery.

Abstract: We are in the midst of large-scale industrial and academic efforts to automate the processes of scientific, technological and creative production through AI-driven assistants. Historically, a fundamental property of these processes in their human form has been their open-endedness: their capacity for generating a seemingly endless supply of novel and meaningful new forms. Do artificial agents have any capacity for such fruitful unguided discovery? To answer this question, we turn to Picbreeder, the canonical exemplar of human-driven open-ended search, in which users collaboratively generated a diverse library of images through interactive evolution of small neural networks. We replicate Picbreeder, replacing human users with frontier Vision Language Models (VLMs). We observe clear qualitative differences between the output of our system and the historical human baseline, and attempt to characterize them using metrics of phylogenetic complexity and visual and semantic salience and novelty. In an effort to identify some of the causal factors contributing these differences, we study the addition of exploratory noise to the agents' selection process, of behavioral diversity between agents, and of narrative momentum in the form of memory of past actions. We make our code available at https://github.com/smearle/picbreeder-vlm.

</details>


### [67] [Confidence Calibration in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23909)
*Noam Michael,Daniel BenShushan,Jacob Bien,Don A. Moore*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs exhibit overconfidence, especially on hard tasks, and underconfidence on easy tasks; the authors propose LifeEval to evaluate calibration across difficulty levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether LLMs' confidence calibration mirrors human biases, specifically examining the hard-easy effect where overconfidence varies with task difficulty.

Method: Preregistered study analyzing LLM confidence vs. accuracy across diverse tasks; development of LifeEval benchmark for calibration evaluation across difficulty levels.

Result: LLMs are, on average, overconfident (confidence exceeds accuracy), but this is moderated by a hard-easy effect: strong overconfidence on hard tests and substantial underconfidence on easy tests.

Conclusion: Current LLMs exhibit human-like miscalibration patterns; LifeEval provides a standardized test for assessing calibration across difficulty levels.

Abstract: We investigate the calibration of large language models' (LLMs') confidence across diverse tasks. The results of our preregistered study show that the current crop of LLMs are, like people, too sure they are right: confidence exceeds accuracy, on average. Importantly, however, this tendency is moderated by a powerful hard-easy effect, wherein overconfidence is greatest on difficult tests; by contrast, easy tests actually show substantial underconfidence. We develop LifeEval, a test for evaluating model calibration across levels of difficulty.

</details>


### [68] [How Much Thinking is Enough? Quantifying and Understanding Redundancy in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23926)
*Zhiyuan Zhai,Xinkai You,Wenjing Yan,Xin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes and quantifies reasoning redundancy in large language models, finding 61-93% step-level redundancy across frontier models, and proves this redundancy is a structural consequence of length-agnostic outcome rewards rather than model-specific artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite widespread observation that reasoning models produce long, self-reflective chains of thought with extensive reformulation and verification, the necessity of such deliberation has never been measured at scale or explained from first principles. This paper closes both gaps.

Method: The authors formalize redundancy as the largest fraction of trailing segmented steps that can be truncated while the model still produces the correct answer. They quantify this across four frontier reasoning models on two mathematical benchmarks (MATH-500), and prove theoretically that redundancy is a structural consequence of length-agnostic outcome rewards.

Result: Step-level redundancy is consistently 61-93% across 8 (model, benchmark) conditions, with median critical prefix being a single step in 6/8 conditions. Even on hardest Level-5 problems, redundancy remains high (46-85%). The formal proof shows no finite expected stopping time is optimal under length-agnostic outcome rewards.

Conclusion: Over-thinking in reasoning models is not a bug to be patched but a structural property of how current reasoning models are trained under length-agnostic outcome rewards, applying regardless of RL algorithm, base model, data distribution, or training method.

Abstract: Reasoning-capable large language models solve hard problems by emitting long chains of thought, paying heavily in latency, GPU time, and energy. Casual inspection of their traces reveals extensive reformulation, verification, and circular self-reflection, yet how much of this deliberation is actually necessary has never been measured at scale or explained from first principles. This paper closes both gaps.
  We formalise reasoning redundancy directly in terms of the reasoning model itself: the redundancy of a correct trace is the largest fraction of its trailing segmented steps that can be truncated while $π$, forced to terminate thinking and emit a final answer, still produces the correct answer. A large-scale quantification across four frontier reasoning models and two mathematical benchmarks shows that step-level redundancy is consistently high -- between 61% and 93% across the 8 (model, benchmark) conditions we study, with the median critical prefix equal to a single segmented step in six of the eight conditions -- that the finding is robust to the choice of judge family, and that although $ρ$ decreases with problem difficulty on MATH-500, all four models remain substantially redundant ($ρ\in [46\%, 85\%]$) even on the hardest Level-5 problems.
  We then prove that this redundancy is a structural consequence of length-agnostic outcome rewards, not a model-specific artefact: under any such reward, no finite expected stopping time is optimal. The result holds regardless of RL algorithm, base model, data distribution, or whether the policy is obtained via RL or distillation; over-thinking is therefore not a bug to be patched in individual models but a structural property of how current reasoning models are trained. Code: https://github.com/zhiyuanZhai20/how-much-thinking-is-enough

</details>


### [69] [Context: Proactive Goal-Directed Intelligence via Composable Sandboxed Programs, Declarative Wiring, and Structured Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23928)
*Gregory Magarshak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Context is the intelligence layer of the Magarshak Architecture, replacing reactive chatbots with proactive goal-directed agents. It uses write-time context assembly, composable sandboxed wisdom programs, and proactive goal stream state machines to advance shared tasks without user prompts. Six formal theorems prove cost bounds, correctness, and dominance over reactive agents. Implemented in the open-source Qbix/Safebox/Safebots stack.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitations of reactive query-response chatbots that wait for user prompts, by enabling proactive, goal-directed agents that autonomously advance shared tasks in multi-participant environments.

Method: Three mechanisms: (1) Write-time context assembly using Groker agents to precompute typed attributes with deterministic caching; (2) Composable sandboxed wisdom programs—LM-generated imperative programs wired to goals via typed stream relations and phase ordering, executable without LM calls; (3) Proactive goal stream state machines that inspect graph state and emit structured interaction content without awaiting input.

Result: Six formal theorems: Context Stability Theorem (cost bounded by semantic change rate), Program Composition Correctness Theorem, Declarative Wiring Soundness Theorem, Proactive Dominance Theorem (proactive agents weakly dominate reactive on expected turns-to-terminal-state), Coordination Overhead Elimination & Quality Preservation (Pareto improvements in multi-participant chats), and Cross-Platform Vote Consistency Theorem.

Conclusion: The Magarshak Architecture with Context layer enables proactive, cost-efficient, and formally verified goal-directed agents, implemented in an open-source stack, with guaranteed improvements over reactive chatbot paradigms.

Abstract: We present Context, the intelligence layer of the Magarshak Architecture, which replaces reactive query-response chatbots with proactive goal-directed agents that advance shared tasks without waiting for user prompts. The architecture rests on three mutually reinforcing mechanisms. Write-time context assembly precomputes enriched typed attributes via Groker agents, assembling interaction context as a deterministic pure function of graph state; context blocks are byte-identical across turns between semantic changes, enabling near-100% KV-cache reuse. Composable sandboxed wisdom programs form a governed library of LM-generated imperative programs declaratively wired to goal types via typed stream relations, composed via phase ordering, and executed at interaction time without further LM calls. Proactive goal stream state machines drive conversations toward terminal states by inspecting graph state and emitting structured interaction content (option arrays, governance affordances, clarification prompts) without awaiting user input. We prove six formal results: the Context Stability Theorem, bounding per-turn LM cost as a function of semantic change rate; a Program Composition Correctness Theorem; a Declarative Wiring Soundness Theorem; the Proactive Dominance Theorem, proving proactive agents weakly dominate reactive agents on expected turns-to-terminal-state; Coordination Overhead Elimination and Quality Preservation, establishing Pareto improvements in multi-participant goal chats; and a Cross-Platform Vote Consistency Theorem. Implemented in the open-source Qbix / Safebox / Safebots stack.

</details>


### [70] [Toward Reliable Design of LLM-Enabled Agentic Workflows: Optimizing Latency-Reliability-Cost Tradeoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23929)
*Ya-Ting Yang,Quanyan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes latency, reliability, and cost tradeoffs in LLM-enabled agentic workflows, proposing performance models and optimal token allocation policies for sequential workflows under constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern AI systems increasingly use multi-agent workflows combining LLM-powered and conventional agents, creating a need to understand fundamental tradeoffs between latency, reliability, and cost in such systems.

Method: The authors introduce parametric performance models for both LLM and non-LLM agents, using an exponential reliability function to capture the relationship between computational effort (reasoning/output tokens) and output quality. They study sequential workflow design under latency and cost constraints.

Result: Key results include a water-filling token allocation policy and characterizations of optimal workflow reliability expressed in terms of shadow prices.

Conclusion: The paper provides a theoretical framework for optimizing multi-agent workflows by balancing latency, reliability, and cost through principled token allocation strategies.

Abstract: Modern AI systems increasingly rely on workflows composed of multiple interacting agents, some powered by large language models (LLMs) and others by conventional computational modules. This paper analyzes the fundamental tradeoffs between latency, reliability, and cost in LLM-enabled agentic workflows. We introduce performance models for both LLM and non-LLM agents that capture the relationship between computational effort and output quality, incorporating the impact of reasoning and output tokens for LLM agents using a parametric exponential reliability function. Then, we study the design of sequential workflows under latency and cost constraints. Main results include a water-filling token allocation policy and characterizations of optimal workflow reliability in terms of shadow prices.

</details>


### [71] [Quantum Frog: Emergent Cooperation and Difficulty Scaling in a Quantized-Time Cooperative Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23930)
*Saad Mankarious*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Quantum Frog is a two-player cooperative game with a quantized-time mechanic where environment advances only when a player acts. Using RL (Tabular Q-Learning, DQN, IDQN, MAPPO), they find: rush strategy is optimal; adding an uncooperative second player is harder than sextupling traffic; cooperative training boosts joint success by 32-34%; emergent strategy is synchronized rushing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To analyze how a novel quantized-time mechanic affects game difficulty, optimal policies, cooperation gaps, and emergent strategies in a cooperative multi-agent setting, using RL as an analytical lens.

Method: Trained agents through five escalating stages (Tabular Q-Learning, DQN, IDQN, MAPPO with centralized critic) across traffic densities of 1-6 cars on an 8×8 grid, evaluating joint success rate and episode length.

Result: (i) Rush strategy (moving upward at every step) is universally optimal due to minimized time exposure. (ii) Adding an uncoordinated second player is harder than sextupling traffic for a single expert. (iii) Cooperative training improves joint success by 32-34 percentage points and reduces episode length from ~90 to ~6 steps. (iv) Emergent cooperative strategy is synchronized rushing, not complex positional coordination.

Conclusion: Shared incentives alone suffice to align agents in time-critical cooperative tasks; quantized-time mechanics fundamentally shape optimal strategies toward minimizing time exposure, with implications for commercial game design and multi-agent learning dynamics.

Abstract: We introduce \emph{Quantum Frog}, a two-player cooperative game built on a novel \emph{quantized-time} mechanic in which the environment advances only when a player acts. Inspired by the classic arcade game Frogger, Quantum Frog requires two frogs to cross an 8$\times$8 grid of traffic and reach the far side together. We use reinforcement learning (RL) as an analytical lens to answer four design questions: (1) how does game difficulty scale with traffic density, (2) what is the optimal single-agent policy and why, (3) how large is the cooperation gap between independent and cooperative two-agent play, and (4) what joint strategy emerges when agents are incentivised to cooperate? We train agents through five escalating stages, Tabular Q-Learning, Deep Q-Network (\DQN), Independent \DQN~(\IDQN), and Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimisation (\MAPPO\ with a centralised critic), evaluating each against traffic densities of one to six cars. Our key findings are: (i) the quantized-time mechanic makes a \emph{rush strategy} (moving directly upward at every step) universally optimal, as time exposure to traffic is minimised; (ii) adding an uncoordinated second player is harder than sextupling the traffic for a single expert player; (iii) cooperative training recovers +32--34 percentage points of joint success rate relative to independent agents and reduces episode length from $\sim$90 to $\sim$6 steps; and (iv) the emergent cooperative strategy is synchronised rushing, not complex positional coordination, illustrating that shared incentives alone suffice to align agents in time-critical cooperative tasks. These findings provide concrete, empirically grounded guidance for the commercial design of Quantum Frog and offer broader insights into the role of environment mechanics in shaping multi-agent learning dynamics.

</details>


### [72] [BODHI: Precise OS Kernel Specification Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23931)
*Zhiming Chang,Ziyang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BODHI is a domain knowledge prompting method that improves LLM-based formal verification specification generation for OS kernels, boosting Pass@1 from 55.10% to 96.73% (Claude Opus 4.6) by adding a structured C-to-Python translation guide with 15 category patterns, achieving model-agnostic gains of +11% to +32% across nine models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual specification writing for OS kernel verification requires deep domain expertise, motivating the use of LLMs for automation. However, existing LLM performance on the OSV-Bench benchmark is limited (best Pass@1 of 55.10%), necessitating a method to improve formal specification generation.

Method: BODHI augments standard few-shot prompts with a structured C-to-Python translation guide covering 15 categories of domain-specific translation patterns, inspired by Structured Chain-of-Thought (SCoT) prompting. The guide separates concerns by addressing pre-condition extraction and post-condition generation as distinct categories.

Result: Evaluated on nine models from six providers (Anthropic, Mistral, Amazon, DeepSeek, Meta, Alibaba) covering dense, MoE, and reasoning architectures, BODHI improves every model tested by +11% to +32%. The best configuration (Claude Opus 4.6 + BODHI) achieves 96.73% Pass@1, reducing both syntax and semantic errors.

Conclusion: Domain knowledge injection is a model-agnostic technique that substantially bridges the gap between general-purpose code generation and formal specification synthesis, with strongest effect on models possessing sufficient instruction-following capability.

Abstract: The formal verification of operating system kernels requires precise specifications that capture the intended behavior of system calls. Writing these specifications manually demands deep domain expertise, motivating the use of large language models (LLMs) to automate the process. However, in OSV-Bench, a benchmark of 245 specification generation tasks derived from the Hyperkernel OS kernel, the best reported Pass@1 is 55.10%. We propose a domain knowledge prompting method (BODHI), which augments the standard few-shot prompt with a structured C-to-Python translation guide covering 15 categories of domain-specific translation patterns. Inspired by Structured Chain-of-Thought (SCoT) prompting, the guide organizes translation by separation of concerns, addressing pre-condition extraction and post-condition generation as distinct categories. Evaluated on nine models from six providers (Anthropic, Mistral, Amazon, DeepSeek, Meta, Alibaba), covering dense, mixture-of-experts and reasoning architectures, BODHI improves every model tested, with gains ranging from +11% to +32%. The best configuration (Claude Opus 4.6 + BODHI) reaches 96.73% Pass@1. BODHI reduces both syntax and semantic errors, with the strongest effect on models that have sufficient instruction-following capability to utilize structured reference material. These results demonstrate that domain knowledge injection is a model-agnostic technique that substantially bridges the gap between general-purpose code generation and formal specification synthesis.

</details>


### [73] [When Correct Beliefs Collapse: Epistemic Resilience of LLMs under Clinical Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23932)
*Boyu Xiao,Xiuqi Tian,Xuwen Song,Haochun Wang,Guanchun Song,Sendong Zhao,Bing Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs exhibit multi-turn sycophancy in clinical dialogues, abandoning correct diagnoses under pressure. Med-Stress tests belief stability, revealing a dissociation between medical knowledge and robustness. RBED (inference-time defense) and R-FT (training-time fine-tuning) mitigate this, with R-FT nearly eliminating belief change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs show high accuracy on medical benchmarks but can exhibit multi-turn sycophancy, abandoning correct diagnoses under escalating pressure in clinical dialogues, posing risks for real-world deployment.

Method: Proposes Med-Stress, a stress test framework evaluating belief stability under escalating pressure. Introduces RBED (Role-Based Epistemic Defense) for inference-time mitigation and R-FT (Resilience-oriented Fine-Tuning) for training-time internalization of evidence-based resistance.

Result: Nine frontier LLMs show a dissociation between initial diagnostic capability and belief stability, with large knowledge-robustness gaps. R-FT nearly eliminates belief change and substantially improves robustness.

Conclusion: Medical knowledge and robustness are dissociable in LLMs; targeted training (R-FT) can effectively close this gap by internalizing resistance to pressure.

Abstract: Despite strong medical benchmark accuracy, LLMs can exhibit severe multi-turn sycophancy in clinical dialogue, abandoning initial correct diagnosis under escalating pressure. We propose \textbf{\textsc{Med-Stress}}, a targeted stress test framework that evaluates belief stability under escalating pressure. Across nine frontier large language models (LLMs), we find a clear dissociation between medical knowledge and robustness: high initial diagnostic capability does not imply high belief stability, yielding large knowledge-robustness gaps for several LLMs. To mitigate this failure mode, we propose a lightweight inference-time defense, \textbf{\texttt{RBED}} (\textbf{R}ole-\textbf{B}ased \textbf{E}pistemic \textbf{D}efense), and \textbf{\texttt{R-FT}} (\textbf{R}esilience-oriented \textbf{F}ine-\textbf{T}uning), a training-time approach that internalizes evidence-based resistance to pressure. Experiments show that \textbf{\texttt{R-FT}} nearly eliminates belief change and substantially improves robustness.

</details>


### [74] [Practical Quantum CIM Empowerment via All-Domestic-Core Agentic Large Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23934)
*Wang Rui,Lu Diannan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper integrates a femtosecond laser-pumped Coherent Ising Machine (CIM) with an LLM-driven agentic system using LangGraph and LangChain to automate QUBO/Ising model calibration, constraint weight iteration, and validation of literature-reported schemes. The system is fully implemented with domestic large models and domestically developed CIM hardware, achieving all-domestic empowerment of quantum CIM. The work also identifies challenges at the intersection of large models and quantum computing, and discovers a reciprocal enhancement paradigm where agent-assisted quantum computing iterations improve the agent's own problem-solving capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the barriers non-specialists face in modeling quantum computing devices for NP-complete problems and reduce the tedious manual iteration of constraint weights and modeling methodologies even for experts, by integrating LLM-driven agents with Coherent Ising Machines.

Method: Integration of a femtosecond laser-pumped Coherent Ising Machine (CIM) with an LLM-driven agentic system built on LangGraph and LangChain frameworks, using domestic large models and domestically developed CIM hardware.

Result: LLMs effectively perform QUBO/Ising model calibration, constraint weight decision iteration, and rapid validation of literature-reported schemes, all implemented with domestic models and hardware. A reciprocal enhancement paradigm was discovered where agent-assisted quantum computing iterations improve the agent's problem-solving capability.

Conclusion: The work successfully realizes robust technological integration of LLM agents with quantum CIM using all-domestic components, laying a foundation for future research while identifying persisting challenges. The discovered reciprocal enhancement paradigm offers a promising direction for addressing these challenges.

Abstract: Quantum computing devices are recognized as powerful tools for solving NP-complete problems. However, the intricacy of their modeling presents notable barriers for non-specialists, while the tedious iteration of constraint weights and modeling methodologies also consumes substantial effort on the part of experts. To address these challenges, this study integrates a femtosecond laser-pumped Coherent Ising Machine (CIM) with an LLM-driven agentic system by leveraging the LangGraph and LangChain frameworks. Comprehensive investigations demonstrate that large language models (LLMs) can effectively perform such tasks in modeling as QUBO/Ising model calibration, constraint weight decision iteration and rapid validation of literature-reported schemes. Notably, all these tasks can be fully implemented based on domestic large models, combined with domestically developed CIM hardware, we truly achieve the practical empowerment of quantum CIM that fully relies on all-domestic agentic large models and hardware. This work successfully realizes robust technological integration, laying a solid foundation for subsequent research. Nevertheless, it also identifies the persisting challenges in the two cutting-edge fields of large models and quantum computing at the current stage. Encouragingly, we unexpectedly discover a promising new paradigm where accumulated knowledge from agent-assisted quantum computing iterations reciprocally enhances the agent's own problem-solving capability, thereby addressing these challenges.

</details>


### [75] [Operationalizing Reconstructive Authority: Runtime Construction, Dependency Resolution, and Execution Gating in Autonomous Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23935)
*Marcelo Fernandez - TraslaIA*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a runtime enforcement model for Reconstructive Authority (RAM) that adds a third execution state (halt) alongside admit/deny, and uses a Recovery Loop with drift detection and execution control to ensure no action executes without constructible authority while enabling conditional liveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agents fail not only from incorrect decisions but from executing decisions whose authority no longer holds at runtime; prior work defined RAM as a validity condition but lacked runtime enforcement mechanisms.

Method: The authors define a runtime execution model with three states (admit/deny/halt), a concrete execution protocol with dynamic dependency resolution and authority reconstruction, and a Recovery Loop that integrates drift detection (IML) with execution control (ACP) to suspend, acquire missing information, and re-attempt reconstruction.

Result: The model guarantees safety (no action without constructible authority) and conditional liveness (execution resumes when authority-defining variables become observable).

Conclusion: This work operationalizes reconstructive authority as a runtime enforcement mechanism, providing the execution semantics to apply RAM in real systems.

Abstract: Autonomous agent systems fail not only due to incorrect decisions, but due to executing decisions whose authority no longer holds at runtime. Prior work defined Reconstructive Authority (RAM) as a condition for valid execution: actions are permitted only if authority can be constructed from current state.
  This paper addresses enforcement at runtime: how to enforce this condition in a running system.
  We introduce a runtime execution model in which authority is evaluated at action time and execution is conditioned on its constructibility. This extends the execution state space beyond admit/deny with a third state, halt, representing cases where authority is undefined due to incomplete or uncertain observability.
  We define a concrete execution protocol including dynamic dependency resolution, authority reconstruction, and explicit decision semantics. We further introduce a Recovery Loop that integrates drift detection (IML) with execution control (ACP), allowing the system to suspend execution, acquire missing information, and re-attempt authority reconstruction.
  We show that this model guarantees safety -- no action is executed without constructible authority -- and conditional liveness: execution resumes when authority-defining variables become observable.
  This work operationalizes reconstructive authority as a runtime enforcement mechanism, providing the execution semantics required to apply RAM in real systems.

</details>


### [76] [Fuzzy, Neutrosophic, and Uncertain Graph Theory: Properties and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23936)
*Takaaki Fujita,Florentin Smarandache*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A comprehensive survey of graph theory under uncertainty, introducing the uncertain graph framework as a unifying paradigm for fuzzy, neutrosophic, and related models, including extensions like hypergraphs, dynamic graphs, and applications in molecular graphs, decision-making, GNNs, knowledge graphs, and cognitive maps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a coherent, unified framework that organizes diverse uncertainty-aware graph models (fuzzy, neutrosophic, etc.) and clarifies their relationships, capabilities, and applications in complex systems.

Method: Systematic literature survey and conceptual synthesis, organizing fundamental concepts, structural properties, graph classes, and parameters under the uncertain graph paradigm, while extending to digraphs, hypergraphs, superhypergraphs, and dynamic graphs.

Result: A unified perspective bridging multiple uncertainty-aware graph models, with practical demonstrations across molecular graphs, decision-making, graph neural networks, knowledge graphs, and cognitive maps.

Conclusion: The uncertain graph framework effectively unifies various graph models under uncertainty, offering a coherent lens for understanding their theoretical foundations and practical utility in complex systems.

Abstract: This book presents a comprehensive and systematic survey of graph theory under uncertainty, with particular emphasis on the unifying role of the uncertain graph framework. It reviews fundamental concepts, structural properties, graph classes, and graph parameters within fuzzy, neutrosophic, and related models, while also introducing a wide range of extensions such as uncertain digraphs, hypergraphs, superhypergraphs, and dynamic graphs. In addition to theoretical developments, the book explores practical applications, including uncertain molecular graphs, decision-making systems, graph neural networks, knowledge graphs, and cognitive maps. By organizing diverse uncertainty-aware graph models within a common perspective, this work provides a coherent framework for understanding their relationships, capabilities, and applications in complex systems.

</details>


### [77] [BoxLitE: A Faithful Knowledge Base Embedding Based on Convex Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23937)
*Bruno F. Lourenço,Hesham Morgan,Ana Ozaki,Aleksandar Pavlović,Emanuel Sallinger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BoxLitE is a KB embedding model for DL-Lite$^{\mathcal{H}}$ that maps concepts to convex regions, enabling convex optimization and weakly faithful embeddings for satisfiable knowledge bases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KB embeddings rarely leverage the power of convexity during learning tasks, despite convex regions being useful for representing concept hierarchies in TBoxes.

Method: BoxLitE maps concepts to convex (box-shaped) regions in vector space, formulating the KB embedding task as a convex optimization problem, with theoretical guarantees for weak faithfulness in DL-Lite$^{\mathcal{H}}$.

Result: For any satisfiable DL-Lite$^{\mathcal{H}}$ KB, there exists a BoxLitE embedding that is a weakly faithful model, demonstrated through a proof-of-concept convex optimization formulation.

Conclusion: BoxLitE provides a principled approach to KB embeddings that combines ABox and TBox reasoning via convex representations, with formal faithfulness guarantees.

Abstract: Knowledge base (KB) embeddings aim at combining the capability of classical knowledge graph embeddings to generalize the information present in facts, the ABox, with conceptual knowledge represented in an ontology language, the TBox. Several authors have recently explored the idea of mapping concepts to convex regions in a vector space. This is useful to represent hierarchies, typically present in TBoxes, since more general concepts can be mapped to larger regions, containing those regions associated with more specific concepts. However, the power of convexity is rarely leveraged during the actual learning tasks. Here, we introduce BoxLitE, a KB embedding model for DL-Lite$^{\mathcal{H}}$ that allows for convex optimization. We show that for any satisfiable DL-Lite$^{\mathcal{H}}$ KB, there is a BoxLitE embedding that is a weakly faithful model. As a proof of concept, we show how to formulate the KB embedding task as a convex optimization problem and how to obtain embeddings with such desirable faithfulness properties.

</details>


### [78] [Authority Inversion in LLM-Mediated Ubiquitous Systems: When Models Trust Users Over Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23938)
*Long Zhang,Zi-bo Qin,Wei-neng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs exhibit Authority Inversion where numerical sensor data is ignored in favor of natural-language user claims, causing near-zero sensor trust (AAI = -0.805). The authors propose Geometric Authority Calibration (GAC), an inference-time intervention that flips 80.2% of incorrect decisions and improves HAR accuracy from 0-1.6% to 21.9-27.5%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs increasingly fuse heterogeneous inputs (sensor measurements + user claims) in ubiquitous systems, how they implicitly allocate authority between conflicting physical sensor data and user claims remains unexamined, raising critical reliability concerns for deployments where sensing must retain priority.

Method: The authors develop a geometric framework of context integration, introduce two audit metrics (Context Integration Ratio CIR and Authority Alignment Index AAI), and propose Geometric Authority Calibration (GAC)—an inference-time layer-level intervention that suppresses misplaced user authority. They evaluate across 4 models (4B-35B parameters, 3 architectures) and 4 datasets with 576 conflict instances.

Result: Extreme authority inversion observed: near-zero sensor trust (AAI = -0.805, Cohen's d = -2.14), unaffected by model capacity. Theory-guided causal injection flips 80.2% of incorrect decisions (vs. <0.4% for random controls). GAC improves HAR accuracy from 0-1.6% to 21.9-27.5%, outperforming prompting baselines.

Conclusion: Authority allocation in LLM-mediated systems must be explicitly audited and application-specifically configured rather than left implicit, as format-dependent authority inversion severely compromises reliability when physical sensing must take priority.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly fuse heterogeneous inputs in ubiquitous systems. Yet, how LLMs implicitly allocate authority when sensor measurements and user claims conflict remains unexamined, raising critical reliability concerns for deployments where physical sensing must retain priority. Unlike explicit traditional fusion, LLMs bury authority allocation within learned representations. We discover this allocation is severely format-dependent: numerical sensor data fails to integrate into answer-relevant model directions, allowing natural-language claims to dominate the final decision, a phenomenon we term \textbf{Authority Inversion}.To diagnose and mitigate this, we develop a geometric framework of context integration, introduce two computable audit metrics, specifically the Context Integration Ratio (CIR) and Authority Alignment Index (AAI), and propose Geometric Authority Calibration (GAC), an inference-time layer-level intervention to suppress misplaced user authority. Evaluating four models (4B to 35B parameters, three architectures) across four datasets totaling 576 conflict instances reveals extreme inversion: on numerical tasks, models exhibit near-zero sensor trust (AAI = -0.805, Cohen's d = -2.14), unaffected by model capacity. Validating our geometric framework, theory-guided causal injection flips 80.2\% of incorrect decisions (vs. <0.4\% for random controls). Practically, GAC improves HAR accuracy from 0 -- 1.6\% to 21.9 -- 27.5\%, outperforming prompting baselines. Ultimately, authority allocation in LLM-mediated systems must be explicitly audited and application-specifically configured rather than left implicit.

</details>


### [79] [DRIVE: Modeling Skills at the Reasoning and Interaction Levels for Web Agents under Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23939)
*Xirui Liu,Sihang Zhou,Yanning Hou,Rong Zhou,Haoyuan Chen,Maolin He,Siwei Wang,Hao Chen,Jian Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DRIVE is a dual-level skill modeling framework that separates web agent experiences into transferable natural language reasoning skills (task logic) and executable programmatic interaction skills (page operations), achieving 52.8% average success rate across WebArena (+7.3% over baseline).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing web agents store reasoning (abstract task logic) and interaction (page-specific operations) knowledge uniformly, causing a dilemma where abstract representations lose executability and concrete representations fail to generalize, limiting capability accumulation on new websites.

Method: DRIVE uses dual-level skill modeling: (1) natural language reasoning skills for transferable task logic, (2) programmatic interaction skills for executable page operations. A scene-aware coordination mechanism retrieves and invokes appropriate skills, and skill-level reflection identifies hierarchy-specific failures for targeted library refinement.

Result: 52.8% average task success rate across five WebArena domains, outperforming skill-free baseline by 7.3 percentage points. Ablations confirm reasoning and interaction skills provide distinct, complementary benefits.

Conclusion: Separating transferable task logic from executable page-level operations enables better generalization and capability accumulation for web agents, with both skill types providing complementary benefits.

Abstract: Web agents require both high-level reasoning (for task decomposition) and low-level interactions (for page elements manipulation) to conduct different tasks. However, these knowledge types differ fundamentally: reasoning knowledge (e.g., booking a flight requires first searching for routes) is abstract and transferable across websites, while interaction knowledge (e.g., clicking the Search button at a specific coordinate on Site A) depends heavily on page-specific contexts. Existing methods store experiences uniformly. This creates a dilemma: abstract representations lose executability on concrete pages, while concrete representations fail to generalize across domains. This entanglement limits capability accumulation: on new websites, agents either fail to recognize reusable task logic due to surface-level differences or attempt infeasible actions from outdated page structures. To disentangle them, we propose DRIVE, a dual-level skill modeling framework separating historical experience into natural language reasoning skills, which capture transferable task logic, and programmatic interaction skills, grounding abstract actions to executable operations. A scene-aware coordination mechanism adaptively retrieves and invokes these dual-level skills based on task semantics. DRIVE also uses skill-level reflection to identify hierarchy-specific failure modes, enabling targeted skill library expansion and refinement. Experiments across five WebArena domains show DRIVE attains an average task success rate of 52.8%, exceeding the skill-free baseline by 7.3 percentage points. Further ablations show reasoning and interaction skills provide distinct, complementary benefits, supporting separation of transferable task logic from executable page-level operations.

</details>


### [80] [Residual Drift Dominates Contradiction in Multi-Turn Constraint Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23940)
*Sebastien Kawada*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multi-turn reasoning systems primarily fail via satisfiable drift (silent violation of prior commitments) rather than logical contradiction. DRIFT-Bench evaluates this across 816 problems, finding MUS-Repair strongest but residual errors remain 98-100% satisfiable drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify and characterize the dominant failure mode in multi-turn reasoning systems, moving beyond the common assumption that contradiction is the primary issue.

Method: Built DRIFT-Bench (816 problems across three constraint domains), a solver-instrumented benchmark. Evaluated four methods across four open-weight models (8B-120B parameters). MUS-Repair uses minimal unsatisfiable subsets for feedback.

Result: MUS-Repair outperforms all baselines (+1.8 to +15.0 pp). Residual errors after structured feedback are 98-100% satisfiable drift, with contradiction dropping to near zero.

Conclusion: Multi-turn systems must separately validate that returned answers respect the maintained state, as forgetting (satisfiable drift) persists even when contradiction is eliminated through repair methods.

Abstract: How do multi-turn reasoning systems fail? The expected answer is logical contradiction, in which the system's maintained state becomes unsatisfiable. We show that the dominant mode is instead satisfiable drift, where the internal state stays consistent while the returned answer silently violates prior commitments. We build DRIFT-Bench (Decomposing Reasoning Into Failure Types), a solver-instrumented benchmark of 816 test problems across three constraint domains, and evaluate four methods on it across four open-weight models (8B-120B parameters). MUS-Repair, which feeds minimal unsatisfiable subsets back to the generator, is strongest in every setting (+1.8 to +15.0 pp over the best non-MUS baseline). But the central finding is what repair leaves behind. After structured feedback, models rarely contradict themselves. They forget. Residual errors are 98-100% satisfiable drift across all settings, while contradiction drops to near zero. Reliable multi-turn systems must separately validate that the returned answer respects the maintained state. Code is available at https://github.com/kaons-research/drift-bench.

</details>


### [81] [MEMOR-E: In-Context and Fine-Tuned LLM Personalization for Alzheimer's Assistive Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23941)
*Maissa Abir Smaili,Eren Sadikoglu,Ransalu Senanayake*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MEMOR-E is a quadruped robot with a tablet interface that uses fine-tuned LLMs and in-context learning to generate stage-aware cognitive summaries from Alzheimer's patient speech, enabling personalized assistive interactions and transparent human-robot interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alzheimer's disease causes progressive declines in memory and language that reduce independence, motivating the need for socially assistive robotic support for patients and caregivers.

Method: The paper presents MEMOR-E, a mobile quadruped robot with an interactive tablet. It fine-tunes LLMs on audio transcriptions from 235 Alzheimer's patients and synthetic healthy controls to emulate stage-consistent cognitive behavior. It also uses in-context learning (ICL) with a second LLM to produce domain- and severity-level cognitive error summaries.

Result: MEMOR-E generates stage-aware, non-diagnostic cognitive summaries that support personalized assistive interactions. Explainable AI mechanisms translate model outputs into transparent, human-readable evidence for caregiver oversight.

Conclusion: The approach enables trustworthy human-robot interaction through transparent, stage-aware cognitive summaries, supporting personalized assistance for Alzheimer's patients and caregivers.

Abstract: Alzheimer's disease is a neurodegenerative disorder marked by progressive declines in memory and language that reduce independence in daily life, motivating socially assistive robotic support. This paper presents MEMOR-E, a mobile quadruped robot with an interactive tablet interface that assists patients and caregivers through medication reminders, routine guidance, memory oriented interactions, and companionship. We evaluated the feasibility of fine tuning large language models (LLMs) to emulate stage consistent cognitive behavior and interpret responses across standard neuropsychological language tasks, using audio transcriptions from 235 Alzheimer's patients and synthetically generated healthy controls. We also report findings on using in context learning (ICL) in LLMs, where a second LLM produced domain and severity level cognitive error summaries. Our results show that MEMOR-E can generate stage aware, non diagnostic cognitive summaries that support personalized assistive interactions, while explainable AI mechanisms translate model outputs into transparent, human readable evidence to enable caregiver oversight and trustworthy human robot interaction.

</details>


### [82] [A Dynamical Framework for Cognitive Processes Based on Transformations and Semantic Equivalence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23942)
*Carlo Cattani,Dioneia Motta Monte-Serrat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a cybernetic cognitive framework where mental states evolve through feedback loops involving transformation, interpretation, and semantic stabilization, formalized using category theory and contraction-based dynamics, with a computational illustration and a linguistic case study on context-dependent meaning formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge dynamical systems, category theory, and cognitive modeling into a unified framework that treats cognition as a feedback-driven process converging toward stable (invariant) semantic interpretations.

Method: A state-space formalism with an iterative update rule X_{t+1} = π(F(f(X_t))) where f=internal transformation, F=interpretative mapping, π=semantic equivalence enforcement; categorical formulation for compositional structure; fixed-point and contraction analyses for stability.

Result: A computational illustration demonstrates operational viability; a qualitative dynamical analysis characterizes stability; a linguistic application shows context-dependent interpretation as convergence trajectories toward stable semantic classes.

Conclusion: Cognition can be unifiedly represented as a feedback-driven process evolving toward invariant interpretations, integrating dynamical systems, category theory, and cognitive modeling.

Abstract: This paper proposes a structural and dynamical framework for modeling cognitive processes within a cybernetic perspective. Cognitive states are represented as elements of a state space evolving through an iterative update rule of the form
  \[
  X_{t+1} = π\big(F(f(X_t))\big),
  \]
  where $f$ describes internal transformations, $F$ represents interpretative mappings, and $π$ enforces semantic equivalence.
  The model is interpreted as a feedback system integrating transformation, observation, and stabilization. A categorical formulation is introduced to capture compositional structure, while the associated dynamics are analyzed through fixed-point arguments and contraction conditions ensuring stability.
  To demonstrate the operational character of the framework, a computational illustration is provided, together with a qualitative analysis of the induced dynamics. A concrete linguistic application shows how context-dependent interpretation can be modeled as a trajectory toward a stable semantic class.
  The proposed approach connects dynamical systems, category theory, and cognitive modeling, and provides a unified representation of cognition as a feedback-driven process evolving toward invariant interpretations.

</details>


### [83] [Spacetime Formation under Requirements: Contextual Realization and Form-Dependent Probability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23943)
*Song-Ju Kim*

Main category: cs.AI
