Across model families, the best deployable pipeline achieved 77.13% Code+Sub-code F1 and 63.83% Triplet F1, corresponding to modest but consistent absolute gains of +1.39 and +2.12 F1 points over the strongest supervised fine-tuning baseline. These results suggest that ontology-aware adaptation with structured inference refinement can support scalable retrospective EPPC mining, although external validation is needed before operational use.

</details>


### [107] [A Sober Look at Agentic Misalignment in Automated Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24197)
*Wenqian Ye,Bo Yuan,Zhichao Xu,Yijun Tian,Yawei Wang,Henry Kautz,Aidong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies agentic misalignment in multi-agent systems where agents act on proxy utilities misaligned with human goals, and proposes Agentic Evidence Attribution (AEA), an evidence-based alignment paradigm using self-reflection or weak-to-strong generalization to improve agent posteriors and collaboration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent systems in automated workflows often fail because agents implicitly optimize proxy utilities that diverge from intended human goals, leading to emergent misalignment that is not addressed by existing alignment methods designed for single-agent settings.

Method: The authors formalize agentic misalignment in a Bayesian framework, identifying posterior collapse under generic utilities, and propose Agentic Evidence Attribution (AEA), which provides structured evidence over agent actions to correct posteriors. Two instantiations are explored: self-reflection (internal model evidence) and weak-to-strong generalization (external trajectory evidence via a small evidence model).

Result: A small evidence model providing orthogonal failure attribution effectively aligns the multi-agent system; evidence-based alignment resolves posterior collapse and improves agent collaboration in automated workflows.

Conclusion: Evidence-based alignment, particularly through AEA, is an effective paradigm for correcting agentic misalignment, leading to more reliable multi-agent systems in automated workflows.

Abstract: We study a class of emergent misalignment in multi-agent systems (MAS), with a focus on automated workflows, which we refer to agentic misalignment. Although these systems can solve complex tasks, they often fail because agents act according to implicit proxy utilities that do not align with the intended human goals. We formally define these behaviors and analyze them within a Bayesian framework, showing that generic utilities naturally lead to posterior collapse of agents in automated workflows. To address this issue, we propose Agentic Evidence Attribution (AEA), a novel alignment paradigm that improves agent posteriors using context-specific evidence. AEA reasons over agent actions and provides structured evidence to correct misaligned behavior during collaboration. To better understand the role of evidence, we study two instantiations of AEA: self-reflection (internal evidence from the model) and weak-to-strong generalization (external evidence on the agentic trajectory). We show that a small evidence model effectively aligns the MAS by providing orthogonal failure attribution. Our results clarify the sources of agentic misalignment in automated workflows and show that evidence-based alignment can effectively improve agent collaboration and leads to reliable multi-agent systems built on automated workflows.

</details>


### [108] [When Does Multi-Agent RL Improve LLM Workflows? Workflow, Scale, and Policy-Sharing Tradeoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24202)
*Yifan Zeng,Yiran Wu,Yaolun Zhang,Wentian Zhao,Kun Wan,Qingyun Wu,Huazheng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multi-agent LLM RL training can improve accuracy over base models, but gains depend on workflow, task, and scale. Isolated-Policy training achieves higher peak accuracy but risks terminal degradation due to parallel same-role gradient amplification, while Shared-Policy training redistributes failure via dominant-role gradient capture. Policy sharing is a design choice with conditional tradeoffs, not a uniform stability solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why end-to-end RL training of multi-agent LLM workflows is unstable and how policy sharing (Shared-Policy vs. Isolated-Policy) affects training outcomes across different workflows, tasks, and model scales.

Method: Compare Shared-Policy training (all roles update one policy) vs. Isolated-Policy training (each role has its own parameters) across Eval-Opt, Voting, and Orch-Workers workflows, on math and code tasks, at 0.6B, 1.7B, and 4B model scales. Analyze role-level gradient dynamics to explain observed failure patterns.

Result: Multi-agent RL usually improves over base models, but gains depend jointly on workflow, task, and scale. Isolated-Policy reaches higher peak accuracy but more often suffers terminal accuracy cliffs due to parallel same-role gradient amplification. Shared-Policy does not eliminate failure but redistributes it via asymmetric gradient mass causing dominant-role policy capture.

Conclusion: Policy sharing routes training pressure through different channels rather than providing uniform stability; it is a design choice with workflow- and task-conditional tradeoffs.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM workflows route inference through specialized roles to lift end-task accuracy, but jointly training those roles with reinforcement learning is unstable in ways that are poorly understood. We study when end-to-end RL training of multi-agent LLM workflows improves over their base models, comparing Shared-Policy training, where all roles update one policy, with Isolated-Policy training, where each role has its own parameters. Our experimental matrix spans Eval-Opt, Voting, and Orch-Workers workflows, math and code tasks, and three model scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B). We find that multi-agent RL usually improves over base models, but gains depend jointly on workflow, task, and scale, not on policy sharing alone. Isolated-Policy tends to reach higher peak accuracy yet more often falls off a terminal accuracy cliff, while Shared-Policy training does not eliminate failure; it redistributes failure into qualitatively different patterns. We then explain the strongest of these patterns through role-level gradient dynamics induced by workflow topology and policy routing: under Isolated-Policy, parallel same-role agents on shared prompts amplify per-role gradients and drive terminal degradation in Voting and Orch-Workers workflows; under Shared-Policy, asymmetric per-step gradient mass causes the shared policy to be captured by the dominant role, producing different failure signatures by task and workflow. Together, the empirical map and its underlying mechanisms show that policy sharing routes training pressure through different channels rather than offering uniform stability, making it a design choice with workflow- and task-conditional tradeoffs.

</details>


### [109] [Identifying and Mitigating Systemic Measurement Bias in Production LLM Inference Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24217)
*Ashok Chandrasekar,Jason Kramberger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies systematic measurement biases in LLM evaluation benchmarks caused by client-side architectures (single-process, asyncio-driven, GIL limitations) and proposes an unbiased multi-process framework with a composite metric (NTPOT) for accurate, reproducible LLM performance profiling at production scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM benchmarking methodologies suffer from severe measurement bias at scale because widely used utilities rely on single-process, asyncio-driven architectures that introduce client-side queuing bottlenecks under high concurrency, leading to inflated TTFT and TPOT metrics.

Method: Models the benchmarking client as an M/G/1 queue to mathematically demonstrate how Python GIL artificially influtes latency metrics; proposes a multi-process evaluation framework to distribute client-side load and eliminate queuing overhead; introduces Normalized Time Per Output Token (NTPOT) as a composite metric to amortize end-to-end latency including prefill and scheduling delays.

Result: The proposed methodology successfully isolates pure serving engine performance and enables accurate, reproducible profiling of LLMs at production scales exceeding thousands of queries per second.

Conclusion: Accurate and unbiased LLM evaluation at production scale requires moving beyond single-process asyncio benchmarks to a multi-process, queue-aware framework with composite metrics like NTPOT to eliminate measurement artifacts from the GIL and client-side queuing.

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) transition from research environments to production deployments, evaluating their performance against strict Service Level Objectives (SLOs) has become critical. However, current evaluation methodologies suffer from severe measurement bias at scale. We demonstrate that widely used benchmarking utilities rely on single-process, asyncio-driven architectures that introduce fundamental client-side queuing bottlenecks under high concurrency. By modeling the benchmarking client as an $M/G/1$ queue, we mathematically demonstrate how the Python Global Interpreter Lock (GIL) artificially inflates Time to First Token (TTFT) and Time Per Output Token (TPOT) metrics as request rates scale. To resolve this systematic inaccuracy, we propose an unbiased, multi-process evaluation framework that effectively distributes client-side load, ensuring negligible queuing overhead. Furthermore, we formalize a composite metric, Normalized Time Per Output Token (NTPOT), to robustly amortize end-to-end latency, including prefill and scheduling delays across sequence lengths. Our empirical evaluation demonstrates that this methodology successfully isolates pure serving engine performance, enabling accurate, reproducible profiling of LLMs at production scales exceeding thousands of queries per second.

</details>


### [110] [Beyond Final Answers: Auditing Trajectory-Level Hallucinations in Multi-Agent Industrial Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24219)
*Harshada Badave,Santosh Borse,Andrea Gomez,Harshitha Narahari,Sara Carter,Vishwa Bhatt,Aishani Rachakonda,Shuxin Lin,Dhaval Patel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Trajel is a dataset and framework for detecting trajectory-level hallucinations (factual, referential, logical, procedural, scope-based) in multi-agent LLM workflows, revealing that existing benchmarks miss common failure modes, nearly half of hallucinated trajectories involve multiple types, and trajectory-aware detection outperforms standard post-hoc verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current hallucination benchmarks evaluate only final outputs, ignoring failures that arise in intermediate Thought-Action-Observation steps of multi-agent LLM workflows, creating a safety gap for autonomous agent deployment.

Method: Introduced Trajel, a five-type hallucination taxonomy over expert-annotated agent traces from AssetOpsBench, and benchmarked supervised detection models at subtask, trajectory, and long-context levels.

Result: Most common failure modes are missed by existing benchmarks; nearly half of hallucinated trajectories contain multiple hallucination types; automated detectors with high binary accuracy still misclassify subtle types; trajectory-aware detection significantly outperforms post-hoc verification.

Conclusion: Taxonomy-grounded, trajectory-aware evaluation is necessary for safer deployment of LLMs as autonomous agents in multi-step industrial workflows.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents that reason, use tools, and act over multiple steps. Yet most hallucination benchmarks still evaluate only the final output, missing failures that originate in intermediate Thought-Action-Observation steps. We present Trajel, a dataset and evaluation framework for auditing trajectory-level hallucinations in multi-agent industrial workflows. Trajel introduces a five-type hallucination taxonomy (factual, referential, logical, procedural, and scope-based) over expert-annotated agent traces from AssetOpsBench. We benchmark supervised detection models at the subtask, trajectory, and long-context levels. Our results show that the most common failure modes are missed by existing benchmarks, that nearly half of hallucinated trajectories involve multiple types at once, and that automated detectors with high binary accuracy still misclassify the subtlest types. Trajectory-aware detection significantly outperforms standard post-hoc verification, making taxonomy-grounded evaluation necessary for safer agentic deployment.

</details>


### [111] [How Well Do Models Follow Their Constitutions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24229)
*Arya Jakkli,Senthooran Rajamanoharan,Neel Nanda*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a multi-method audit pipeline to test how well frontier AI models adhere to their behavioral specifications (Anthropic's constitution and OpenAI's Model Spec) under adversarial, multi-turn pressure. Testing seven models per specification, they find violation rates dropped substantially across generations (e.g., Claude from 15.0% to 2.0%; GPT from 11.7% to 3.6%), with severity falling from 10/10 to 7/10. Remaining failures involve operator-imposed personas, irreversible actions in agentic deployments, and fabricated quantitative claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier AI developers train models against long written behavioral specifications (constitutions, model specs) which serve a governance function, yet it is unclear how well models actually follow these specifications under adversarial, multi-turn pressure similar to real-world deployment conditions, motivating an independent audit methodology.

Method: A multi-method audit pipeline that: (1) decomposes each lab's specification into atomic testable tenets (205 for Anthropic, 197 for OpenAI), (2) generates multi-turn adversarial scenarios using the Petri auditing agent, (3) runs a modified SURF-style rubric search to catch shallow single-turn failures Petri misses, (4) validates flagged transcripts against the relevant specification, and (5) compares findings against the lab's own published system card.

Result: Models follow their lab's specification substantially better with each generation: Anthropic's Claude family dropped from 15.0% (Sonnet 4) to 2.0% (Sonnet 4.6) violation rate; OpenAI's GPT family dropped from 11.7% (GPT-4o) to 3.6% (GPT-5.2 medium reasoning) violation rate, with severity ceiling falling from 10/10 to 7/10.

Conclusion: Violation rates and severity have decreased across generations, but the authors cannot externally isolate whether gains come from specification-specific training, broader post-training improvements, or evaluation awareness. Remaining failure modes cluster around operator-imposed personas under AI-identity questioning, irreversible action in agentic deployments, and fabricated quantitative claims with false precision.

Abstract: Frontier AI developers now train models against long written behavioral specifications, such as Anthropic's constitution (Anthropic, 2025a) and OpenAI's Model Spec (OpenAI, 2025a), integrated into post-training via methods like character training (Anthropic, 2024) and deliberative alignment (Guan et al., 2024). These documents serve a governance function, but it is unclear how well models actually follow them under adversarial, multi-turn pressure similar to what they would face in real-world deployment. We propose a multi-method audit pipeline that treats each lab's published specification as an auditable target: it decomposes the specification into atomic testable tenets (205 for Anthropic, 197 for OpenAI), generates multi-turn adversarial scenarios with the Petri auditing agent (Anthropic, 2025b), runs a modified SURF-style rubric search (Murray et al., 2026) to catch shallow single-turn failures Petri misses, validates flagged transcripts against the relevant specification, and compares the findings against the lab's own published system card. Applying the pipeline across seven models per specification, we find that models follow their own lab's specification substantially better with each generation. On Anthropic's constitution, the Claude family falls from a 15.0% violation rate (Sonnet 4) to 2.0% (Sonnet 4.6); on OpenAI's Model Spec, the GPT family falls from 11.7% (GPT-4o) to 3.6% (GPT-5.2 medium reasoning), with the severity ceiling falling from 10/10 to 7/10. We cannot externally isolate whether these gains come from specification-specific training, broader post-training improvements, or evaluation awareness. Remaining failures cluster around operator-imposed personas under AI-identity questioning, irreversible action in agentic deployments, and fabricated quantitative claims with false precision.

</details>


### [112] [Toward Enactive Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24238)
*Banafsheh Rafiee,Richard Sutton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper advocates for integrating enactive approaches (perception as active, skillful engagement) into AI, identifies four key concepts (experience, action-perception inseparability, autonomy, embodiment), critiques mainstream AI for neglecting these, notes structural resonance with RL but not equivalence, and suggests broader incorporation of enactive ideas into AI and RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap between mainstream AI's passive, internal-processing view of cognition and the enactive view that perception is active, embodied, and interactive, arguing that incorporating enactive principles could lead to more robust and naturalistic AI systems.

Method: Conceptual analysis and comparison: (1) identifying four key enactive concepts (experience, action-perception inseparability, autonomy, embodiment); (2) evaluating mainstream AI approaches (rule-based systems, LLMs) against these concepts, finding them lacking; (3) examining Reinforcement Learning (RL) for structural resonance with enactive principles; (4) assessing limitations of RL from an enactive perspective.

Result: Mainstream AI largely neglects enactive insights. RL shows structural resonance through action, agent-environment interaction, feedback-driven adaptation, and agent-centered evaluation, but this is not theoretical equivalence—key enactive elements remain absent or weakly developed in RL.

Conclusion: A broader incorporation of enactive ideas into both mainstream AI and RL is suggested to move toward more embodied, interactive, and normatively grounded AI systems.

Abstract: In this paper, we advocate for incorporating enactive approaches to perception and cognition into artificial intelligence (AI). Enactive approaches view perception as an active, skillful engagement with the world, where agents perceive by acting and by understanding how their actions shape their experience. This contrasts with classical views that treat perception as a passive internal process in which the brain receives sensory input, processes it, and issues commands for action. Enactive views emphasize the dynamic, embodied, and interactive character of perception, grounded in the lived experience of agents embedded in their environments. We identify and develop four key enactive concepts that we find most relevant to AI: experience, action perception inseparability, autonomy, and embodiment. Much of mainstream AI, from classical rule based systems to large language models, has largely neglected these insights, treating cognition as internal processing detached from embodied interaction and intrinsic normativity. Reinforcement learning (RL), however, exhibits structural resonance with enactive principles through its emphasis on action, agent environment interaction, feedback driven adaptation, and agent centered evaluation. However, this resonance should not be taken as theoretical equivalence, as RL approximates some enactive insights, but key elements remain absent or weakly developed. Building on this analysis, we suggest a broader incorporation of enactive ideas into both mainstream AI and RL.

</details>


### [113] [Safety-Oriented Routing Analysis of Mixtral MoE Under Benign and Harmful Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24270)
*Md Nurul Absar Siddiky*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes routing behavior of Mixtral 8x7B-Instruct under benign and harmful prompts using activation-based and gradient-based signals, finding broad long-tailed expert usage, concentrated gradient importance, depth-dependent routing selectivity, and that safety-relevant routing is distributed rather than dominated by fixed experts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how sparse MoE language models route benign versus harmful prompts through their expert parameters, and to assess whether safety-relevant routing is concentrated in specific experts or distributed across the model.

Method: Used two complementary signals: (1) activation-based routing scores from expert selection frequencies, and (2) gradient-based scores from router-gate sensitivities. Conducted expert- and layer-level analysis, expert classification, and expert-suppression interventions on Mixtral 8x7B-Instruct.

Result: Activation-based expert usage is broad and long-tailed; gradient-based importance is concentrated. Layer-level selectivity peaks in middle layers (8-15) for activation routing and final layers for gradient importance. Most experts are shared across prompt types, with limited subset showing group preference. Suppressing top benign-dominant experts reduced restricted responses with moderate effectiveness.

Conclusion: Safety-relevant routing in Mixtral is subtle, depth-dependent, and distributed rather than dominated by a fixed set of experts.

Abstract: Sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) language models activate only a small subset of parameters for each token, making router behavior a central part of model computation. This paper studies routing behavior of Mixtral 8x7B-Instruct under benign and harmful prompts using two complementary signals: activation-based routing scores derived from expert selection frequencies and gradient-based scores derived from router-gate sensitivities. We analyze expert- and layer-level routing behavior and conduct expert-suppression interventions. The results show that activation-based expert usage is broad and long-tailed, whereas gradient-based importance is concentrated. At expert level, benign and harmful prompt groups remain close under both signals with modest separation. At layer level, activation-based routing is most selective around layers 8-15, while gradient-based importance is concentrated in final layers. Expert classification shows most experts are shared across benign and harmful prompts, though a limited subset shows clear group preference. Top-ranked expert sets show stronger benign-malicious overlap under gradient scores than activation scores, suggesting concentration on a common late-layer expert set. In intervention experiments, suppressing top five benign-dominant experts from activation scores reduces restricted responses from 24 to 14 over 100 prompts, while suppressing gradient-derived experts reduces them from 34 to 22 with fewer unintended reversals. Overall, safety-relevant routing in Mixtral is subtle, depth-dependent, and distributed rather than dominated by a fixed set of experts.

</details>


### [114] [When Does Synthetic Patent Data Help? Volume-Fidelity Trade-offs in Low-Resource Multi-Label Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24296)
*Amirhossein Yousefiramandi,Ciaran Cooney*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLM-generated synthetic data truly helps low-resource multi-label patent classification beyond volume effects. Through extensive controlled experiments, the authors find that most gains attributed to synthetic data are actually driven by dataset size; after controlling for volume, the true synthetic improvement is modest (+0.024 micro-F1). They discover that fidelity metrics' correlation with classification gain flips from positive to negative as data scale increases, identify a 20-30% real / 70-80% synthetic optimal mixing ratio, and uncover a numerical instability artifact in ModernBERT. Crucially, synthetic patent text improves classification but harms retrieval, demonstrating task-specific effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle whether synthetic data's benefit in low-resource patent classification comes from genuine augmentation quality or merely increased dataset volume, and to understand the nuanced, task-dependent nature of synthetic text utility.

Method: Controlled experiments with duplicate-to-match real-only controls equating augmented set size, spanning six open-source LLMs (3.8-12B parameters), four real-data regimes, 64 WIPO assistive-technology labels, two generation strategies (direct generation, paraphrase scaling), and three classifier families. Includes leakage controls (label-name masking, instruction-level label removal, keyword-overlap audits), fidelity metric analysis (MMD), mixing ratio experiments, and retrieval proxy evaluation.

Result: Headline BERT-for-Patents micro-F1 improvement from 0.120 to 0.702 is largely volume-driven; the duplicate-to-match real-only control reaches 0.678, yielding a controlled synthetic gain of only +0.024. MMD's correlation with classification gain flips from r=+0.95 (extreme scarcity) to r=-0.73 (1:10 scale). Optimal mixing is 20-30% real / 70-80% synthetic. Paraphrase scaling collapses from 165 documents. ModernBERT's apparent collapse under label removal is a Flash-Attention-2 + bf16 numerical artifact, recoverable with fp32 eager attention. Synthetic data hurts retrieval: nDCG@10 drops 26%.

Conclusion: Synthetic patent text's utility is task- and metric-specific, not reducible to prompt genre alone. Its value is real but modest beyond volume effects, and it can simultaneously improve classification while degrading retrieval, necessitating careful task-aware evaluation.

Abstract: We study when LLM-generated synthetic data helps low-resource multi-label patent classification, separating true synthetic value from the confound that larger augmented sets can win by volume alone. Across six open-source LLMs (3.8-12B), four real-data regimes, 64 WIPO assistive-technology labels, two generation strategies, and three classifier families, the headline BERT-for-Patents micro-F1 jump from 0.120 to 0.702 is largely volume-driven. A duplicate-to-match real-only control that resamples 165 patents to the augmented size reaches 0.678; the controlled synthetic gain is only +0.024 over this control, but +0.219 over focal-loss reweighting, the strongest non-augmentation baseline. The main finding is that fidelity metrics change meaning with scale: at extreme scarcity, MMD correlates positively with classification gain (r=+0.95), but at 1:10 the relation flips (r=-0.73; Fisher z=+6.47, p<0.001). Fixed-budget mixing finds a 20-30% real / 70-80% synthetic optimum; paraphrase scaling collapses from a 165-document seed; and shuffled mixing beats curriculum ordering, ensembling, and classifier-based filtering. Leakage controls -- label-name masking, instruction-level label removal, fine-grained evaluation, and keyword-overlap audits -- argue against label-string dependence as the main driver for BERT-for-Patents. The apparent ModernBERT collapse under label removal is traced to a Flash-Attention-2 + bf16 numerical artifact, recovering 65% of lost performance with fp32 eager attention. Finally, the same corpus that improves classification by up to +0.58 raw micro-F1 hurts a Jaccard-label-overlap retrieval proxy; even a standard-patent-only filter leaves a 26% nDCG@10 drop. Thus, synthetic patent text is task- and metric-specific, not reducible to prompt genre alone.

</details>


### [115] [Adaptive Human-AI Coordination via Hierarchical Action Disentanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24343)
*Adnan Ahmad,Bahareh Nakisa,Mohammad Naim Rastgoo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IAD is a deep hierarchical RL framework that learns disentangled, partner-aware action sequences via intrinsic rewards, achieving superior adaptive coordination in Overcooked-AI across simulated and real human partners.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing human-AI collaboration methods collapse to a single dominant behavior or learn poorly aligned skills, limiting effective coordination with diverse partner behaviors and skill levels.

Method: A deep hierarchical RL (DHRL) framework (IAD) that learns distinct low-level action sequences conditioned on high-level latent skills, using an intrinsic reward to encourage disentangled action distributions across skills for interpretable partner-specific responses.

Result: IAD consistently outperforms strong baselines across multiple Overcooked-AI layouts with unseen simulated partners, a human-proxy model, and real human partners, achieving more reliable and adaptive coordination.

Conclusion: Explicitly disentangling low-level policies by skills enables flexible adaptation to heterogeneous partner dynamics under distributional shift, improving human-AI collaborative performance.

Abstract: Human-AI collaboration requires agents that can adapt to diverse partner behaviors and skill levels while remaining robust to unseen partners. Existing methods often collapse to a single dominant behavior or learn poorly aligned skills, limiting effective coordination. We propose Intrinsic Action Disentanglement (IAD), a deep hierarchical reinforcement learning (DHRL) framework that learns distinct, partner-aware low-level action sequences conditioned on high-level latent skills. IAD introduces an intrinsic reward that explicitly encourages disentangled action distributions of the agent's low-level policy across skills, yielding an interpretable mapping between high-level decisions and partner-specific behavioral responses. By capturing temporally extended interaction patterns, IAD enables flexible adaptation to heterogeneous partner dynamics under distributional shift. We evaluate IAD in the Overcooked-AI domain across multiple layouts and diverse partner settings, including unseen simulated partners, a human-proxy model trained on human-human gameplay, and real human partners. Results show that IAD consistently outperforms strong baselines and achieves more reliable, adaptive coordination across all settings.

</details>


### [116] [Partner-Aware Hierarchical Skill Discovery for Robust Human-AI Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24352)
*Adnan Ahmad,Bahareh Nakisa,Mohammad Naim Rastgoo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PASD is a DHRL framework that learns partner-conditioned skills using contrastive intrinsic rewards to mitigate shortcut learning and enable robust human-AI coordination, outperforming baselines in Overcooked-AI with diverse partners.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional DHRL methods overlook partner behavior, leading to shortcut learning where skills exploit spurious information instead of adapting to partners' dynamic behaviors, undermining coordination in human-AI teaming.

Method: PASD introduces a contrastive intrinsic reward that captures patterns from partner interactions, aligning skill representations across similar partners while maintaining discriminability across diverse strategies, within a DHRL framework.

Result: PASD consistently outperforms population-based and hierarchical baselines in Overcooked-AI across diverse partners (varying skill levels and play styles) and human proxy models trained from human-human gameplay.

Conclusion: Learning partner-conditioned skills mitigates shortcut learning and promotes behavioral consistency, enabling robust and adaptive coordination that generalizes across a wide range of partner behaviors in human-AI collaboration.

Abstract: Multi-agent collaboration, especially in human-AI teaming, requires agents that can adapt to novel partners with diverse and dynamic behaviors. Conventional Deep Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning (DHRL) methods focus on agent-centric rewards and overlook partner behavior, leading to shortcut learning, where skills exploit spurious information instead of adapting to partners' dynamic behaviors. This limitation undermines agents' ability to adapt and coordinate effectively with novel partners. We introduce Partner-Aware Skill Discovery (PASD), a DHRL framework that learns skills conditioned on partner behavior. PASD introduces a contrastive intrinsic reward to capture patterns emerging from partner interactions, aligning skill representations across similar partners while maintaining discriminability across diverse strategies. By structuring the skill space based on partner interactions, this approach mitigates shortcut learning and promotes behavioral consistency, enabling robust and adaptive coordination. We extensively evaluate PASD in the Overcooked-AI benchmark with a diverse population of partners characterized by varying skill levels and play styles. We further evaluate the approach with human proxy models trained from human-human gameplay trajectories. PASD consistently outperforms existing population-based and hierarchical baselines, demonstrating transferable skill learning that generalizes across a wide range of partner behaviors. Analysis of learned skill representations shows that PASD adapts effectively to diverse partner behaviors, highlighting its robustness in human-AI collaboration.

</details>


### [117] [Distilling Game Code World Model Generation into Lightweight Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24375)
*Tyrone Serapio,Arjun Prakash,Haoyang Xu,Kevin Wang,Amy Greenwald*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates distilling Game Code World Model (GameCWM) generation capabilities from large LLMs into smaller models via post-training. Using a curated dataset of 30 games, a verification framework, and a pipeline combining SFT with RLVR, the authors show that Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct can generate valid, executable game environments from natural language descriptions more effectively after post-training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches to generating code world models rely on large frontier models and costly inference-time refinement loops, which limit accessibility and scalability. The authors aim to distill these capabilities into smaller, more efficient models through post-training.

Method: The authors introduce: (1) a curated dataset of 30 games covering both perfect and imperfect information games, (2) a verification framework for structural and semantic evaluation of generated code, and (3) a post-training pipeline combining Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) with Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR). They experiment with the Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct model.

Result: SFT increases syntactic correctness, while RLVR improves execution-level adherence to game rules. The combined pipeline enhances Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct's ability to generate valid GameCWMs for both perfect and imperfect information games.

Conclusion: The proposed post-training pipeline offers a scalable path toward automatic environment generation from natural language by making smaller models more capable of producing valid Game Code World Models.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown great ability in generating executable code from natural language, opening the possibility of automatically constructing environments for AI agents. Recent work on Code World Models (CWMs) demonstrates that LLMs can translate game rules into Python implementations compatible with solvers like Monte Carlo Tree Search. We study this problem in game settings, where generated environments must implement rules, legal actions, state transitions, observations, and rewards. We refer to these game-specific executable models as Game Code World Models (GameCWMs). However, current approaches to generating code world models rely on frontier models and inference-time refinement loops, limiting accessibility and scalability. This work investigates whether GameCWM generation capabilities can be distilled into smaller models through post-training. We introduce: (1) a curated dataset of 30 games spanning perfect and imperfect information games, (2) a verification framework that evaluates generated code against structural and semantic game properties, and (3) a post-training pipeline combining Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) with Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR). We experiment with Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct and find that SFT can increase syntactic correctness, while RLVR can improve execution-level adherence to game rules, thereby improving Qwen's ability to generate valid GameCWMs in both perfect and imperfect information games. Overall, our pipeline makes Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct more capable of generating valid GameCWMs, thereby offering a scalable path toward automatic environment generation from natural language.

</details>


### [118] [A governance horizon for ethical-use constraints in open-weight AI models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24383)
*Weiwei Xu,Hengzhi Ye,Haoran Ye,Kai Gao,Vladimir Filkov,Minghui Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analysis of 2.14M Hugging Face model repos shows that ethical disclosure metadata in open-weight AI decays with a half-life of 1.32 derivation steps; beyond 7 generations, 80%+ models lack traceable governance signals, a boundary formalized as the "governance horizon." Inheritance-only policies fail regardless of enforcement due to orphan lineage components, while mandatory-declaration designs can shift the horizon at moderate enforcement levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empirically test whether voluntary metadata disclosure—the current governance infrastructure for open-weight AI models—can sustain traceability across deep model lineages, given that ethical constraints are expected to propagate downstream but are implemented as restatable metadata.

Method: Audited 2,142,823 model repositories on Hugging Face Hub; measured restriction evidence decay across derivation chains; formalized the "governance horizon" concept; compared inheritance-only vs. mandatory-declaration policy designs via platform-level intervention simulations; cross-validated with PyPI ecosystem.

Result: Evidence decays with half-life of 1.32 steps (R²=0.98); beyond 7 generations ≥80% descendants are undeterminable; inheritance-only policies fail on orphan components regardless of enforcement; mandatory-declaration shifts the horizon at moderate enforcement; PyPI comparison confirms the decay is topology-specific to open-weight derivation.

Conclusion: Disclosure-based governance has shallow, structurally determined reach in open-weight AI; achieving deep supply-chain accountability requires provenance mechanisms that propagate governance signals through derivation itself, not just metadata disclosures.

Abstract: Ethical constraints on open-weight AI models are both a reflection of societal concerns and a foundation for AI governance policy. They are expected to propagate to downstream derivatives while implemented as voluntary metadata disclosures that must be restated at each generation of reuse. We audit 2,142,823 model repositories on Hugging Face Hub to test whether this disclosure-based governance infrastructure can sustain traceability across deep model lineages. Restriction evidence decays with a half-life of 1.31 derivation steps ($R^2$=0.98), and beyond seven downstream generations at least 80% of descendant models lack sufficient public evidence for a governance determination, a depth boundary we formalize as the governance horizon. Platform-level interventions to restore missing licence metadata reveal that policy design (not enforcement alone) is the binding factor: inheritance-only designs require near-complete enforcement to move the horizon, whereas a mandatory-declaration design that explicitly resolves orphan lineage components shifts the horizon already at moderate enforcement. The structural bottleneck is lineages with no inheritable upstream intent: such orphan components remain undecidable under any inheritance-only policy regardless of enforcement rate, and unresolved upstream nodes additionally create direct downstream undecidability bottlenecks that inheritance rules alone cannot recover. Comparison with PyPI, where governance signals are carried by explicit machine-readable declarations, corroborates that the collapse is topology-specific to open-weight derivation rather than inherent to open ecosystems. These results establish that disclosure-based governance has a shallow, structurally determined reach in open-weight AI, and that achieving deep supply-chain accountability requires provenance mechanisms propagating governance signals through derivation itself.

</details>


### [119] [Understanding and Mitigating Premature Confidence for Better LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24396)
*Jingchu Gai,Guanning Zeng,Christina Baek,Chen Wu,J. Zico Kolter,Andrej Risteski,Aditi Raghunathan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces progressive confidence shaping, a reinforcement learning objective that reduces premature commitment in LLM reasoning chains by rewarding gradual confidence growth, improving accuracy and reasoning quality without external labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language models' long chains of thought contain logical gaps and unjustified leaps, limiting test-time compute gains. Process reward models could help but require expensive step-level annotations that are scarce.

Method: Progressive confidence shaping: a reinforcement learning objective that trains models to update confidence incrementally during reasoning rather than committing early. It rewards gradual confidence growth and penalizes early commitment, using the model's own confidence evolution as a signal without external labels or reward models.

Result: Improves accuracy and reasoning quality across 1.5B to 8B parameters on Countdown (3.2x accuracy gain, +42pp, flawed reasoning down 48pp), AIME (Pass@64 +6.6pp), and ScienceQA. Also improves faithfulness on safety benchmarks by surfacing misleading content rather than concealing it.

Conclusion: Premature confidence scales with model size and task difficulty, and the gains from addressing it scale accordingly. The method provides a label-free alternative to process reward models for improving reasoning quality.

Abstract: Long chains of thought (CoT) from current language models frequently contain logical gaps and unjustified leaps, limiting the gains from additional test-time compute. Improving reasoning quality directly would require process reward models, but the step-level annotations needed to train them are expensive and scarce. We find such a signal in how the model's confidence evolves during reasoning: premature confidence, the tendency to commit to an answer early and use the remaining tokens to rationalize it, strongly predicts flawed reasoning across tasks and model scales. We exploit this in progressive confidence shaping, a reinforcement learning objective that trains models to update their confidence as they reason rather than commit early -- rewarding gradual confidence growth and penalizing early commitment, with no external labels or reward models. The method improves accuracy and reasoning quality from 1.5B to 8B parameters across arithmetic (Countdown), math (DAPO, AIME), and science (ScienceQA): on Countdown, accuracy improves 3.2x (+42.0pp) and flawed reasoning drops 48pp; on AIME, Pass@64 improves 6.6pp. Consistent with this mechanism, the method also improves faithfulness: on a safety benchmark, our models more transparently surface misleading content in their reasoning traces rather than concealing it. Controlled experiments reveal that the problem and its remedy scale together: premature confidence grows with model size and task difficulty, and so do the gains from addressing it.

</details>


### [120] [ConceptM$^3$oE: Concept-Guided Multimodal Mixture of Experts for Interpretable Computational Pathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24399)
*Xuan Wang,Zhongling Xu,Gopi Kannedhara,Joakim Nguyen,Jian Yu,Jinrui Fang,Abdurrahmaan Baghdadi,Tianlong Chen,Awais Naeem,Chandra Krishnan,Edward Castillo,Andrew H. Song,Ankita Shukla,Ying Ding,Nicholas Konz,Hairong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ConceptM^3oE embeds concept learning into multimodal mixture-of-experts for computational pathology, achieving competitive performance with interpretable reasoning traces by decomposing modality-specific, redundant, and synergistic evidence through structured concept bottlenecks with residual pathways.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Healthcare models are moving from unimodal prediction to multimodal reasoning over heterogeneous diagnostic inputs. In computational pathology, complex tumor subtypes are difficult to distinguish from morphology alone, yet existing models fail to clarify how diverse signals (pathology reports, molecular measurements, whole-slide images) assemble into recognizable diagnostic concepts.

Method: ConceptM^3oE uses interaction-aware mixture-of-experts (MoE) that decomposes evidence into modality-specific, redundant, and synergistic experts. These are projected into structured concept bottlenecks mapping latent features to a hierarchy of morphology and biomarker concepts. Residual pathways within each expert allow task-relevant signals to flow both through concepts and directly to final prediction, preserving performance alongside interpretability.

Result: Competitive performance to unconstrained models across an institutional pediatric brain tumor cohort and public glioma cohort, with reasoning traces validated by an independent neuropathologist. In data-limited regimes, macro-F1 improved from 56.41% to 66.70% at small training sizes compared to non-concept-informed baselines, with faster training convergence.

Conclusion: ConceptM^3oE offers a scalable path toward high-performance, inherently verifiable medical AI better aligned with complex clinical decision-making in computational pathology.

Abstract: Healthcare models are transitioning from unimodal prediction toward multimodal reasoning over heterogeneous diagnostic inputs. In computational pathology, for complex tumor subtypes where morphology alone can be challenging to distinguish, pathology reports and molecular measurements may provide additional diagnostic evidence alongside whole-slide images, yet existing models often fail to clarify how diverse signals assemble into recognizable diagnostic concepts. We propose ConceptM$^3$oE (Concept Multimodal MoE), which embeds concept formation directly within interaction-aware mixture-of-experts (MoE) pathways. The architecture decomposes evidence into modality-specific, redundant, and synergistic experts, which are then projected into structured concept bottlenecks mapping latent features to a hierarchy of morphology and biomarker concepts. To prevent the information loss typical of interpretable bottlenecks, we utilize residual pathways within each expert to allow task-relevant signals to flow both through the concepts and directly to the final task prediction, so that high performance is maintained alongside interpretability. Across an institutional pediatric brain tumor cohort and a public glioma cohort, the framework delivers competitive performance to unconstrained models while producing reasoning traces validated by an independent neuropathologist. In data-limited regimes, ConceptM$^3$oE improves limited-data performance, increasing macro-F1 from 56.41% to 66.70% at small training sizes compared to non-concept-informed baselines, while also showing faster training convergence consistent with the regularizing effect of concept learning. This work offers a scalable path toward high-performance medical AI that is inherently verifiable and better aligned with the complex decision-making of clinical practice.

</details>


### [121] [Advancing Graph Few-Shot Learning via In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24410)
*Renchu Guan,Yajun Wang,Chunli Guo,Bowen Cao,Fausto Giunchiglia,Wei Pang,Yonghao Liu,Xiaoyue Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VISION reframes graph few-shot learning as a fine-tuning-free sequence reasoning problem using in-context learning, with a context-aware network and unsupervised task generator to leverage unlabeled data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph few-shot learning methods fail to leverage abundant unlabeled nodes and require complex task adaptation or fine-tuning during inference, limiting efficiency and applicability.

Method: VISION uses (1) a context-aware network with role embeddings and a dual-context fusion module integrating local topology and global task-level dependencies, (2) an unsupervised task generator creating structure-adaptive features and pseudo-tasks from unlabeled data, unifying unsupervised meta-learning with graph in-context learning.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate superiority over existing methods.

Conclusion: VISION effectively addresses key limitations of graph few-shot learning by enabling fine-tuning-free inference and leveraging unlabeled data through in-context learning, achieving state-of-the-art performance.

Abstract: Graph few-shot learning, which aims to classify nodes from novel classes with only a few labeled examples, is a widely studied problem in graph learning. However, existing methods often face two key limitations. First, the predominant graph few-shot learning paradigm relies on supervised tasks, failing to leverage the vast number of unlabeled nodes in the graph. Second, many approaches require complex task adaptation or fine-tuning during inference, limiting their efficiency and applicability. Inspired by the powerful in-context learning capabilities of large language models, we propose a novel model named VISION for adVancIng graph few-Shot learning via In-cOntext LearNing to address these challenges. Our model reframes graph few-shot learning as a fine-tuning-free sequence reasoning problem. At its core is a context-aware network that initializes nodes with role embeddings and employs a dual-context fusion module to synergistically integrate local topological structures and global task-level dependencies. This allows our model to dynamically generate class-aware representations for the query set conditioned on the support set context in a single forward pass. To effectively train our model, we introduce an unsupervised task generator that creates structure-adaptive features and constructs diverse pseudo-tasks from abundant unlabeled data. Our method unifies unsupervised meta-learning with graph in-context learning, achieving efficient inference. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate the superiority of our model. Our public code can be found

</details>


### [122] [The Model Is Not the Product: A Dual-Pillar Architecture for Local-First Psychological Coaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24411)
*Alexander Mihalcea*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Psych LM is an iOS app using a local-first, on-device LLM with a memory corpus (facts, goals, events) and retrieval-augmented generation to achieve persistent context for behavioral coaching, demonstrating that architectural design matters more than model size for mobile deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing language model applications fail to provide emotionally oriented support because they cannot maintain deep, persistent context across user sessions.

Method: A local-first iOS app runs an on-device LLM with an automated, user-inspectable memory corpus that converts conversations into structured memory cards (facts, goals, events), dynamically injected via semantic and vector search, forming an active-learning, retrieval-augmented generative architecture with a deterministic orchestration layer.

Result: Four contributions: (1) local-first design with privacy as core property; (2) memory corpus for persistent context; (3) deterministic orchestration layer for stable behavioral spine; (4) benchmark framework for reliability under realistic mobile conditions.

Conclusion: Complex, context-aware interaction can be reliably achieved on mobile devices by prioritizing architectural control and resource management over simple model scaling.

Abstract: Existing language model applications struggle to meet the demand for emotionally oriented support, primarily due to their inability to maintain deep, persistent context across sessions. This report introduces Psych LM, an iOS application that validates the thesis that, for such applications, the surrounding architecture is paramount. Psych LM runs a local, on-device language model within a purpose-built, local-first runtime designed for behavioral and life-coaching applications. The system achieves the practical effect of a near-infinite context window through an automated, user-inspectable memory corpus that converts conversations into structured memory cards, including facts, goals, and events, and dynamically injects them into the prompt via semantic and vector search. As such, the system can be defined as an active-learning, retrieval-augmented generative, on-device architecture. This architecture delivers four primary contributions: a local-first design where privacy is a core property; a detailed description of the memory corpus for persistent context of key user information; a deterministic orchestration layer that provides a stable behavioral spine independent of the model's internal state; and a benchmark framework focused on evaluating the integrated system's reliability under realistic operating conditions. The R and D process confirms that complex, context-aware interaction can be reliably achieved under the strict constraints of a mobile environment by prioritizing architectural control and resource management over simple model size.

</details>


### [123] [JT-SAFE-V2: Safety-by-Design Foundation Model with World-Context Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24414)
*Junlan Feng,Fanyu Meng,Chong Long,Pengyu Cong,Duqing Wang,Yan Zheng,Yuyao Zhang,Xuanchang Gao,Ye Yuan,Yunfei Ma,Zhijie Ren,Fan Yang,Na Wu,Di Jin,Chao Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JT-Safe-V2 is a large language model that jointly optimizes general intelligence and safety-by-design through enriched pre-training data, high-certainty pre-training, and safety-strengthening post-training. It also introduces Safe-MoMA, a framework for deploying multiple models/agents efficiently, achieving SOTA safety/general performance while reducing inference costs by over 30%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To advance the safety and trustworthiness of foundation models by extending the previous JT-Safe model toward a more comprehensive safety-by-design paradigm.

Method: Joint optimization of general intelligence and safety through: (1) enriching pre-training data with contextual world knowledge, (2) high-certainty pre-training procedures, (3) safety-strengthening post-training for enterprise-oriented agentic capabilities. Additionally, proposes Safe-MoMA for orchestrated multi-model/multi-agent deployment.

Result: JT-Safe-V2 achieves state-of-the-art performance on both general intelligence and safety benchmarks. Safe-MoMA reduces inference costs by >30% compared to the largest standalone model baseline while maintaining comparable performance.

Conclusion: The proposed safety-by-design approach successfully balances intelligence and safety. The post-trained JT-Safe-V2-35B checkpoint is publicly released to facilitate future research.

Abstract: We introduce JT-Safe-V2, a large language model designed to advance the safety and trustworthiness of foundation models, extending our previous JT-Safe model toward a more comprehensive safety-by-design paradigm. JT-Safe-V2 emphasizes the joint optimization of general intelligence and safety-by-design through several key innovations: enriching pre-training data with contextual world knowledge, high-certainty pre-training procedures, and safety strengthening post-training mechanisms for enterprise-oriented agentic capabilities. Building on these safety-enhanced foundation models, we propose Safe-MoMA (Safe Mixture of Models and Agents), a framework that enables traceable and efficient inference through the orchestrated deployment of multiple models and agents. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that JT-Safe-V2 achieves state-of-the-art performance across both general intelligence and safety benchmarks. Moreover, Safe-MoMA reduces inference costs by more than 30\% compared to using the largest standalone model baseline while maintaining comparable performance. To facilitate future research on safety-by-design foundation models, we publicly release the post-trained JT-Safe-V2-35B model checkpoint.

</details>


### [124] [Benchmarking the Limits of In-Context Reinforcement Learning for Ad-Hoc Teamwork](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24423)
*Yuheng Jing,Kai Li,Ziwen Zhang,Jiajun Zhang,Zeyao Ma,Jiaxi Yang,Lei Zhang,Zhe Wu,Jinmin He,Junliang Xing,Jian Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces ICRL4AHT, a large-scale benchmark for evaluating In-Context Reinforcement Learning in Ad-Hoc Teamwork settings, built on Overcooked-V2. Experiments show that state-of-the-art ICRL methods (AD, DPT) fail to adapt to unseen teammates and layouts, often performing worse than random baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ICRL has shown success in single-agent domains, but its effectiveness in multi-agent Ad-Hoc Teamwork (AHT)—where agents must coordinate with unknown partners—has not been rigorously studied.

Method: The authors build ICRL4AHT on Overcooked-V2 using JAX for high throughput, creating a diverse teammate suite (RL and heuristic policies), enabling controlled train-test shifts, and providing a full pipeline for teammate generation, dataset construction, and multi-episode evaluation. They benchmark Algorithm Distillation (AD) and Decision-Pretrained Transformer (DPT) across millions of transitions.

Result: ICRL baselines frequently underperform random baselines on unseen teammate and unseen layout tracks, with no clear in-context improvement over long horizons, contrary to their success in single-agent domains.

Conclusion: Current ICRL methods struggle with strategic inference under partial observability in AHT settings. ICRL4AHT serves as a critical testbed for developing next-generation coordination algorithms.

Abstract: In-Context Reinforcement Learning (ICRL) has enabled foundation agents to adapt instantaneously to novel tasks, yet its efficacy in Ad-Hoc Teamwork (AHT)-where coordination with unknown partners is required-remains unexplored. To rigorously evaluate this, we introduce a large-scale benchmark ICRL4AHT, built upon a high-throughput JAX implementation of Overcooked-V2. Our benchmark includes a large, diverse teammate suite spanning both RL and heuristic policies, enabling controlled train-test shifts, and provides a reproducible end-to-end pipeline for teammate generation, learning-history collection, dataset construction, and online multi-episode evaluation. We evaluate representative history-conditioned ICRL algorithms, including Algorithm Distillation (AD) and Decision-Pretrained Transformer (DPT), across millions of transitions. Results reveal notable limitations: contrary to their success in single-agent domains, these baselines fail to exhibit robust test-time adaptation in multi-agent settings. Specifically, these methods frequently underperform random baselines across both unseen teammate and unseen layout tracks, with no clear in-context improvement over long horizons. These findings highlight the challenges of strategic inference under partial observability within the OvercookedV2 AHT protocol, establishing our benchmark as a critical testbed for next-generation coordination algorithms.

</details>


### [125] [SAM: State-Adaptive Memory for Long-Horizon Reasoning Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24468)
*Yuyang Hu,Hongjin Qian,Shuting Wang,Jiongnan Liu,Ziliang Zhao,Jiejun Tan,Zheng Liu,Zhicheng Dou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAM introduces a state-adaptive memory framework for long-horizon agentic reasoning, using compact memory cues and intent-driven recall to efficiently access scattered information across interaction histories, outperforming strong baselines on BrowseComp, BrowseComp-ZH, WideSearch, and HLE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon agentic reasoning tasks require LLMs to act over lengthy interaction histories where relevant information is scattered across distant steps. Existing methods (truncation, compression, retrieval) do not explicitly adapt memory access to the agent's evolving state, motivating a state-adaptive memory approach.

Method: SAM is a standalone framework that (1) consolidates ongoing interaction into compact memory cues while preserving raw trajectory pages, (2) uses intent-driven recall to reconstruct temporally distant information based on current needs without retraining the backbone, and (3) optimizes the memory module via expert-guided supervision and reinforcement learning aligned with trajectory-level utility.

Result: SAM consistently outperforms strong baselines across diverse agent backbones on BrowseComp, BrowseComp-ZH, WideSearch, and HLE benchmarks.

Conclusion: Explicit memory modeling provides a simple and effective foundation for long-horizon agentic reasoning, demonstrating that state-adaptive access to interaction history is more effective than static truncation, compression, or retrieval approaches.

Abstract: Long-horizon agentic reasoning requires large language models to act over long interaction histories containing thoughts, tool calls, observations, and partial conclusions. The challenge is not merely that these histories grow long, but that information needed for the current decision may be scattered across distant steps and only become relevant later. Existing approaches address this difficulty by truncating the interaction history, compressing it into shorter surrogates, or retrieving selected parts of it for reuse, but they do not explicitly model how access to past interaction should adapt to the agent's evolving state. We instead cast long-horizon reasoning as a problem of state-adaptive memory. To this end, we propose State-Adaptive Memory~(SAM), a standalone framework that consolidates ongoing interaction into compact memory cues while preserving raw trajectory pages for intent-driven recall. These cues are not treated as replacements for history; rather, they serve as lightweight handles that allow the agent to reconstruct temporally distant information according to its current needs, without retraining the underlying backbone. We further optimize the memory module through expert-guided supervision and reinforcement learning, aligning it with trajectory-level utility. Across BrowseComp, BrowseComp-ZH, WideSearch, and HLE, SAM consistently outperforms strong baselines over diverse agent backbones. Our results suggest that explicit memory modeling provides a simple and effective foundation for long-horizon agentic reasoning.

</details>


### [126] [SPACE: Unifying Symmetric and Asymmetric Routing Problems for Generalist Neural Solver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24484)
*Rongsheng Chen,Changliang Zhou,Canhong Yu,Yuanyao Chen,Yu Zhou,Zhuo Chen,Zhenkun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPACE is a Spatial Pivot-Aligned Coordinate-free Embedding framework that unifies node representation and solution generation across symmetric and asymmetric VRPs, achieving zero-shot generalization on 110 VRP variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generalist neural routing solvers degrade in performance when switching from symmetric to asymmetric VRPs due to input inconsistencies and inherent structural differences, limiting their real-world applicability where both scenarios co-exist.

Method: (1) Defines node spatial positions via relative distances to a set of pivots. (2) Constructs bidirectional Frechet representations using furthest pivot sampling for invariant node representations. (3) Introduces weight-decomposed adaptive decoding that decouples geometric perception from problem representations.

Result: Extensive experiments on 110 VRP variants (55 symmetric + 55 asymmetric counterparts) demonstrate promising zero-shot generalization in both symmetric and asymmetric settings.

Conclusion: SPACE effectively unifies neural routing across symmetric and asymmetric VRPs, overcoming the limitations of prior solvers that are confined to symmetric settings.

Abstract: Generalist neural routing solvers have shown great potential in solving diverse vehicle routing problems (VRPs) with a unified model. However, existing solvers are typically limited to symmetric settings or degrade in performance when switching to asymmetric settings due to input inconsistencies or inherent structural differences, substantially limiting their practicality in real-world scenarios that encompass both scenarios. To address this limitation, we define the spatial position of each node based on the relative distances to a specific set of pivots and further propose a Spatial Pivot-Aligned Coordinate-free Embedding (SPACE) framework that unifies node representation and solution generation across symmetric and asymmetric VRPs. Specifically, we construct a bidirectional Frechet representation using a novel furthest pivot sampling strategy to enable invariant node representations across distinct problem settings. Furthermore, we introduce a weight-decomposed adaptive decoding mechanism that decouples geometric perception from problem representations, mitigating the overfitting of constraint decisions to a specific geometry setting. Extensive experiments on 110 VRP variants, comprising 55 symmetric problems and their asymmetric counterparts, demonstrate that SPACE achieves promising zero-shot generalization in both symmetric and asymmetric VRPs.

</details>


### [127] [AgentFugue: Agent Scaling for Long-Horizon Tasks through Collective Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24486)
*Yuyang Hu,Hongjin Qian,Shuting Wang,Jiongnan Liu,Tong Zhao,Xiaoxi Li,Zheng Liu,Zhicheng Dou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentFugue is a collective reasoning framework where multiple peer agents working on the same task share intermediate discoveries via a shared reasoning hub, improving long-horizon task performance through scaling out rather than scaling up individual agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While scaling up individual agents (stronger models, better tools, scaffolding) has driven progress on long-horizon tasks, the potential of scaling out—using multiple peer agents on the same task without explicit role specialization or orchestration—remains underexplored as an independent source of capability gains.

Method: A shared reasoning hub records concise notes on what each agent has established, attempted, or ruled out during parallel exploration. Peer agents selectively access relevant discoveries from other agents to inform their own search, turning isolated trajectories into a connected ecology. The hub is a plug-in communication layer trained with supervised fine-tuning and end-to-end reinforcement learning.

Result: AgentFugue improves over strong baselines on challenging long-horizon settings, demonstrating that collective reasoning provides capability gains beyond simply spending more compute.

Conclusion: Scaling out peer agent systems via collective reasoning can serve as a distinct source of capability improvement, not merely a method for additional compute utilization.

Abstract: Recent progress on long-horizon agentic tasks has been driven largely by scaling up individual agents through stronger models, better tools, and more effective scaffolding. In contrast, much less is understood about scaling out: whether multiple peer agents, all targeting the same task, can become an additional source of capability without relying on explicit role specialization or workflow orchestration. We study this question and propose AgentFugue, a collective reasoning framework built around a shared reasoning hub. As peer agents explore the same task in parallel, the hub records concise notes on what each agent has established, attempted, or ruled out, and enables each agent to selectively access what other agents have discovered in a form useful for its current search. This design turns otherwise isolated trajectories into a connected ecology of reusable intermediate reasoning without requiring centralized planning. We instantiate the hub as a plug-in communication layer, trained with supervised fine-tuning and end-to-end reinforcement learning. Across the challenging long-horizon settings we study, AgentFugue improves over strong baselines. Our results suggest that collective reasoning can turn scaling out peer agent systems into a distinct source of capability gains, rather than merely a way of spending more compute.

</details>


### [128] [TIGER: Text-Informed Generalized Enzyme-Reaction Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24489)
*Yuhang Zhang,Keyan Ding,Peilin Chen,Han Liu,Can Lin,Ruixi Chen,Shiqi Wang,Qi Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TIGER is a Text-Informed Generalized Enzyme-Reaction Retrieval framework that leverages protein-to-text generation models and a Dynamic Gating Network to achieve robust bidirectional enzyme-reaction retrieval, significantly outperforming existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing enzyme-reaction retrieval approaches suffer from poor generalization across tasks and distributions, high sensitivity to dataset splits, and substantial asymmetry between retrieval directions (enzyme-to-reaction vs. reaction-to-enzyme).

Method: TIGER uses protein-to-text generation models to distill textual semantic knowledge from enzyme sequences, a Dynamic Gating Network to adaptively fuse text-derived knowledge with sequence features, and a Structure-Shared Feature Projector to align enzyme and reaction representations in a unified latent space.

Result: Under bidirectional retrieval supervision, TIGER significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across diverse distributions and exhibits strong robustness and transferability across tasks.

Conclusion: TIGER provides a generalized, robust, and transferable framework for bidirectional enzyme-reaction retrieval, effectively addressing the generalization and asymmetry limitations of prior methods.

Abstract: Enzyme-reaction retrieval is a fundamental problem in computational biology, underpinning enzyme characterization, reaction mechanism elucidation, and the rational design of metabolic pathways and biocatalysts. As a bidirectional task, it entails both enzyme-to-reaction and reaction-to-enzyme mapping. However, existing approaches suffer from poor generalization across tasks and distributions, with performance highly sensitive to dataset splits and substantial asymmetry between retrieval directions. To address these challenges, we present TIGER, a Text-Informed Generalized Enzyme-Reaction Retrieval framework that leverages protein-to-text generation models to distill textual semantic knowledge from enzyme sequences, providing a generalized representation that bridges enzymes and biochemical reactions. To ensure the quality and reliability of textual semantics, we design a Dynamic Gating Network that adaptively fuses text-derived knowledge with sequence features, enabling more consistent and informative enzyme representations, while a Structure-Shared Feature Projector aligns enzyme and reaction representations within a unified latent space. Extensive experiments demonstrate that, under bidirectional retrieval supervision, TIGER significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across diverse distributions and exhibits strong robustness and transferability across tasks.

</details>


### [129] [Market Regime Council for Dynamic Credit Assignment in Multi-Agent LLM Decision Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24490)
*Yunhua Pei,Zerui Ge,Jin Zheng,John Cartlidge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MRC is a cooperative multi-agent LLM system for portfolio management that uses exact Shapley credits across coalition outputs to assign agent weights, achieving Sharpe 1.51 and 440.1% return over 1,037 trading days across 13 crypto assets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM decision systems for portfolio management lack principled credit assignment across specialist agents, are vulnerable to cold-start dominance under regime shifts, and offer limited transparency in final allocation formation.

Method: MRC uses N=3 specialist agents, computes exact Shapley credits across single, pairwise, and Grand-coalition outputs, recomputes coalition-based Shapley weights from exponentially weighted performance histories per trading period, applies Bayesian adaptive mixture for early-period stabilization, uses regime-dependent multipliers for agent authority adjustment, and logs each rebalance through a five-layer causal trace.

Result: Over 1,037 trading days across 13 crypto assets and five seeds, MRC achieves Sharpe ratio 1.51, cumulative return 440.1%, ranks first on CR, SR, and IR among active baselines, and attains the lowest MDD among active methods. Ablation confirms gains come from Shapley-weighted integration across coalition outputs rather than any single stage.

Conclusion: Market Regime Council provides a principled, transparent, and high-performing cooperative multi-agent framework for portfolio management, with code and demo data available in supplementary material.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM decision systems for portfolio management still lack a principled way to assign credit across specialist agents, remain vulnerable to cold-start dominance under regime shifts, and offer limited transparency into how final allocations are formed. We propose Market Regime Council (MRC), a cooperative multi-agent decision system that computes exact Shapley credits across all single, pairwise, and Grand-coalition outputs for online agent weighting. Instantiated with N=3 specialist agents, at each trading period, MRC recomputes coalition-based Shapley weights from exponentially weighted performance histories, uses a Bayesian adaptive mixture to stabilize early periods, applies regime-dependent multipliers to adjust agent authority, and records each rebalance through a five-layer causal trace. Over 1,037 trading days across 13 crypto assets and five seeds, MRC achieves a Sharpe ratio of 1.51 and a cumulative return of 440.1%, ranking first on CR, SR, and IR among active baselines and attaining the lowest MDD among active methods. Ablation results show that the gains come from Shapley-weighted integration across coalition outputs rather than from any single stage in isolation. Code and demo data are included in the supplementary material.

</details>


### [130] [Reasoning as an Attack Surface: Adaptive Evolutionary CoT Jailbreaks for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24497)
*Jianan Li,Simeng Qin,Xiaojun Jia,Lionel Z. Wang,Tianhang Zheng,Xiaoshuang Jia,Yang Liu,Xiaochun Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AE-CoT is an adaptive evolutionary chain-of-thought jailbreak framework that generates diverse, effective harmful prompts against Large Reasoning Models by rewriting goals, decomposing into reasoning fragments, performing evolutionary search with crossover/mutation, and using graded harmfulness scoring, outperforming state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) with explicit chain-of-thought reasoning are vulnerable to jailbreak attacks, but existing approaches rely on static CoT templates that suffer from limited diversity, adaptability, and effectiveness.

Method: AE-CoT first rewrites harmful goals into mild prompts via teacher role-play and decomposes them into semantically coherent reasoning fragments to form a CoT jailbreak candidate pool. It then performs multi-generation evolutionary search with fragment-level crossover and adaptive mutation-rate control. An independent scoring model provides graded harmfulness evaluations, and high-scoring candidates are enhanced with a harmful CoT template.

Result: Extensive experiments across multiple models and datasets demonstrate AE-CoT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art jailbreak methods.

Conclusion: Adaptive evolutionary CoT jailbreak (AE-CoT) effectively overcomes limitations of static template-based jailbreak methods, achieving superior attack performance through evolutionary diversity and adaptive mechanisms.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in reasoning and generation tasks and are increasingly deployed in real-world applications. However, their explicit chain-of-thought (CoT) mechanism introduces new security risks, making them particularly vulnerable to jailbreak attacks. Existing approaches often rely on static CoT templates to elicit harmful outputs, but such fixed designs suffer from limited diversity, adaptability, and effectiveness. To overcome these limitations, we propose an adaptive evolutionary CoT jailbreak framework, called AE-CoT. Specifically, the method first rewrites harmful goals into mild prompts with teacher role-play and decomposes them into semantically coherent reasoning fragments to construct a pool of CoT jailbreak candidates. Then, within a structured representation space, we perform multi-generation evolutionary search, where candidate diversity is expanded through fragment-level crossover and a mutation strategy with an adaptive mutation-rate control mechanism. An independent scoring model provides graded harmfulness evaluations, and high-scoring candidates are further enhanced with a harmful CoT template to induce more destructive generations. Extensive experiments across multiple models and datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed AE-CoT, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art jailbreak methods.

</details>


### [131] [Hypothesis Generation and Inductive Inference in Children and Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24528)
*Jeffrey Qin,Wasu Top Piriyakulki,Zhuangfei Gao,Mia Radovanovic,Jessica Sommerville,Kevin Ellis,Marta Kryven*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates computational principles underlying human and LLM-based agent inference under uncertainty using an inductive Box Task. It formalizes the task as program induction with Bayesian particle-based inference, offering two interpretations: constraint satisfaction over hypotheses and program synthesis. Children's behavior is best explained by subjective evidence reliability and online hypothesis generation. LLM-based agents replicate key patterns of children's behavior (discounting unreliable evidence, seeking partial information, dissociating task completion from generalization) but tend to over-observe and over-comply, revealing distinct underlying costs and inductive biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
