TL;DR: The paper introduces Trajectory Proper Score (TPS), a strictly proper scoring rule for evaluating uncertainty in language-model agent trajectories, proving it elicits the full prefix-conditioned success-probability process—unlike existing metrics (AUROC, AUPRC, Trajectory ECE, scalarized scores) which target weaker objects. The method handles censored trajectories via a reduced score, and experiments on four benchmarks confirm these theoretical differences are practically significant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic uncertainty quantification (UQ) evaluations conflate ranking usefulness (discrimination) with probabilistic truthfulness (calibration), and metrics like AUROC, AUPRC, Trajectory ECE, and scalarized scores evaluate only collapsed summaries or resolution-blind calibration, not the full prefix-conditioned success-probability trace $q_t = P^π(Y=1 | H_t)$ that agents emit.

Method: Building on prequential proper scoring, the authors propose the Trajectory Proper Score (TPS), a predictor-agnostic family of strictly proper trajectory-level scoring rules. TPS strictly elicits the success-probability process under complete observation. For censored trajectories, the complete-data score is projected onto the observable stopped prefix, yielding an exact $q_Z$-weighted reduced score and a tractable approximation when $q_Z$ is unestimated.

Result: Experiments on StrategyQA, Tau2-Bench, HotpotQA, and WebShop show: (1) probability recalibration substantially changes TPS while leaving rank metrics nearly unchanged, and (2) the tractable censored approximation can change the verdict relative to complete-only evaluation.

Conclusion: TPS provides a theoretically grounded and practically distinguishable alternative to existing trajectory-level UQ metrics, rigorously targeting the full prefix-conditioned success-probability process rather than weaker summary objects.

Abstract: Language-model agents increasingly emit uncertainty signals throughout a trajectory, but existing agentic UQ evaluations often conflate ranking usefulness with probabilistic truthfulness. AUROC, AUPRC, risk-coverage, Trajectory ECE, and scalarized trajectory scores evaluate discrimination, binwise calibration, or collapsed summaries, but do not strictly elicit the full prefix-conditioned success-probability trace $q_t = P^π(Y=1 | H_t)$. Building on prequential proper scoring, we introduce the Trajectory Proper Score (TPS), a predictor-agnostic family of strictly proper trajectory-level scoring rules for any per-step uncertainty signal calibrated into a probability of eventual success. We prove that TPS strictly elicits the success-probability process under complete observation, within the chosen score family and weight schedule. We extend the construction to administratively censored trajectories by projecting the complete-data score onto the observable stopped prefix, yielding an exact $q_Z$-weighted reduced score and a tractable approximation when $q_Z$ is unestimated. We further show that common trajectory evaluators target weaker objects than the full prefix-conditioned probability process: Trajectory ECE is resolution-blind, while scalarized Trajectory Brier elicits only the collapsed scalar, not the full trace. Experiments on StrategyQA, Tau2-Bench, HotpotQA, and WebShop show that these theoretical distinctions are operationally visible: probability recalibration can substantially change TPS while leaving rank metrics nearly unchanged, and the tractable censored approximation can change the verdict relative to complete-only evaluation.

</details>


### [156] [PRIMA: Operational Patterns for Resilient Multi-Agent Research with Verifiable Identity and Convergent Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24775)
*Sasank Annapureddy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PRIMA introduces three operational patterns (resilience-and-recovery, sub-agent discipline, multi-phase engineering) atop a formal protocol to address failure modes that emerge in long-running multi-agent LLM research systems, validated via a Graph Isomorphism case study that produced a novel research paper.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-running multi-agent LLM research systems exhibit failure modes invisible to single-shot evaluation, including upstream throttling, task drift, narrating tools instead of using them, apologetic revisions, and misinterpreting context as executable directives.

Method: PRIMA provides (1) a resilience-and-recovery layer for rate-limit detection, typed pause records, and resume without re-execution; (2) a sub-agent operating discipline with structural prompts for task-fidelity, tool-use, revision, and context-boundary norms; (3) a multi-phase application pattern separating orthogonal draft and cross-document harmonization. Underlying protocol includes a research-program specification language, dual-metric scoring (LLM rubric + sandboxed code), meta-optimization loop, event-driven persistence, hook-based middleware, context compaction, and multi-provider abstraction. Agent identities use prime powers for collision-free IDs and verifiable cluster membership.

Result: Theoretical guarantees: O(k) verification, O(V+E) DAG validation, collision-free identity via Fundamental Theorem of Arithmetic. Case study on Graph Isomorphism produced a six-step protocol yielding a research paper with a new canonical-form algorithm, three theorems, and five conjectures.

Conclusion: PRIMA's operational patterns and formal protocol effectively address long-running multi-agent failure modes, demonstrated by producing meaningful research artifacts with formal guarantees on identity, verification, and validation.

Abstract: Operating LLMs as coordinated multi-agent research systems over multi-hour runs surfaces failure modes that single-shot evaluation cannot: upstream providers throttle without warning, sub-agents drift the task to fit accessible tools, narrate machinery instead of using it, open revision iterations with self-apology, or treat upstream context as executable directives. We present PRIMA, whose primary contributions are three operational patterns for surviving these failure modes: (1) a resilience-and-recovery layer that detects upstream rate-limit signals, persists a typed pause record to disk, and resumes long-running runs without re-executing converged work even across process restarts; (2) a sub-agent operating discipline encoding task-fidelity, tool-use, revision, and inter-step context-boundary norms as a structural prompt layer; (3) a multi-phase application pattern for structured engineering deliverables pairing orthogonal draft steps with an explicit cross-document harmonization pass before final synthesis. These sit atop a foundational protocol: a research-program specification language with explicit convergence criteria, a dual-metric scoring engine (LLM-judged rubric plus sandboxed code), an outer meta-optimization loop, event-driven persistence, hook-based middleware, context compaction, and a multi-provider LLM abstraction. Agent identities derive from prime powers, giving collision-free identifiers and trivially-verifiable cluster membership without a central registry. Theoretical guarantees include $O(k)$ verification, $O(V+E)$ DAG validation, and identity collision freedom by the Fundamental Theorem of Arithmetic. A Graph Isomorphism case study grounds the architectural claims in a generated artifact: a six-step protocol that produced a research paper proposing a new canonical-form algorithm with three theorems and five conjectures.

</details>


### [157] [GRAIL: AI translation for scientists application workflow on satellite data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24784)
*Zhuocheng Shang,Ahmed Eldawy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRAIL translates Python geospatial scripts into scalable Spark programs using an agentic LLM pipeline, without requiring scientists to learn distributed computing frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain scientists write Python scripts for satellite imagery analysis but these scripts cannot scale to large datasets; scientists lack the expertise to manually port code to distributed frameworks like Spark.

Method: GRAIL adapts RDPro (a Scala library) into LLM-ready form via structured documentation, API aliases, and repair-oriented error logs; translation uses a LangGraph pipeline that decomposes code generation into explicit sections with guided I/O, enabling targeted repair without full regeneration.

Result: GRAIL successfully translates real-world geospatial Python workflows into Spark programs with verified correctness and improved scalability.

Conclusion: An agentic translation system can bridge the gap between single-node Python geospatial analysis and distributed execution without requiring scientists to adopt new frameworks or relying on fine-tuned LLMs.

Abstract: Domain scientists increasingly develop Python scripts to analyze satellite imagery but they lack scalability to large-scale data. This paper demonstrates GRAIL, an agentic translation system that converts Python geospatial workflows into executable Spark-based programs without requiring scientists to learn a new framework. Rather than fine-tuning a specialized LLM model, GRAIL adapts RDPro, a Scala library for satellite data analysis, to make it LLM-ready using structured documentation, API alias functions, and repair-oriented error logs. Translation is structured as a LangGraph pipeline that decomposes code generation into explicit sections with guided inputs and outputs, enabling targeted repair without regenerating the full program. We demonstrate GRAIL on real-world geospatial workflows and showcase the correctness and scalability of the translated code.

</details>


### [158] [PANDO: Efficient Multimodal AI Agents via Online Skill Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24785)
*Yubo Li,Yidi Miao,Haotian Shen,Yuxin Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PANDO is a single-rollout online skill-distillation framework for multimodal web agents that improves efficiency by maintaining a structured Skill Library with progress reflection, confidence-based skill demotion, hierarchical routing, visual compression, and cache-aware prompting, achieving 58.3% success rate on VisualWebArena (outperforming SGV at 54.0% and WALT at 45.2%) while using 58-61% fewer tokens and introducing three trajectory-level efficiency metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal web agents improve performance by increasing inference-time computation (rollout search, verifier passes, skill discovery, model stacks), raising the question of whether agents can become more efficient with accumulated experience rather than more expensive. The authors identify three recurring inefficiencies in VisualWebArena trajectories: repeat-action loops, hidden discovery costs, and low prompt-cache reuse.

Method: PANDO is a single-rollout online skill-distillation framework that maintains a structured Skill Library. It combines progress reflection, confidence-based skill demotion (down-ranking poorly performing skills), hierarchical routing (selecting appropriate skills), visual compression, and cache-aware prompting. The system distills skills online from a single rollout trajectory without requiring pre-evaluation discovery budgets.

Result: On 910 VisualWebArena tasks, PANDO achieves 58.3% success rate, outperforming SGV (54.0%) and WALT (45.2%), while using 58% fewer tokens than SGV and 61% fewer tokens than WALT, with no pre-evaluation discovery budget. A 300-task ablation shows rules/routines drive most success gains, while routing, compression, and cache-aware prompting convert larger skill libraries into lower marginal token cost.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that web agents can become more efficient with accumulated experience rather than more expensive, and introduces three trajectory-level efficiency metrics (Action Repetition Rate, Step Overhead Ratio, Prompt Cache Utilization) to evaluate efficiency beyond terminal success.

Abstract: Recent advances in multimodal web agents often rely on increased inference-time computation, including rollout search, verifier passes, offline skill discovery, and specialist model stacks. This raises a central question: can a web agent become more efficient as it accumulates experience, rather than more expensive? We first analyze trajectories from VisualWebArena and identify three recurring sources of inefficiency: repeat-action loops, hidden discovery costs, and low prompt-cache reuse. We then introduce PANDO, a single-rollout online skill-distillation framework that maintains a structured Skill Library and combines progress reflection, confidence-based skill demotion, hierarchical routing, visual compression, and cache-aware prompting. On the full set of 910 VisualWebArena tasks, PANDO achieves a 58.3% success rate, outperforming SGV (54.0%) and our WALT reproduction (45.2%), while using 58% fewer tokens than SGV and 61% fewer tokens than WALT, without any pre-evaluation discovery budget. A 300-task ablation further shows that rules and routines provide most of the success gains, while routing, compression, and cache-aware prompting convert the larger skill library into lower marginal token cost. Finally, we introduce three trajectory-level efficiency metrics -- Action Repetition Rate, Step Overhead Ratio, and Prompt Cache Utilization -- to make efficiency visible beyond terminal success.

</details>


### [159] [CoRe-Code: Collaborative Reinforcement Learning for Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24812)
*Zhihao Dou,Qinjian Zhao,Zhongwei Wan,Xiaoyu Xia,Sumon Biswas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoRe-Code is a multi-agent framework using a Planner-Coder paradigm with GRPO-based reinforcement learning to improve role specialization and coordination in LLM-based code generation, achieving superior accuracy and efficiency across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive code generation lacks global planning, leading to locally coherent but globally suboptimal solutions. Existing approaches like CoT and multi-agent systems have limited role specialization and coordination, hindering performance on complex tasks.

Method: Proposes CoRe-Code with a Planner-Coder paradigm (Planner produces high-level plans, Coder executes them) and a collaboration-aware reinforcement learning stage based on Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to enhance role specialization and inter-agent alignment.

Result: Outperforms existing RL-based and multi-agent methods across multiple benchmarks of varying difficulty using three base models, showing consistent improvements in accuracy, execution time, and memory usage.

Conclusion: CoRe-Code demonstrates that combining a simple Planner-Coder paradigm with collaboration-aware RL (GRPO) enhances both role specialization and coordination, yielding effective and efficient multi-agent code generation that generalizes to other agent frameworks.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance in code generation, but most methods rely on autoregressive decoding without global planning, often leading to locally coherent yet globally suboptimal solutions (e.g., failing test cases or inefficient complexity). While recent approaches such as Chain-of-Thought (CoT) and multi-agent systems (MAS) introduce planning, their limited role specialization and coordination hinder performance on complex tasks. To address the challenges of coordination and specialization in multi-agent code generation, we propose Collaborative Reinforcement Code (CoRe-Code), a framework for role specialized LLM agents that enhances inter-agent coordination to generate more accurate and efficient code. CoRe-Code adopts a simple Planner-Coder paradigm, where the Planner produces high-level plans and the Coder executes them to generate code. We further introduce a collaboration-aware reinforcement learning stage based on Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to enhance role specialization and alignment. Experiments show that CoRe-Code outperforms a wide range of existing RL-based and multi-agent methods. In addition, we demonstrate that CoRe-Code can generalize to other multi-agent frameworks (e.g., Retrieval and Debugging agents), highlighting its flexibility and scalability. We evaluate CoRe-Code on multiple benchmarks of varying difficulty using three base models. Compared to existing baselines, the results show consistent improvements in accuracy, while also achieving higher efficiency in terms of execution time and memory usage, demonstrating the effectiveness and practicality of CoRe-Code.

</details>


### [160] [Agent Manufacturing: Foundation-Model Agents as First-Class Industrial Entities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24823)
*Yilei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a fifth manufacturing paradigm, Agent Manufacturing, where foundation-model-based autonomous agents handle coordinative cognition (interpretation, planning, negotiation, governance) traditionally done by engineers and managers, shifting from physical/routine-cognitive automation to cognitive coordination work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors argue that previous manufacturing paradigms (mechanization, electrification, programmable automation, Smart Manufacturing) each shifted specific work types from humans to machines, but the coordinative cognitive layer (interpretive, allocative, diagnostic, negotiative, governance work) remained human. A fifth transition is now underway where this layer is being redistributed by foundation-model agents.

Method: The paper provides a conceptual definition and operational characterization of Agent Manufacturing, distinguishing it from existing concepts like cognitive manufacturing and Industry 5.0, and contrasting it with classical multi-agent manufacturing systems that operated only within closed protocol spaces.

Result: The paper defines Agent Manufacturing as a system whose principal coordination mechanism is reasoning by foundation-model agents capable of interpreting open-ended goals, long-horizon planning, tool/machine invocation, and negotiation with other agents and humans. This is a narrower, more falsifiable definition than prior literature.

Conclusion: A fifth manufacturing paradigm is emerging where foundation-model-based autonomous agents primarily redistribute coordinative cognitive work, not physical or routine-cognitive work. This shift is distinct from and more specific than prior paradigms and concepts.

Abstract: Manufacturing has passed through four widely recognized paradigms - mechanization, electrification, programmable automation, and Smart Manufacturing - each defined by the kind of work it shifted from humans to machines. In every case, one layer of industrial work remained fundamentally human: the coordinative cognition of production, comprising the interpretive, allocative, diagnostic, negotiative, and governance work exercised by engineers, planners, and operational managers. We argue that a fifth transition is now underway in which this layer, rather than the physical or routine-cognitive layers below it, is what foundation-model-based autonomous agents primarily redistribute. We name this paradigm Agent Manufacturing and define it operationally: a manufacturing system is an instance of Agent Manufacturing when its principal coordination mechanism is reasoning performed by foundation-model agents that can interpret open-ended goals, plan over long horizons, invoke tools and machines, and negotiate with other agents and humans. This is a narrower and more falsifiable definition than the existing literature on cognitive manufacturing or Industry 5.0 provides, and it distinguishes the paradigm sharply from classical multi-agent manufacturing systems, which were autonomous only within closed protocol spaces.

</details>


### [161] [Test-Time Deep Thinking to Explore Implicit Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24828)
*Wentong Chen,Xin Cong,Zhong Zhang,Yaxi Lu,Siyuan Zhao,Yesai Wu,Qinyu Luo,Haotian Chen,Yankai Lin,Zhiyuan Liu,Maosong Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TTExplore is a framework that uses a trained thinker model to infer implicit rules from interaction history, improving agent performance by 14-19 points on embodied tasks via a stable reinforcement learning pipeline that avoids evaluating intermediate reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents frequently fail in environments with implicit rules (hidden constraints that must be inferred through interaction), leading to repetitive trial-and-error loops and task failure.

Method: Proposes Test-Time Exploration (TTExplore) with a thinker-actor architecture where the thinker infers implicit rules from interaction history. Uses a novel stable RL pipeline: uses task-level scores as indirect rewards to bypass evaluating intermediate reasoning, and retains only one thinking node per trajectory to address reward sparsity. Trains Exp-Thinker (7B model) with this pipeline.

Result: On five text-based embodied tasks, TTExplore with Exp-Thinker improves baseline agent performance by an average of 14-19 points.

Conclusion: Explicitly reasoning about implicit rules during test time effectively enhances agent performance in environments governed by hidden constraints.

Abstract: With the continuous advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs), intelligent agents are becoming increasingly vital. However, these agents often fail in environments governed by implicit rules--hidden constraints that cannot be observed directly and must be inferred through interaction. This causes agents to fall into repetitive trial-and-error loops, ultimately leading to task failure. To address this challenge, we propose Test-Time Exploration (TTExplore), a framework where a thinker component analyzes interaction history to infer these implicit rules and guide an actor. Effective exploration in this setting critically depends on the reasoning ability of the thinker. However, evaluating deep reasoning trajectories is inherently unstable and difficult, which poses a major obstacle to effective training. To overcome this issue, we introduce a novel and stable reinforcement learning pipeline. The core idea is to use accurate task-level scores as indirect rewards to bypass the difficulty of evaluating intermediate reasoning, and to retain only a single thinking node per trajectory to alleviate reward sparsity. Using this pipeline, we train a specialized 7B model, Exp-Thinker. Experiments on five text-based embodied tasks show that TTExplore equipped with Exp-Thinker improves baseline agent performance by an average of $14$-$19$ points, demonstrating the effectiveness of explicitly reasoning about implicit rules.

</details>


### [162] [Geo-Expert: Towards Expert-Level Geological Reasoning via Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24844)
*Chenyou Guo,Zongqi Liu,Yizhou Zhang,Zhaorui Jiang,Ze Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Geo-Expert is a family of parameter-efficient geological LLMs fine-tuned with LoRA on a custom-curated instruction dataset, demonstrating that a domain-aligned 8B model outperforms 70B generalists and GPT-4o on geological reasoning, with a 32B variant approaching frontier reasoning models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose LLMs applied to Geology hallucinate on subsurface structures and deep-time evolution, while current AI in Earth sciences focuses on surface remote sensing and GIS, leaving a gap in specialized geological reasoning.

Method: Fine-tuned three base models (Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-32B, Gemma-3-27B) using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) on a custom-curated high-quality instruction dataset processed via a custom instruction synthesis pipeline; evaluated on a novel domain-specific benchmark (Geo-Eval).

Result: A domain-aligned 8B model outperforms open-weight 70B generalists and GPT-4o on specialized geological reasoning; a 32B variant approaches frontier reasoning models.

Conclusion: Provides a reproducible recipe for democratizing scientific LLMs and establishes a baseline for geological AI, with the optimized 8B model offering a competitive cost-performance ratio for deployment.

Abstract: While general-purpose Large Language Models (LLMs) applied to Geology often hallucinate when reasoning about subsurface structures and deep-time evolution, current AI in Earth sciences predominantly targets surface remote sensing and GIS. To bridge this gap, we introduce Geo-Expert, a family of parameter-efficient geological LLMs fine-tuned on a custom-curated, high-quality instruction dataset processed using our custom instruction synthesis pipeline. We investigate the impact of model scaling and architecture by fine-tuning three base models: Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-32B, and Gemma-3-27B, with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) method. Our extensive evaluation on a novel domain-specific benchmark, Geo-Eval, reveals that a domain-aligned 8B model can outperform open-weight 70B generalists and proprietary GPT-4o on specialized geological reasoning, while a 32B variant approaches frontier reasoning models. The optimized 8B model further offers a competitive cost-performance ratio for deployment. This work provides a reproducible recipe for democratizing scientific LLMs and establishes a baseline for geological artificial intelligence.

</details>


### [163] [Solving Combinatorial Counting Problems with Weighted First-Order Model Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24845)
*Yuanhong Wang,Juhua Pu,Yuxu Zhou,Yuyi Wang,Ondřej Kuželka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Cofola is a typed declarative language for combinatorial counting that uses first-order logic primitives (sets, bags, tuples, sequences, circles, partitions, compositions) with arithmetic constraints, compiled via a three-phase pipeline to weighted first-order model counting (WFOMC) while preserving symmetries for domain-liftable fragments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Combinatorial counting problems are pervasive in AI, statistics, and discrete mathematics, but existing solutions are either brittle (closed-form derivations) or destroy exchangeability (naive propositional encodings). A more systematic, uniform, and symmetry-preserving approach is needed.

Method: The authors design Cofola, a typed declarative language with built-in combinatorial primitives (sets, bags, tuples, sequences, circles, partitions, compositions) and relational/arithmetic constraints. They define a denotational semantics and a three-phase compilation pipeline: (1) preprocessing, (2) decomposition, and (3) symmetry-preserving encoding, which reduces problems to WFOMC with coefficient-extraction constraints.

Result: On a suite of representative combinatorial counting problems (textbook math problems to multi-object scenarios beyond prior frameworks), Cofola produces concise specifications and achieves practical end-to-end solving via a uniform pipeline.

Conclusion: Cofola demonstrates that a typed, first-order declarative language with symmetry-preserving compilation can make combinatorial counting systematic and practical, outperforming prior work in expressiveness and uniformity.

Abstract: Combinatorial counting problems pervade artificial intelligence, statistics, and discrete mathematics. Whether the task is enumerating subsets, multisets, permutations, partitions, or compositions under structural and arithmetic constraints, solving it remains a stubbornly manual exercise. Closed-form derivations are powerful but brittle, while naive encodings to propositional model counting or constraint satisfaction destroy the exchangeability that makes counting tractable in the first place. We present Cofola (COmbinatorial counting LAnguage with First-Order logic), a typed declarative language whose primitives are the combinatorial objects that recur in everyday counting questions, including sets, bags, tuples, sequences, circles, partitions, and compositions, together with natural relational and arithmetic constraints over them. A denotational semantics maps every Cofola program to a well-defined combinatorial counting problem, and a three-phase compilation pipeline (preprocessing, decomposition, and symmetry-preserving encoding) reduces this problem to a weighted first-order model counting (WFOMC) instance augmented with coefficient-extraction constraints. To stay inside known domain-liftable fragments whenever possible, the encoding groups indistinguishable entities, breaks the symmetry of unordered groupings lexicographically, and encodes sequences and circles via order axioms. On a suite of representative combinatorial counting problems, ranging from textbook math problems to multi-object scenarios that the closest prior framework cannot express, Cofola produces concise specifications and a uniform solving pipeline that is practical end-to-end.

</details>


### [164] [Clustering as Reasoning: A $k$-Means Interpretation of Chain-of-Thought Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24867)
*Xuanting Xie,Zhaochen Guo,Bingheng Li,Xingtong Yu,Zhifei Liao,Zhao Kang,Yuan Fang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KCoT unifies Chain-of-Thought reasoning with graph representation learning by formalizing a mathematical correspondence between Transformer blocks and k-means clustering, using iterative assignment and update steps for graph-structured reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph CoT methods use disjoint architectures and fixed graph representations, which limit step-by-step semantic-topological interaction and interpretability.

Method: Proposes KCoT, a unified framework that: (1) establishes a formal mathematical correspondence between Transformer blocks and k-means clustering; (2) introduces a Semantic Discriminating Prompt to formulate reasoning as iterative assignment and update steps; (3) uses a structure-grounded alignment strategy to fuse topological priors with thought-conditioned representations.

Result: Consistent improvements over state-of-the-art methods on standard benchmarks, validating clustering as a principled mechanism for CoT-based graph learning.

Conclusion: Clustering can serve as a principled mechanism for interpreting and enhancing CoT reasoning on graph-structured data, and the proposed KCoT framework effectively bridges semantic and topological interactions.

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting has shown promise in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) on text-attributed graphs (TAGs). This work reframes CoT-based graph learning through the principle of clustering as reasoning, offering a $k$-means interpretation of how iterative reasoning operates over graph-structured data. We observe that existing graph CoT methods rely on disjoint architectures and fixed graph representations, limiting step-by-step semantic-topological interaction and interpretability. To overcome this limitation, we propose a unified framework named KCoT that integrates CoT reasoning with graph representation learning. Our key theoretical result reveals a formal mathematical correspondence between a Transformer block and the $k$-means algorithm, allowing reasoning to be interpreted as iterative assignment and update steps. Based on this insight, we introduce a Semantic Discriminating Prompt that explicitly formulates these steps as structured CoT reasoning, together with a structure-grounded alignment strategy to fuse topological priors with evolving thought-conditioned representations. Experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over state-of-the-art methods, validating clustering as a principled mechanism for CoT-based graph learning.

</details>


### [165] [Inverting the Shield: Systematically Generating Safety Tests from Policy Specifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24883)
*Xiaoyue Lu,Xianglin Yang,Haijun Liu,Jiahao Liu,Kuntai Cai,Yan Xiao,Jin Song Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: POLARIS is a framework that converts natural-language safety policies into First-Order Logic representations and Semantic Policy Graphs, enabling coverage-driven, reproducible safety testing of LLMs through systematic exploration of compositional violation patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM safety evaluation methods (benchmarks, red-teaming) depend heavily on expert knowledge, offer limited systematic guarantees, and are vulnerable to rapid obsolescence.

Method: POLARIS compiles unstructured policies into FOL representations, constructs a Semantic Policy Graph encoding violation scenarios as traversable paths, and systematically explores this graph to instantiate executable test queries.

Result: POLARIS achieves higher policy coverage and attack success counts compared to established baselines.

Conclusion: Bridging formal methods and AI safety provides a principled, automated, and verifiably traceable approach to ensuring LLMs adhere to safety-critical policies.

Abstract: The widespread integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) necessitates rigorous and systematic safety evaluation. Existing paradigms either rely on constructed benchmarks to assess safety from predefined perspectives, or employ dynamic red-teaming to probe potential vulnerabilities. While effective, these approaches face challenges, as they depend heavily on expert domain knowledge, offer limited systematic guarantees, and are vulnerable to rapid obsolescence. To address these limitations, we introduce a novel framework POLARIS that brings the rigor of specification-based software testing to AI safety. POLARIS first compiles unstructured natural-language policies into First-Order Logic (FOL) representations, establishing a traceable link between high-level rules and concrete test cases. This formalization enables the construction of a Semantic Policy Graph, where complex policy violation scenarios are encoded as traversable paths. By systematically exploring this graph, POLARIS uncovers compositional violation patterns, which are then instantiated into executable natural-language test queries, enabling coverage-driven and reproducible safety testing. Experiments demonstrate that POLARIS achieves higher policy coverage and attack success counts compared to established baselines. Crucially, by bridging formal methods and AI safety, POLARIS provides a principled, automated approach to ensuring LLMs adhere to safety-critical policies with verifiable traceability. We release our code at https://github.com/huac-lxy/POLARIS.

</details>


### [166] [TaBIIC2: Interactive Building of Ontological Taxonomies using Weighted Self-Organizing Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24899)
*Mathieu d'Aquin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an interactive tool for progressively building ontological taxonomies from tabular data using weighted self-organizing maps for clustering, striking a middle ground between fully manual analysis and fully automatic methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building ontological taxonomies of concepts and subconcepts from tabular data is complex and challenging. Manual analysis is difficult, while purely automatic approaches (e.g., agglomerative clustering, LLM-based analysis) produce overwhelming results with little user control.

Method: The tool employs weighted self-organizing maps (SOMs) as a clustering method to create an arbitrary number of distinct clusters based on value distributions of entity characteristics. It integrates mechanisms for rapidly creating concepts that group instances from tabular data, enabling progressive and interactive taxonomy construction with intentional definitions of clusters.

Result: The tool enables progressive and interactive construction of taxonomic hierarchies with identifiable intentional definitions of clusters, offering a middle ground between purely manual and fully automatic approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed tool balances user control and automation, making ontology taxonomy construction from tabular data more accessible and manageable than either fully manual or fully automatic methods.

Abstract: Ontologies represent the conceptual knowledge of a domain. At the core of an ontology is the taxonomy of concepts and subconcepts that represent specific entities, which can be complex to build. In many cases, information is available in the form of records describing the characteristics of relevant entities, i.e., tabular data. Identifying patterns and similarities in such data can serve as a basis for identifying concepts and organizing them. However, doing so manually can be challenging, and purely automatic approaches, such as agglomerative clustering or relying on a large language model to analyze the data, can leave the user with overwhelming results and little control. In this paper, we describe a tool that enables the progressive and interactive construction of a taxonomy of concepts by identifying clusters as well as their intentional definitions. To do so, we rely on weighted self-organizing maps as a clustering method because they enable the creation of an arbitrary number of clusters that are distinct with respect to the distributions of values of specific characteristics of the clustered entities. We show that, by integrating this mechanism and others for rapidly creating concepts that group together instances from tabular data, this tool represents a middle ground between purely manual analysis and automatic methods for building ontological taxonomies.

</details>


### [167] [ProActor: Timing-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Proactive Task Scheduling Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24900)
*Lei Ding,Bin He,Chenguang Wang,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProActor is a unified framework for proactive conversational task scheduling that uses domain-agnostic automated annotation for scalable RL, systematic proactiveness metrics (timing quality + action alignment), and GRPO-based optimization with RULER and stage-aware composite rewards. It also introduces ART-F infrastructure for efficient training (4-8x speedups on LoRA fine-tuning of 4-bit Qwen2.5-14B). Experiments show improved proactive timing while maintaining action consistency comparable to SOTA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing task-oriented agents are reactive (await explicit instructions) and lack generalizable end-to-end solutions for measuring and optimizing anticipatory, proactive behaviors.

Method: ProActor framework with: (1) domain-agnostic auto-annotation generating full opportunity time windows for RL, (2) metrics for timing quality and reference action alignment, (3) GRPO-based RL with RULER-based rewards and stage-aware composite rewards, and (4) ART-F infrastructure (request-adaptive inference clusters + DDP training on single-node multi-GPU) for efficient LoRA training.

Result: Significant improvements in proactive timing on two newly auto-annotated datasets, with action consistency comparable to SOTA baselines. Ablations validate distinct composite reward variations. Training achieves 4-8x speedups.

Conclusion: RULER-based rewards with proactiveness rubrics improve timing quality; stage-aware composite rewards balance timing quality and action alignment. The proposed infrastructure enables feasible timing-aware RL exploration.

Abstract: Proactive task-oriented agents must autonomously anticipate user needs, identify actionable opportunities, and trigger software actions at appropriate moments - fundamentally shifting from reactive systems that await explicit instructions. However, existing approaches lack generalizable end-to-end solutions for measuring and optimizing such anticipatory behaviors.
  This paper introduces ProActor, a unified framework for conversational task scheduling that integrates: (1) a domain-agnostic automated annotation methodology that enables scalable proactiveness reinforcement learning (RL) by generating full opportunity time windows instead of rigid point labels, (2) systematic proactiveness metrics capturing both timing quality and reference action alignment, and (3) RL optimization using GRPO with various reward designs. Our insight is that RULER-based rewards with proactiveness rubrics are crucial for improving timing quality, and that proactiveness optimization enabled by stage-aware composite rewards is key to balancing timing quality and reference action alignment.
  Timing-aware RL requires extensive exploration, demanding efficient infrastructure. We develop ART-F, an adaptive framework combining request-adaptive inference clusters with DDP-based training on single-node multi-GPU systems, enabling LoRA training of 4-bit Qwen2.5-14B-ProActor-Q4 with 4-8x speedups. Experiments on two newly auto-annotated datasets demonstrate significant improvements in proactive timing while maintaining action consistency comparable to state-of-the-art (SOTA) baselines. Ablations validate the effectiveness of distinct composite reward variations.

</details>


### [168] [Noise-Robust Financial Numerical Entity Attribute Tagging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24910)
*Hsin-Min Lu,Chen-Yang Lai,Yi-Jhen Li,Ju-Chun Yen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NORA is a noise-robust multi-attribute tagging model for financial numerical entities that uses instance-specific weighting and NPK filtering to handle label noise in XBRL data, achieving strong results on a new 6.6M-instance benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing FNE studies suffer from noisy labels due to manual XBRL filings and overlook important attributes beyond concept name (e.g., reporting-time relation, measurement scale, accounting sign).

Method: Proposes NORA with task-aware instance-specific weighting to reduce noisy label influence, and a Neighborhood Prior-adjusted KNN (NPK) filtering method for reliable evaluation on noisy test sets; constructs a large-scale multi-attribute benchmark with 6.6M instances.

Result: NORA outperforms state-of-the-art noisy-label baselines (Co-teaching, Mixup, SSR, SelfMix) in Accuracy, Macro F1, and Weighted F1 for concept name and time-relation prediction, and remains competitive on scale and sign prediction under both unfiltered and noise-filtered test settings.

Conclusion: Jointly modeling rich FNE attributes while accounting for label noise is valuable and effective for real-world financial filings.

Abstract: Financial Numerical Entity (FNE) understanding aims to recover the meaning of numerical mentions in financial reports. Existing studies primarily focus on concept name prediction and face two important limitations. First, labels derived from inline XBRL may contain errors because filings are usually prepared manually. Second, other important FNE attributes, such as reporting-time relation, measurement scale, and accounting sign, are less emphasized. We propose \textbf{NO}ise-\textbf{R}obust Tagging for Rich Financial Numerical Entity \textbf{A}ttributes (\textsc{NORA}) to address these gaps. NORA uses task-aware instance-specific weighting to attenuate the influence of noisy labels during training, and we further propose the Neighborhood Prior-adjusted KNN (NPK) filtering method for more reliable evaluation on real-world noisy test sets. In addition, we construct a large-scale benchmark containing 6.6 million instances with multi-attribute labels and filing metadata. Experiments show that \textsc{NORA} performs strongly compared with state-of-the-art noisy-label baselines, including Co-teaching, Mixup, SSR, and SelfMix. Moreover, NORA is robust under both unfiltered and noise-filtered test settings. It achieves the best Accuracy, Macro F1, and Weighted F1 for concept name and time-relation prediction, while remaining competitive on scale and sign prediction. These results demonstrate the value of jointly modeling rich FNE attributes while accounting for label noise in real-world financial filings.

</details>


### [169] [Energy Shields for Fairness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24926)
*Filip Cano,Thomas A. Henzinger,Konstantin Kueffner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces energy shields, a novel probabilistic fairness enforcement mechanism that uses physics-inspired energy functions to smoothly nudge decision sequences toward fairness, providing both short-term safety guarantees (running fairness stays within target interval) and long-term liveness guarantees (limit fairness converges to desired interval).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional fairness shields enforce runtime fairness deterministically and abruptly, ignoring the dynamic nature of fairness over sequences of decisions. There is a need for a smoother, adaptive approach that accounts for past decisions and provides both short-term and long-term guarantees.

Method: Energy shields are lightweight adaptive controllers that monitor decision sequences and intervene probabilistically using physics-inspired energy functions. The nudging force increases with unfairness. A synthesis procedure constructs the least intrusive shield for a given target specification.

Result: Energy shields are the first fairness shields to provide both short-term safety (running fairness stays within target interval with high probability) and long-term liveness (limit fairness lies within limit target interval). Experimental results demonstrate efficiency compared to existing fairness shields.

Conclusion: Energy shields offer a smoother, probabilistic alternative to deterministic fairness enforcement, balancing short-term constraints with long-term fairness goals while minimizing intrusiveness through a synthesis procedure.

Abstract: Runtime fairness is not a one-time constraint but a dynamic property evaluated over a sequence of decisions.
  To ensure fairness at runtime, it is necessary to account for past decisions, information neglected by conventional, static classifiers.
  Traditional fairness shields enforce runtime fairness abruptly,
  by intervening \emph{deterministically} whenever a sequence of decisions violates the target for a running fairness measure. This motivates our \emph{main conceptual contribution: \textbf{energy shields}.}
  An energy shield is a novel, lightweight, adaptive controller that monitors a sequence of decisions and intervenes \emph{probabilistically} to ensure runtime fairness smoothly, by utilizing physics-inspired energy functions to nudge the sequence toward fairness:
  the more unfair the decisions, the stronger the nudging force becomes. This makes energy shields the \emph{\textbf{first}} fairness shields to provide both \emph{short-term safety and long-term liveness guarantees}.
  Safety ensures that the running fairness measure stays within a running target interval with high probability,
  and liveness ensures that the limit of the fairness measure lies within the limit target interval.
  Intuitively, the short-term specifies the tolerated fairness values and the long-term specifies the desired fairness values.
  We also provide a synthesis procedure for constructing the least intrusive energy shield for a given target specification, and demonstrate its efficiency experimentally.
  We evaluate our energy shields against existing fairness shields through the lens of short- and long-term fairness.

</details>


### [170] [Towards Multi-Turn Dialog Systems for Industrial Asset Operations and Maintenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24953)
*Chengrui Li,Rujing Li,Yitong Bai,Rui Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A supervisor-specialist multi-agent architecture for industrial multi-turn QA that uses structured artifact reuse, dynamic replanning, and parallel tool execution, achieving 54.5% better planning effectiveness, 37.8% better task completion, and 4.2x faster subsequent turns via reduced tool invocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional plan-execute single-agent architectures struggle with multi-turn industrial QA due to limited cross-turn context maintenance and inability to reuse intermediate results, leading to redundant tool invocations and inefficiency.

Method: A supervisor-specialist multi-agent architecture incorporating: (1) structured artifact reuse across turns, (2) dynamic replanning, and (3) parallel tool execution to alleviate tool invocation bottlenecks.

Result: Planning effectiveness improved by 54.5%, task completion by 37.8%; tool-time share reduced from 47.3% to 26.3%; turns 2-5 approximately 4.2x faster than the first turn.

Conclusion: The proposed multi-agent architecture with artifact reuse and parallel execution significantly outperforms baseline single-agent approaches in industrial multi-turn dialog systems.

Abstract: Industrial asset operations and maintenance question answering is inherently multi-turn, iterative, and highly dependent on external tool invocation. However, the conventional plan-execute single-agent architecture exhibits clear limitations in maintaining cross-turn context, and reusing intermediate results. In this paper, we present a multi-turn dialog system designed for industrial scenarios based on a supervisor-specialist multi-agent architecture. To alleviate tool invocation bottlenecks, the system incorporates structured artifact reuse, dynamic replanning, and parallel tool execution. Evaluation results show that our system achieves better response quality compared with the baseline, with planning effectiveness increasing by 54.5% and task completion improving by 37.8%. System profiling further shows that cross-turn artifact reuse effectively reduces redundant tool invocation, decreasing the tool-time share from 47.3% to 26.3% and making turns 2-5 approximately 4.2x faster than the first turn.

</details>


### [171] [Mitigating Object Hallucinations in Vision-Language Models through Region-Aware Attention Recalibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24957)
*Yuanzhi Xu,Qian Gao,Jun Fan,Guohui Ding,Zhenyu Yang,Sixue Lin,Yuteng Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A training-free, region-aware adaptive weighting mechanism that dynamically corrects object hallucination in LVLMs by computing outlier-resistant statistical midpoints across attention heads and using inter-head disagreement to modulate hallucination-inducing attention paths via continuous penalty modulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches to mitigate object hallucination in LVLMs (data-driven fine-tuning, contrastive decoding, attention head truncation) frequently compromise either computational efficiency or feature space continuity, motivating a training-free, non-heuristic alternative.

Method: A training-free inference strategy using: (1) outlier-resistant statistical midpoint across attention heads as a stable visual anchor, (2) inter-head disagreement mapped across regions to dynamically determine intervention budgets, (3) continuous penalty modulation to suppress hallucination-inducing attention paths while preserving generative fluency.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on CHAIR, POPE, and MME benchmarks, substantially curtailing both instance- and sentence-level hallucinations against contemporary baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed region-aware adaptive weighting mechanism effectively rectifies visual-semantic misalignments without training or heuristic truncations, achieving robust and efficient hallucination mitigation while preserving generative fluency.

Abstract: The generation of factually incorrect objects, commonly known as object hallucination, remains a persistent challenge in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs). Current approaches to address this issue - ranging from expensive data-driven fine-tuning and high-latency contrastive decoding to rigid attention head truncation - frequently compromise either computational efficiency or the continuity of the model's feature space. To overcome these limitations, we introduce a novel, training-free inference strategy that operates as a region-aware adaptive weighting mechanism to dynamically correct semantic drift without relying on abrupt heuristic truncations. By computing an outlier-resistant statistical midpoint across various attention heads, we establish a stable anchor for reliable visual representations. We then utilize the inter-head disagreement mapped across regions to dynamically determine intervention budgets, gently suppressing hallucination-inducing attention paths through a continuous penalty modulation. This recalibration process effectively rectifies visual-semantic misalignments while fully preserving generative fluency and language priors. Comprehensive evaluations on standard multimodal benchmarks, including CHAIR, POPE, and MME, reveal that our strategy substantially curtails both instance- and sentence-level hallucinations. The results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance against contemporary baselines, confirming our method's efficiency and algorithmic robustness. Our code will be public.

</details>


### [172] [NeurIPS: Neuro-anatomical Inductive Priors for Sphere-based Brain Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24993)
*Sijin Yu,Zijiao Chen,Zhenyu Yang,Zihao Tan,Jiakun Xu,Zhongliang Liu,Shengxian Chen,Wenxuan Wu,Xiangmin Xu,Xin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NeurIPS introduces a surface-based fMRI decoder that uses a Selective ROI Spherical Tokenizer and a Structure-Guided Mixture of Experts to leverage anatomical variation as a predictive prior, achieving SOTA performance for surface decoders on NSD with dramatically faster convergence (10 vs. 600 epochs) and robust few-shot adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current fMRI decoders suffer from a performance-fidelity trade-off where efficient 1D ID encoders outperform geometrically faithful surface-based models, partly due to inefficient surface tokenization and the failure to use anatomy as a predictive signal.

Method: NeurIPS combines two innovations: (1) a Selective ROI Spherical Tokenizer (SRST) for efficient geometric encoding, and (2) a Structure-Guided Mixture of Experts (SG-MoE) that explicitly models individual anatomy using cortical features, reframing anatomical variation from a nuisance to a powerful inductive prior.

Result: On the Natural Scenes Dataset, NeurIPS establishes SOTA for surface decoders, achieves performance comparable to strong 1D baselines, converges dramatically faster (10 vs. 600 epochs), enables rapid adaptation with only 20% of data, and scales robustly with cohort size.

Conclusion: By leveraging anatomical priors via cortical features (not memorizing subject IDs), NeurIPS provides a principled and scalable path toward robust, generalizable brain decoding, causally validated by ablations.

Abstract: Current fMRI decoders face a performance-fidelity trade-off where efficient ID encoders outperform geometrically faithful surface-based models. We argue this is partly driven by inefficient surface tokenization and the failure to use anatomy as a predictive signal. We present NeurIPS, a framework that improves surface-based decoding by reframing anatomical variation from a nuisance to a powerful inductive prior. NeurIPS unites two innovations: a Selective ROI Spherical Tokenizer (SRST) for efficient geometric encoding, and a Structure-Guided Mixture of Experts (SG-MoE) that explicitly models individual anatomy using cortical features. On the Natural Scenes Dataset, NeurIPS establishes a new state-of-the-art for surface decoders and achieves performance comparable to strong 1D baselines. This is achieved with unprecedented efficiency, as the model converges dramatically faster (10 vs. 600 epochs). This efficiency enables rapid adaptation to new subjects using only 20% of data and ensures robust scalability as the training cohort is expanded. Ablations provide causal evidence that these gains are driven by the model's use of cortical features, not by memorizing subject IDs. By leveraging anatomical priors, NeurIPS provides a principled and scalable path toward robust, generalizable brain decoding.

</details>


### [173] [Privacy-Preserving Local Language Models for Longitudinal Data Retrieval in Chronic Dermatologic Disease: Implementation in Pemphigus Patients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25020)
*Abdurrahim Yilmaz,Ayşe Esra Koku Aksu,Duygu Yamen,Vefa Asli Erdemir,Mehmet Salih Gurel,Gulsum Gencoglan,Joram M. Posma,Burak Temelkuran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A locally deployed privacy-preserving small language model (SLM, Qwen3 4B) achieved 82.25% accuracy in retrieving 56 clinical features and generated high-quality longitudinal summaries from 541 visit notes of 30 pemphigus patients, with dermatologists preferring AI summaries in 53.3% of evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chronic dermatologic diseases like pemphigus generate extensive longitudinal clinical documentation that is difficult to review comprehensively during routine visits, increasing clinician workload and the risk of missing critical historical information.

Method: Retrospective case series of 30 pemphigus patients (541 visit notes, 89,336 words). A locally deployed SLM (Qwen3 4B Thinking 2507) was queried to retrieve 56 clinically relevant features annotated by two expert dermatologists and generate one final summary report per patient. Feature retrieval accuracy was evaluated across 1,680 tasks, and summary quality was rated by dermatologists.

Result: Mean accuracy of 82.25% across 1,680 feature retrieval tasks. Dermatologists rated AI summaries highly: overall quality (8.23-8.47), clinical accuracy (7.93-8.20), and usefulness (8.47-8.50). No significant inter-evaluator differences. AI summaries preferred in 53.3% of evaluations.

Conclusion: Privacy-preserving, locally deployed SLMs can outperform medical experts and reliably generate clinically meaningful longitudinal summaries, potentially supporting clinical decision-making with appropriate oversight.

Abstract: Chronic dermatologic diseases such as pemphigus require long-term follow-up, generating extensive longitudinal clinical documentation that is difficult to review comprehensively during routine visits and increasing clinician workload as well as the risk of missing critical historical information. We evaluated whether a locally deployed, privacy-preserving small language model (SLM) could retrieve structured clinical features and generate longitudinal summaries from long-term dermatology follow-up records. In this retrospective case series, thirty pemphigus patients contributed 541 visit notes that were aggregated into full longitudinal records (89,336 words); 56 clinically relevant features were annotated by two expert dermatologists. The locally deployed SLM (Qwen3 4B Thinking 2507) was queried with each complete record to retrieve 56 features and generate one final report summaries. Across 1,680 feature retrieval tasks, mean accuracy was 82.25%. Dermatologists' ratings of AI-generated summaries were high for overall quality (8.23-8.47), clinical accuracy (7.93-8.20), and usefulness (8.47-8.50), with no significant inter-evaluator differences and an overall preference for AI summaries in 53.3% of evaluations. These findings suggest that privacy-preserving, locally deployed SLMs can outperform medical experts and reliably generate clinically meaningful longitudinal summaries. SLMs may support clinical decision-making when integrated with appropriate oversight.

</details>


### [174] [AION: Next-Generation Tasks and Practical Harness for Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25045)
*Tianxiang Zhan,Xiaobao Song,Tong Guan,Shirui Pan,Ming Jin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AION, a time series harness that moves beyond fixed forecasting benchmarks by formalizing tasks as (task file, workspace, validation interface) tuples and incorporating temporal grounding, knowledge-grounded reasoning, and reliability mechanisms, demonstrated through a Kaggle Store Sales case study to produce more detailed process traces and review steps compared to standard agent-based approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series research needs to move beyond clean, fixed forecasting benchmarks toward realistic tasks that combine prediction, reasoning, tool use, and decision support, as existing benchmarks miss temporal constraints, evidence checks, and review processes.

Method: The paper formalizes next-generation time series tasks as three-component tuples (task file, workspace, validation interface) and presents AION, a harness built from six component groups: agents, skills, rules, memory, evaluation, and protocols, guided by three design principles: temporal grounding, temporal knowledge-grounded reasoning, and reliability mechanisms (post-experiment analysis and layered review).

Result: A Kaggle Store Sales case study demonstrates that AION produces more detailed process traces, more artifacts, and more review steps compared to the same base agent operating in OpenCode direct build mode.

Conclusion: The results argue for a paradigm shift from fixed, clean tasks to realistic ones under real-world constraints in time series research.

Abstract: Time series research is moving beyond fixed forecasting benchmarks toward realistic tasks that combine prediction, contextual reasoning, tool use, and structured decision support. Most benchmarks are built around clean data and short evaluation loops; agents alone may miss temporal constraints, evidence checks, or review before finalizing outputs. We first formalize next-generation time series tasks as three-component tuples consisting of a task file, a workspace, and a validation interface. We then present AION, a time series harness built from six component groups: agents, skills, rules, memory, evaluation, and protocols. In this harness, we use three design principles: temporal grounding, temporal knowledge-grounded reasoning, and reliability mechanisms such as post-experiment analysis and layered review. A Kaggle Store Sales case study shows that the harness produces more detailed process traces, more artifacts, and more review steps than the same base agent operating in OpenCode direct build mode. Taken together, these results argue for a paradigm shift from fixed tasks to realistic ones under real-world constraints.

</details>


### [175] [Evolutionary Enhanced Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Cooperative Air Combat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25091)
*Chengwei Li,Junlin Liu,Yang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ACE-MAPPO integrates evolutionary algorithms with MAPPO to improve exploration efficiency, sample utilization, and policy generalization for multi-aircraft cooperative air combat, outperforming baseline methods in training stability, convergence speed, and win rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MARL methods suffer from insufficient exploration efficiency, low sample utilization, and poor policy generalization in BVR multi-aircraft cooperative engagements due to high-dimensional state spaces, discrete action commands, and strongly adversarial dynamic environments.

Method: ACE-MAPPO hybrid framework integrating evolutionary algorithms with MAPPO, featuring (1) genetic soft update mechanism for population diversity, (2) evolutionary-augmented prioritized trajectory replay for sparse high-value sample utilization, and (3) adversarial evolutionary curriculum learning for adaptive difficulty progression.

Result: ACE-MAPPO outperforms MAPPO and other baselines in training stability, convergence speed, and win rate in multi-aircraft cooperative air combat scenarios.

Conclusion: The proposed ACE-MAPPO framework effectively addresses MARL limitations in BVR cooperative air combat decision-making, validating its superiority over existing methods.

Abstract: As modern air combat evolves toward beyond-visual-range (BVR) multi-aircraft cooperative engagements, autonomous decision-making for unmanned combat aerial vehicles (UCAVs) faces significant challenges due to high-dimensional state spaces, discrete action commands, and strongly adversarial dynamic environments. To overcome the limitations of existing multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) methods in such settings, namely insufficient exploration efficiency, low sample utilization, and poor policy generalization, we propose Adversarial Curriculum and Evolutionary-enhanced Multi-agent Proximal Policy Optimization (ACE-MAPPO), a hybrid learning framework that integrates evolutionary algorithms with MAPPO. Specifically, a genetic soft update mechanism is introduced to enhance population diversity and mitigate convergence to local optima. An evolutionary-augmented prioritized trajectory replay strategy is further employed to improve the utilization of sparse high-value samples. In addition, an adversarial evolutionary curriculum learning mechanism is designed to enable adaptive training with progressively increasing difficulty. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms MAPPO and other baseline algorithms in terms of training stability, convergence speed, and win rate, validating its effectiveness in multi-aircraft cooperative air combat scenarios.

</details>


### [176] [RECTOR: Priority-Aware Rule-Based Reranking for Compliance-Aware Autonomous Driving Trajectory Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25095)
*Hadi Hajieghrary,Benedikt Walter,Chaitanya Shinde,Paul Schmitt,Miguel Hurtado*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RECTOR is a post-generation reranking layer for autonomous driving trajectory selection that uses a tiered rulebook (Safety≻Legal≻Road≻Comfort) with differentiable proxies and scene-conditioned applicability, combined with a deterministic ε-lexicographic selection rule. It reduces Safety+Legal violations from 28.58% to 20.42% on Waymo Open Motion Dataset without retraining the predictor, and maintains robustness under adversarial confidence corruption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous driving stacks select one trajectory from multi-modal candidates; picking by model confidence alone ignores safety, traffic-law, and comfort constraints, leading to violations that could be avoided with rule-aware selection.

Method: RECTOR scores candidates against a tiered rulebook (Safety≻Legal≻Road≻Comfort) using differentiable proxies and scene-conditioned applicability, then selects via a deterministic ε-lexicographic rule that preserves cross-tier priority without retraining the predictor.

Result: On Waymo Open Motion Dataset validation_interactive (43,219 instances, K=6), rule-aware selection reduces Safety+Legal violations from 28.58% to 20.42% and Total from 40.32% to 32.41% vs confidence-only. Under adversarial confidence corruption, confidence-only fails 100% while rule-aware selectors reject injected mode ~96%.

Conclusion: Rule-aware ranking provides empirical improvement, while the lexicographic guarantee is a structural differentiator that no weight calibration can replicate, offering robustness without retraining.

Abstract: Autonomous driving stacks must pick one trajectory from a multi-modal candidate set; choosing by model confidence ignores safety, traffic-law, and comfort constraints. We present \textsc{RECTOR} (Rule-Enforced Constrained Trajectory Orchestrator), a post-generation reranking layer that scores candidates against a tiered rulebook (Safety~$\succ$~Legal~$\succ$~Road~$\succ$~Comfort) via differentiable proxies and a scene-conditioned applicability mechanism, then selects with a deterministic $\varepsilon$-lexicographic rule that preserves cross-tier priority by construction -- without retraining the predictor.
  On the Waymo Open Motion Dataset \texttt{validation\_interactive} split (43{,}219 augmented instances, $K{=}6$), under Protocol~B (28-rule proxy catalog, oracle applicability) rule-aware selection cuts Safety+Legal violations from 28.58\% to 20.42\% and Total from 40.32\% to 32.41\% versus confidence-only on the same candidates. A uniform-weight weighted-sum baseline matches binary compliance on this benchmark -- the empirical lift comes from rule-aware ranking, while the lexicographic guarantee is the structural differentiator no weight calibration can replicate. Under adversarial confidence corruption, confidence-only selection fails in 100\% of scenarios while both rule-aware selectors reject the injected mode in $\sim$96\%. All figures are proxy-evaluator results (not a safety certificate), open-loop, 5\,s horizon, U.S.\ rules, validation split.

</details>


### [177] [Trust but Verify: Prover-Verifier Deliberation for Selective LLM Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25133)
*João Sedoc,Baotong Zhang,Dean Foster*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prover-Verifier Deliberation (PVD) is an inference-time protocol for selective prediction where a prover defends answers with checkable sub-claims and a verifier issues Accept/Challenge/Reject verdicts. On GPQA Diamond, the Accept+No Change (ANC) subset yields ~30pp precision gap over the complement. The signal transfers across model families but can collapse when the verifier lacks domain competence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowing when a language model is correct is almost as important as being correct. The paper seeks a mechanism for selective prediction—producing answers with structured confidence verdicts so the system can report high-confidence answers while abstaining on uncertain cases.

Method: PVD is an inference-time protocol grounded in interactive proof theory. A prover defends a candidate answer through checkable sub-claims; a verifier issues targeted challenges and returns Accept, Challenge, or Reject. The protocol is characterized empirically via coverage-precision behavior. The Accept+No Change (ANC) subset is taken as the high-confidence output.

Result: On GPQA Diamond (Claude Sonnet 4.6 prover, Claude Haiku 4.5 verifier), ANC yields ~30pp HC-Prec gap over the non-ANC complement. Robustness experiments show high HC-Prec transfers across GPT and Gemini model families. On Humanity's Last Exam, weak pairings can collapse or invert the ANC signal. PVD supplies a distinct argument-defensibility signal compared to self-consistency, multi-agent debate, and Reflexion.

Conclusion: PVD provides a practical selective prediction mechanism with empirically reliable confidence signals. Verifier strictness and domain competence determine the selection gap size, and the approach offers a distinct signal type beyond existing methods.

Abstract: Reliably knowing when a language model is correct is almost as important as being correct. We introduce prover-verifier deliberation (PVD), an inference-time protocol grounded in interactive proof theory, as a mechanism for selective prediction: the protocol produces both an answer and a structured confidence verdict, allowing a system to report high-confidence answers while abstaining on uncertain cases. In each dialogue, a prover defends a candidate answer through checkable sub-claims while a verifier issues targeted challenges and returns \textsc{Accept}, \textsc{Challenge}, or \textsc{Reject}. Because frozen language models are imperfect provers and verifiers operating over a noisy channel, formal soundness and completeness guarantees do not transfer; instead, we characterize the protocol empirically through its coverage-precision behavior. Our main experiment uses Claude Sonnet 4.6 as prover and Claude Haiku 4.5 as verifier on GPQA Diamond. Questions accepted with no answer revision, which we call Accept + No Change (ANC), are reported as the high-confidence subset; we evaluate this subset by its precision and coverage. ANC separates reliable from unreliable answers, yielding a $\sim$30pp HC-Prec gap over the non-ANC complement. Robustness experiments with GPT and Gemini pairings show that high HC-Prec can transfer across model families, while verifier strictness and domain competence largely determine the size of the selection gap. On Humanity's Last Exam, weaker prover-verifier pairings can collapse or invert the ANC signal, illustrating a practical failure mode when the verifier operates outside its effective region. Comparisons with self-consistency, universal self-consistency, multi-agent debate, and Reflexion suggest that prover-verifier deliberation supplies a distinct argument-defensibility signal for selective prediction.

</details>


### [178] [Representation Without Control: Testing the Realization Effect in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25151)
*Ciarán Walsh,Emilio Barkett*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show behavioral sensitivity to the realization effect in prompts, but their risk-choice patterns diverge from human predictions. While Gemma's internal representations encode realization status (decodable at layer 18), steering this representation fails to causally influence downstream decisions, demonstrating that latent readout does not imply behavioral reliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLM behavioral outputs reflect human-like cognitive mechanisms or merely prompt-sensitive surface patterns, specifically through the lens of the realization effect from behavioral economics.

Method: Three-level analysis: (1) prompt-only behavioral sensitivity, (2) linear readout of internal representations from residual stream activations, and (3) causal control via activation steering along the decoded direction.

Result: Prompt-only results show systematic condition sensitivity but do not match human realization-effect patterns. Gemma's layer-18 residual stream contains a linearly decodable realization-status signal that generalizes. However, steering along this direction does not reliably shift risk choices, confirmed by positive-scale and negative sign-symmetry runs.

Conclusion: Behavioral sensitivity, latent readout, and causal control are distinct properties that do not automatically co-occur; successful latent readout alone is insufficient evidence that a model behaviorally relies on a representation during downstream decision-making.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used as behavioral simulators, but it remains unclear when their outputs reflect human-like cognitive mechanisms rather than prompt-sensitive surface patterns. We study this question through the realization effect, a well-characterized finding in behavioral economics in which risk-taking differs systematically after paper versus realized gains and losses. We evaluate LLM behavior at three levels: prompt-only behavioral sensitivity, linear readout of internal representations, and causal control via activation steering. Prompt-only results show systematic condition sensitivity, but the directional pattern does not reproduce human realization-effect predictions. Gemma's residual stream contains a linearly decodable realization-status signal at layer 18 that generalizes to held-out prompts. Steering along this direction does not, however, reliably shift downstream risk choices, a null result that holds across positive scales and in a negative sign-symmetry run. Behavioral sensitivity, latent readout, and causal control are three distinct properties that do not automatically co-occur, and successful latent readout is insufficient evidence that a model behaviorally relies on a representation during downstream decision-making.

</details>


### [179] [SimuWoB: Simulating Real-World Mobile Apps for Fast and Faithful GUI Agent Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25160)
*Guohong Liu,Jialei Ye,Pengzhi Gao,Wei Liu,Jian Luan,Yunxin Liu,Yuanchun Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SimuWoB is a fully synthetic benchmark for mobile GUI agents with 120 challenging tasks, using a virtual environment generation framework that creates high-fidelity, reward-validated tasks deployed as backend-free webpages. Experiments show current SOTA agents achieve only 27.92% average success rate (17.82% on long-horizon tasks), revealing significant weaknesses in complex scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mobile GUI benchmarks prioritize reproducibility but are limited to open-source apps or file-operation tasks due to difficulty in constructing rewards for real applications, creating a gap between benchmark settings and real-world usage. Most benchmarks also focus on basic grounding and navigation with limited coverage of complex, long-horizon interactions.

Method: The authors build a robust virtual environment generation framework that synthesizes high-fidelity tasks and environments with automatic reward validation. Each environment is deployed as a backend-free webpage accessible via URL for efficient, reproducible evaluation. The benchmark includes 120 challenging tasks spanning diverse types and difficulty levels.

Result: Average success rate of SOTA mobile GUI agents is only 27.92%, dropping to 17.82% on long-horizon tasks. Evaluation results from synthetic environments generalize well when compared with real-world sample tasks.

Conclusion: Current mobile GUI agents have substantial weaknesses in complex, long-horizon scenarios. SimuWoB provides diagnostic insights across key capability dimensions and implications for future mobile GUI agent development.

Abstract: Mobile GUI agents powered by large language models have progressed rapidly, creating urgent needs for realistic and comprehensive evaluation. Existing benchmarks prioritize reproducibility but are often limited to open-source apps or file-operation tasks for the difficulty of constructing rewards on real applications, leaving a gap between benchmark settings and real-world usage. Moreover, most benchmarks focus on basic grounding and navigation, with limited coverage of complex, long-horizon interactions. To address these limitations, we introduce SimuWoB, a fully synthetic benchmark for mobile GUI agents with 120 challenging tasks spanning diverse types and difficulty levels. We build a robust virtual environment generation framework that synthesizes high-fidelity tasks and environments, and automatically provides valid rewards for each task. Each environment is deployed as a backend-free webpage accessible via URL, enabling efficient and reproducible evaluation. We conduct comprehensive experiments on several state-of-the-art mobile GUI agents. The average success rate is only 27.92%, dropping to 17.82% on long-horizon tasks, which reveals substantial weaknesses in current agents under complex scenarios. Evaluation result comparison with real-world sample tasks demonstrate that agent assessments based on our synthetic environment generalize well. We further provide diagnostic insights across key capability dimensions and discuss implications for future mobile GUI agent development.

</details>


### [180] [SpecAlign: A Semantic Alignment Framework for SystemVerilog Assertion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25181)
*Jaime Rafael Imperial,Hao Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SpecAlign is a framework that uses entailment-based classification, chain-of-thought reasoning, and self-consistency voting to evaluate and iteratively refine semantic alignment between LLM-generated SystemVerilog Assertions and natural language specifications, without requiring golden RTL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
