Motivation: Existing LLM approaches to SVA generation focus on syntactic validity and formal verification outcomes but fail to quantify semantic alignment with natural language specifications, leading to hallucinated or misaligned assertions that reduce confidence and increase debugging effort when golden RTL is unavailable.

Method: SpecAlign introduces two iterative alignment loops using entailment-based classification to assess both natural language properties and SVAs against the design specification. It improves alignment decisions by generating multiple reasoning paths via chain-of-thought prompting and aggregating them through self-consistency voting. Misaligned assertions are analyzed to produce actionable feedback, and a quantitative alignment score measures semantic consistency across iterations.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that SpecAlign effectively detects semantic inconsistencies and improves assertion alignment without relying on golden RTL, serving as a scalable complement to traditional formal verification evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: SpecAlign provides a scalable, RTL-free complement to formal verification metrics for evaluating and refining semantic alignment of LLM-generated SVAs with natural language specifications.

Abstract: Existing Large Language Model (LLM) approaches to SystemVerilog Assertion (SVA) generation primarily focus on syntactic validity and formal verification outcomes, while semantic alignment between generated assertions and natural language specifications remains difficult to quantify. As a result, hallucinated or misaligned SVAs can reduce confidence and increase debugging efforts in the absence of golden RTL. This paper presents SpecAlign, a framework for semantic evaluation and refinement of LLM-generated SVAs. SpecAlign introduces two iterative alignment loops that assess both natural language properties and SVAs against the design specification using entailment-based classification. We improve alignment decisions by generating multiple reasoning paths using chain-of-thought prompting and aggregating them via a self-consistency voting mechanism. Misaligned assertions are analyzed to generate actionable feedback for refinement. We further define a quantitative alignment score to measure semantic consistency across iterations. Experimental results demonstrate that SpecAlign effectively detects semantic inconsistencies and improves assertion alignment without relying on golden RTL, providing a scalable complement to traditional formal verification evaluation metrics.

</details>


### [181] [DarkForest: Less Talk, Higher Accuracy for Multi-Agent LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25188)
*Yi Li,Songtao Wei,Dongming Jiang,Zhichun Guo,Qiannan Li,Bingzhe Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DarkForest is a controlled-communication coordination framework for multi-agent LLM systems that first keeps agents independent, then parses and clusters their responses, estimates a calibrated belief distribution, and allows a coordinator to access only policy-permitted evidence. It achieves up to 30.7% improvement over baselines and reduces token consumption by up to 6.5x.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interaction-heavy multi-agent LLM methods suffer from error propagation (incorrect reasoning being adopted and amplified) and high communication overhead (increased token consumption, latency, and inference cost).

Method: DarkForest: (1) keeps agents independent to produce answers without seeing others' outputs; (2) parses raw responses into structured candidate records; (3) groups semantically equivalent candidates into clusters; (4) estimates a calibrated belief distribution over clusters using agent reliability, confidence, parse quality, support-pattern reliability, and independence corrections; (5) a coordinator receives only policy-permitted evidence from this belief state.

Result: On six reasoning benchmarks, DarkForest achieves leading overall quality, improves the strongest baseline by up to 30.7% on benchmark metrics, and reduces token consumption by up to 6.5x compared with communication-heavy baselines.

Conclusion: Controlled communication via structured belief estimation can match or exceed the reasoning quality of interaction-heavy multi-agent systems while drastically reducing computational cost.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems improve reasoning by combining outputs from multiple agents, but interaction-heavy methods can introduce error propagation and high communication overhead. When agents exchange raw responses or reasoning traces, incorrect intermediate reasoning may be adopted and amplified, leading to confident but wrong consensus; multi-round communication also increases token consumption, latency, and inference cost. In this paper, we propose a controlled-communication coordination framework named DarkForest. DarkForest first keeps agents independent, so each agent produces an answer without seeing the others' outputs. It then parses the raw responses into structured candidate records, groups semantically equivalent candidates into clusters, and estimates a calibrated belief distribution over these clusters using agent reliability, confidence, parse quality, support-pattern reliability, and independence corrections. A coordinator receives only policy-permitted evidence from this belief state with controlled communication. Experiments on six reasoning benchmarks show that DarkForest achieves leading overall quality, improves the strongest baseline by up to 30.7\% on benchmark metrics, and reduces token consumption by up to $6.5\times$ compared with communication-heavy baselines.

</details>


### [182] [Boosting Inference with Guided Reasoning: Stochastic Exploration for Recursive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25230)
*Andrew Corbett,Archit Sood,Anna Tzatzopoulou,Sai-Aakash Ramesh,Tim Dodwell*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a probabilistic inference framework for understanding recursive neural architectures, where deterministic recursion is treated as a limit of stochastic reasoning dynamics. Using guided stochastic exploration—perturbing reasoning trajectories and reweighting via early-stopping heads—the authors propose three label-free diagnostics (local stability, guide alignment, cloud-token entropy) that predict improvement potential and output trustworthiness. This achieves 98.0% (from 85.9%) on Sudoku-Extreme and identifies guide misalignment on Maze-Hard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing recursive architectures succeed on structured reasoning but their inference-time behavior is poorly understood. The paper aims to reframe deterministic recursion as approximate inference over latent trajectories, enabling principled diagnostics and improvement strategies without retraining.

Method: The authors (1) treat deterministic recursion as the zero-noise, one-particle limit of a latent dynamical system; (2) propose guided stochastic exploration—adding stochastic perturbations to propose neighboring trajectories, then reweighting them using the model's existing early-stopping head; (3) derive three label-free diagnostics: local stability (robustness to perturbations), guide alignment (consistency with the early-stopping head), and cloud-token entropy (diversity of trajectory ensemble).

Result: On Sudoku-Extreme, exact-solve accuracy improves from 85.9% to 98.0% without retraining. On Maze-Hard, the diagnostics detect a misaligned guide, which validation performance later confirms. The diagnostics predict from inference traces alone whether improvement is possible and which outputs to trust.

Conclusion: The probabilistic inference view of recursive reasoning unifies understanding of when and why trajectory-level improvements work, and provides actionable, label-free diagnostics that characterize both the room for improvement and the reliability of the model's internal guide.

Abstract: Recent work on recursive architectures has shown that tiny neural networks can be surprisingly powerful on structured reasoning tasks. The trick is to model reasoning trajectories with a latent dynamical system. We argue that the inference-time behaviour of these architectures is best understood as approximate inference over latent reasoning trajectories, with deterministic recursion as the one-particle, zero-noise limit. We make this view operational through guided stochastic exploration: stochastic perturbations of the reasoning dynamics propose neighbouring trajectories, and the model's existing early-stopping head reweights them online. The framework yields three label-free diagnostics: local stability, guide alignment, and cloud-token entropy. These predict, from inference traces alone, whether the procedure will help and which of its outputs to trust. On Sudoku-Extreme it lifts exact-solve accuracy from $85.9\%$ to $98.0\%$ without retraining; on Maze-Hard the diagnostics flag a misaligned guide, as validation performance later confirms. The same machinery thus characterises both when recursive reasoning has room to improve at the trajectory level and when the model's internal guide can recover it.

</details>


### [183] [Meta-Agent: From Task Descriptions to Verified Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25233)
*Andy Xu,Yu-Wing Tai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Meta-Agent is a two-phase framework that automatically builds and executes specialized multi-agent systems from natural-language tasks. It uses a task planner to decompose problems into directed acyclic graphs of agents with contracts and verification, integrates web search for grounding, and employs a three-level error attribution mechanism for targeted recovery. Experiments show consistent improvements in success rate, error recovery, and workflow stability over strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent frameworks are brittle as workflows scale; small intermediate errors propagate through agent interactions, and insufficient grounding and weak verification mechanisms limit reliability.

Method: A two-phase framework: (1) Construction phase — task planner decomposes problems into DAGs of agent specs with I/O contracts and verification, web search module grounds specs, code generation produces prompts/tools, construction-time verification triggers targeted regeneration on failure; (2) Execution phase — coordinator dispatches subtasks, execution-time verification gates intermediate outputs, and a three-level error attribution mechanism (local, upstream, structural failures) enables targeted recovery (local retries, partial re-execution, re-decomposition).

Result: Consistent improvements in task success rate, error recovery, and workflow stability across coding, contextual learning, and open-ended reasoning tasks compared to strong multi-agent baselines and ablations.

Conclusion: Tightly integrating planning, grounding, and verification is critical for building reliable multi-agent systems.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly used to solve complex, multi-step tasks, but existing multi-agent frameworks remain brittle as workflows grow in scale and depth. Small errors at intermediate stages can propagate through agent interactions, while insufficient grounding and weak verification mechanisms further limit reliability. We present Meta-Agent, a two-phase framework that automatically constructs and executes specialized multi-agent systems from natural-language task descriptions. In the construction phase, a task planner decomposes a problem into a directed acyclic graph of agent specifications with explicit input/output contracts and verification criteria. A web search module grounds each specification with external evidence, and a code generation module produces system prompts and tool configurations. A construction-time verification stage then validates generated artifacts and triggers targeted regeneration when failures are detected. In the execution phase, a coordinator dispatches subtasks across the agent graph while execution-time verification gates intermediate outputs. We further introduce a three-level error attribution mechanism that distinguishes local, upstream, and structural failures, enabling targeted recovery strategies ranging from localized retries to partial re-execution and re-decomposition. We evaluate Meta-Agent across coding, contextual learning, and open-ended reasoning tasks. Experiments against strong multi-agent baselines and ablation studies demonstrate consistent improvements in task success rate, error recovery, and workflow stability. The results highlight the importance of tightly integrating planning, grounding, and verification for building reliable multi-agent systems.

</details>


### [184] [FrontierOR: Benchmarking LLMs' Capacity for Efficient Algorithm Design in Large-Scale Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25246)
*Minwei Kong,Chonghe Jiang,Ao Qu,Wenbin Ouyang,Zhaoming Zeng,Xiaotong Guo,Zhekai Li,Junyi Li,Yi Fan,Xinshou Zheng,Xi Jing,Yikai Zhang,Zhiwei Liang,Seonghoo Kim,Runqing Yang,Zijian Zhou,Sirui Li,Han Zheng,Wangyang Ying,Ou Zheng,Chonghuan Wang,Jinglong Zhao,Hanzhang Qin,Cathy Wu,Paul Pu Liang,Jinhua Zhao,Hai Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FrontierOR is a new benchmark evaluating whether LLMs can design scalable, efficient optimization algorithms for realistic large-scale problems, finding that even the strongest models succeed only in 31% of cases against Gurobi and 50% on hard tasks with test-time evolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks are limited to small or simplified examples far below real-world scale and complexity, and practical OR problems require designing scalable algorithms that exploit problem structure and outperform direct formulation-and-solve baselines.

Method: FrontierOR includes 180 tasks derived from top-tier OR papers, each with standardized instances and a hidden, expert-verified evaluation suite. Seven LLMs (frontier, cost-effective, and open-source) are evaluated in both one-shot and test-time evolution settings.

Result: The strongest one-shot model outperforms Gurobi in only 31% of cases on both solution quality and computational efficiency; strong coding agents with test-time evolution achieve only 50% on selected hard tasks.

Conclusion: FrontierOR establishes a practical evaluation platform showing that frontier models still struggle to move from executable formulations to efficient optimization algorithms, enabling future systematic testing of LLM-based optimization algorithm design.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for optimization modeling and solver-code generation, yet practical operations research and optimization problems often require a harder capability: designing scalable algorithms that exploit problem structure and outperform direct formulation-and-solve baselines. Existing benchmarks are limited to small or simplified examples far below real-world scale and complexity. We introduce FrontierOR, among the first benchmarks to systematically evaluate LLM-based efficient algorithm design for realistic large-scale optimization problems. FrontierOR includes 180 tasks derived from methodologically diverse papers published in top-tier operations research venues, each with standardized instances and a hidden, expert-verified evaluation suite. We evaluate seven LLMs spanning frontier, cost-effective, and open-source models both in one-shot and test-time evolution settings. The results reveal that frontier models still struggle to move from executable formulations to efficient optimization algorithms: the strongest one-shot model outperforms Gurobi in only 31% of cases in both solution quality and computational efficiency, and even strong coding agents with test-time evolution achieve only 50% on selected hard tasks. FrontierOR establishes a practical evaluation platform for LLM-based optimization algorithm design, which enables future LLMs and agents to be systematically tested on whether they can move beyond correct formulation toward a feasible, high-quality, and efficient algorithm. Our FrontierOR Benchmark is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/efficient-opt-bench-F03D.

</details>


### [185] [LipoAgent: Coordinating Fine-Tuned LLM Agents for Safer Lipid Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25250)
*Leshu Li,An Lu,Haiyu Wang,Zhibin Feng,Conghui Duan,Qing Bao,Zongmin Zhao,Sai Qian Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LipoAgent is a safety-aware multi-agent LLM framework that improves lipid nanoparticle discovery by enforcing toxicity as a prerequisite for efficiency prediction, achieving 32% better mRNA transfection efficiency prediction and validated wet-lab results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing lipids that are both effective and biologically safe remains a major bottleneck in LNP-based nucleic acid delivery; toxicity makes efficiency prediction clinically irrelevant.

Method: LipoAgent combines domain-specific finetuning with a conditional prediction objective (toxicity prerequisite for efficiency), multi-agent verification, and lightweight human oversight for disagreement resolution.

Result: Average 32% relative improvement in mRNA transfection efficiency prediction across multiple foundation models; wet-lab validation confirmed virtual screening rankings translate to biological outcomes.

Conclusion: LipoAgent effectively addresses the safety-efficiency trade-off in lipid design, with code publicly available.

Abstract: Lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) are among the most clinically mature platforms for nucleic acid delivery, yet designing lipids that are both effective and biologically safe remains a major bottleneck. In practical screening, toxicity is a decision-level constraint: if a lipid is toxic, its efficiency prediction is clinically irrelevant. We propose LipoAgent, a safety-aware multi-agent LLM framework for lipid discovery. LipoAgent combines domain-specific finetuning with a conditional prediction objective that enforces toxicity as a prerequisite for efficiency prediction, and further improves reliability via multi-agent verification with lightweight human oversight when disagreement persists. Across multiple foundation models, LipoAgent achieves an average 32% relative improvement in mRNA transfection efficiency prediction compared with other reported models for lipid design. Wet-lab validation confirms that virtual screening rankings reliably translate to biological transfection outcomes. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/SAI-Lab-NYU/LipoAgent.git.

</details>


### [186] [Whose Alignment? Comparing LLM Process Alignment Across Diverse Organizational Decision Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25256)
*Niklas Weller,Emilio Barkett*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper challenges the single-target framing of AI alignment with organizations, proposing process alignment (measuring whether LLMs weight information like the organization) instead of mere output agreement. Using ECHR Article 6 decisions, process alignment strongly predicts accuracy (r=0.85, p<.001), but this relationship collapses in German credit decisions (r=0.15, p=.60), revealing that in contested domains, high process alignment is neither achievable nor unconditionally desirable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors argue that aligning AI systems with organizational decision-making is typically framed as a single-target problem (making the model behave like the organization), but this obscures a deeper pluralistic challenge where different organizations or contexts may have conflicting decision-making processes.

Method: The authors develop a decision-policy capturing method to measure process alignment (whether LLMs weight information as the organization does, not just reaching same conclusions) and apply it to two domains: ECHR Article 6 decisions and German consumer credit decisions.

Result: In ECHR Article 6, process alignment strongly predicts output accuracy (r=0.85, p<.001) and externalization improves alignment for poorly-aligned models. In German consumer credit decisions, this relationship collapses (r=0.15, p=.60): interventions produce inconsistent effects and the benchmark encodes discriminatory historical patterns.

Conclusion: Output agreement alone cannot distinguish a model that has internalized organizational policy from one that merely approximates outcomes; process-level measurement is necessary for pluralistic alignment evaluation. In contested domains, high process alignment is neither achievable via externalization nor unconditionally desirable.

Abstract: Aligning AI systems with organizational decision-making is typically framed as a single-target problem: make the model behave like the organization. We argue this framing obscures a deeper pluralistic challenge. We rely on a decision-policy capturing method to measure process alignment: whether an LLM weights information as the organization does, not merely whether it reaches the same conclusions. Applying this method to ECHR Article 6 decisions, process alignment strongly predicts output accuracy (r = 0.85, p < .001) and externalization substantially improves alignment for poorly-aligned models. Applying it to German consumer credit decisions, this relationship collapses (r = 0.15, p = .60): interventions produce inconsistent effects and the benchmark encodes potentially discriminatory historical patterns. This contrast is itself a pluralistic alignment finding: in contested domains, high process alignment is neither achievable via externalization nor unconditionally desirable. Output agreement alone cannot distinguish a model that has internalized an organizational policy from one that merely approximates its outcomes; process-level measurement is a necessary component of any pluralistic alignment evaluation.

</details>


### [187] [AI Cartography: Mapping the Latent Landscape of AI Benchmark Ecosystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25272)
*Michael Hardy,Anka Reuel,Lijin Zhang,Jodi M. Casabianca,Sang Truong,Yash Dave,Hansol Lee,Benjamin Domingue,Sanmi Koyejo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a framework using CFA and Generalizability Theory to analyze measurement noise in AI benchmarks. Analyzing 4,000+ models from the Open LLM Leaderboard, it finds that current scoring underestimates benchmark relationships, items exhibit local dependence, contributor metadata explains ~9% of ranking variance, manifest-score scaling slopes are unreliable (Rβ=0.53) while latent general-factor slopes are stable (Rg=0.97), and provides diagnostics for trustworthy benchmark comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregate leaderboard scores contain substantial unquantified measurement noise, making it unclear whether rankings reflect genuine capability differences or evaluation artifacts.

Method: Applied Confirmatory Factor Analysis (CFA) and Generalizability Theory to 4,000+ models from the Open LLM Leaderboard to decompose sources of ranking variance.

Result: (1) Current reporting underestimates benchmark relationships; (2) local dependence among items undermines benchmark validity; (3) contributor metadata explains ~9% of rank-relevant variance, more than architecture or deployment; (4) manifest-score scaling slope has low reliability (Rβ=0.53) while latent general-factor slope is highly stable (Rg=0.97).

Conclusion: Provides actionable diagnostics for determining benchmark trustworthiness and improving benchmark design, with unique insights into which benchmarks reflect LLM size versus post-training practices.

Abstract: While aggregate leaderboard scores drive AI development, they contain substantial measurement noise whose sources and magnitudes remain unquantified, making it unclear when rankings reflect genuine capability differences versus evaluation artifacts. We introduce a framework for measuring the latent landscape in AI benchmark ecosystems. Applying Confirmatory Factor Analysis (CFA) and Generalizability Theory to 4,000+ models from the Open LLM Leaderboard, we decompose sources of ranking variance and establish: (1) structures assumed in current reporting practice underestimate the strength of relationships between benchmarks; (2) evidence of local dependence among leaderboard items, undermining uses of benchmarks as measurement instruments under current scoring systems; (3) contributor metadata explains more rank-relevant variance ($\approx9\%$) than architecture or deployment categories in this context; (4) a manifest-score "scaling law" slope has low reliability ($R_β=0.53$); by contrast, the latent general-factor size slope is highly stable across ecosystem controls ($R_g=0.97$). We are able to provide unique insights into benchmark dynamics, such as which benchmarks are a function of LLM size and which can be oppositely impacted by post-training practices. We provide actionable diagnostics to determine how benchmark rankings can be trusted and how benchmark design can be improved.

</details>


### [188] [Context-CoT: Enhancing Context Learning via High-Quality Reasoning Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25354)
*Hongbo Jin,Mingnan Zhu,Jingqi Tian,Xu Jiang,Zhongjing Du,Haoran Tang,Siyi Xie,Qiaoman Zhang,Jiayu Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show a major gap in context learning—dynamically extracting and applying new knowledge from complex contexts—achieving only 17.2% average performance on CL-Bench, compared to their strength in static reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify and quantify the capability gap in LLMs' context learning—their ability to dynamically extract, internalize, and apply new knowledge from task-specific contexts—as opposed to static pretrained knowledge reasoning.

Method: Evaluated frontier LLMs on CL-Bench, a benchmark designed to test context learning ability.

Result: Frontier models solve only 17.2% of context-dependent tasks on average, revealing a significant capability gap.

Conclusion: LLMs struggle substantially with context learning compared to static reasoning, indicating a critical area for improvement.

Abstract: While LLMs excel at reasoning over prompts using static pretrained knowledge, they struggle significantly with context learning-the ability to dynamically extract, internalize, and apply new knowledge from complex, task-specific contexts. Recent evaluations on the CL-Bench reveal a critical capability gap: frontier models solve only 17.2% of context-dependent tasks on average.

</details>


### [189] [Second Guess: Detecting Uncertainty Through Abstention and Answer Stability in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25394)
*Ashwath Vaithinathan Aravindan,Mayank Kejriwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Second Guess is a lightweight, parameter-free prompting technique for abstention in MCQA that leverages the insight that models that truly know an answer will select it consistently, while uncertain models show unstable behavior when an "I don't know" option is added. It achieves 10.81% composite risk improvement on SLMs (2B-8B parameters) and maintains 8% improvement on fine-tuned models where entropy-based methods degrade.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often generate confident but incorrect answers rather than abstaining when uncertain, and this problem is especially acute for small language models (SLMs) due to computational constraints and autonomous operation, highlighting the need for reliable uncertainty detection.

Method: A lightweight, parameter-free prompting technique called Second Guess for abstention in multiple-choice question answering (MCQA) that detects uncertainty by observing whether a model consistently selects the same answer when an "I don't know" option is added; consistent selection indicates true knowledge while unstable behavior indicates uncertainty.

Result: Evaluated on four open models (2B-8B parameters) and four benchmarks, Second Guess achieves the highest composite risk improvement of 10.81%. It maintains an 8% composite risk improvement on fine-tuned models where entropy-based methods degrade, and improves most for lower-performing models.

Conclusion: Second Guess is an effective, parameter-free abstention technique for SLMs in MCQA that outperforms entropy-based methods, especially on fine-tuned and lower-performing models, and all code/results are publicly available for reproduction.

Abstract: Large language models often generate confident but incorrect answers rather than abstaining when uncertain. This problem is particularly acute for small language models (SLMs), where computational constraints and autonomous operation amplify the need for reliable uncertainty detection. We propose _Second Guess_, a lightweight, parameter-free prompting technique for abstention in multiple-choice question answering (MCQA) that is well-suited for SLMs. Our key empirical insight is that models which truly know an answer will select it consistently, while uncertain models exhibit unstable behavior when an ``I don't know'' option is added. Evaluated on four open models (2B-8B parameters) and four benchmarks, Second Guess achieves the highest composite risk improvement of 10.81\%. Notably, it maintains an 8\% composite risk improvement on fine-tuned models where entropy-based methods degrade, and improves most for lower-performing models. All code and results required to reproduce this work is available in https://github.com/Mystic-Slice/second-guess

</details>


### [190] [Towards end-to-end LLM-based censoring-aware survival analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25399)
*Yishu Wei,Hexin Dong,Yi Lin,Jiahe Qian,Yi Liu,Yifan Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMSurvival is a framework that enables large language models to perform censoring-aware survival analysis on tabular clinical data by reformulating time-to-event prediction as pairwise ranking, outperforming traditional models like Cox PH and deep learning survival models on ICU mortality and fracture risk prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are rarely used as end-to-end survival models because censoring in survival analysis prevents straightforward supervised fine-tuning of LLMs.

Method: LLMSurvival reformulates time-to-event prediction as pairwise ranking among comparable subjects, and derives test-time risk by aggregating comparisons against anchor individuals from the training cohort.

Result: LLMSurvival improves concordance over Cox PH by 3.1% (ICU mortality) and 0.5% (fracture risk), and over three deep learning survival models by 2.1% (ICU mortality) and 2.8% (fracture risk) on average.

Conclusion: The LLMSurvival framework serves as a proof of concept for an integrated, censoring-conscious approach to survival analysis via LLMs, demonstrating high portability and superior performance over expert-curated scores.

Abstract: Objective: Survival analysis is central to medical prediction, yet large language models (LLMs) are rarely used as end-to-end survival models because censoring prevents straightforward supervised fine-tuning. Here we present LLMSurvival, a framework that enables censoring-aware survival analysis with unmodified LLMs operating directly on tabular clinical data.
  Materials and Methods: LLMSurvival reformulates time-to-event prediction as pairwise ranking among comparable subjects, and derives test-time risk by aggregating comparisons against anchor individuals from the training cohort.
  Results: Across two clinical tasks (ICU mortality prediction in MIMIC-IV and fragility fracture prediction in a NewYork-Presbyterian/Weill Cornell Medicine cohort), LLMSurvival improves overall concordance over Cox proportional hazards modeling by 3.1% for ICU mortality and 0.5% for fracture risk, 2.1% on average for ICU mortality and 2.8% for fracture risk over three established deep learning survival models.
  Discussion: The results show that survival modeling with censoring can be made compatible with LLM fine-tuning through comparison-based reformulation. The framework demonstrates high portability and superior performance over expert curated scores like SAPS-II and FRAX scores across diverse clinical context. Furthermore, the framework supports local deployment, as compact, publicly available base models provide sufficient performance.
  Conclusion: The LLMSurvival framework serves as a proof of concept for an integrated, censoring-conscious approach to survival analysis via LLMs.

</details>


### [191] [CODESKILL: Learning Self-Evolving Skills for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25430)
*Yanzhou Li,Yiran Zhang,Xiaoyu Zhang,Xiaoxia Liu,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CODESKILL is an LLM-based framework that uses reinforcement learning to extract, evolve, and maintain multi-granularity procedural skills from coding-agent trajectories, improving downstream task performance by 9.69% over no-skill baselines while keeping skill bank size stable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill construction methods rely on fixed prompts and heuristic update rules, lacking principled mechanisms for knowledge selection, abstraction, and maintenance to optimally serve downstream agents.

Method: CODESKILL reformulates skill extraction and skill-bank maintenance as a learnable management policy trained with reinforcement learning, using a hybrid reward combining dense rubric-based skill-quality feedback and sparse verifiable execution feedback from a frozen downstream agent.

Result: On EnvBench, SWE-Bench Verified, and Terminal-Bench 2, CODESKILL improves average pass rate by 9.69 over no-skill baseline and 4.01 over strongest prompt-based/memory baseline, with stable skill bank size during iterative construction.

Conclusion: Learnable skill management policies outperform fixed heuristics and prompts for coding agent self-evolution, enabling effective procedural knowledge extraction and compact maintenance.

Abstract: Coding agents produce rich trajectories while solving software-engineering tasks. To enable agent self-evolution, these trajectories can be distilled into reusable procedural skills that compactly encode experience to guide future behavior. However, existing skill construction and maintenance methods often rely on fixed prompts and heuristic update rules, leaving it unclear how knowledge should be selected, abstracted, and maintained to best serve downstream agents. We propose CODESKILL, an LLM-based framework that reformulates skill extraction and skill-bank maintenance as a learnable management policy. CODESKILL extracts multi-granularity procedural skills from coding-agent trajectories, evolves skills with new experience, and maintains a compact skill bank for future task solving. We train CODESKILL with reinforcement learning, using a hybrid reward that combines dense rubric-based skill-quality feedback with sparse verifiable execution feedback from the frozen downstream agent. Experiments on EnvBench, SWE-Bench Verified, and Terminal-Bench 2 show that CODESKILL improves average pass rate by 9.69 over the no-skill baseline and by 4.01 over the strongest prompt-based or memory baseline, while maintaining the skill bank at a stable size during iterative construction.

</details>


### [192] [Security of OpenClaw Agents: Fundamentals, Attacks, and Countermeasures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25435)
*Yuntao Wang,Jianle Ba,Han Liu,Yanghe Pan,Jintao Wei,Zhou Su,Tom H. Luan,Linkang Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper surveys the security landscape of OpenClaw agents—a new class of open-source LLM-driven autonomous agents with persistent memory and high autonomy—categorizing threats like skill poisoning, cognitive manipulation, multi-agent cascading failures, and supply-chain vulnerabilities, and reviewing defense mechanisms within a layered framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid evolution of LLM-driven autonomous agents has given rise to OpenClaw frameworks that operate continuously with persistent memory and high autonomy, which introduces a substantially enlarged attack surface that traditional security analyses do not cover.

Method: The paper presents a comprehensive survey: it first examines the general architecture and key characteristics distinguishing OpenClaw agents from traditional AI agent systems, then categorizes security and privacy threats into a layered framework analyzing how vulnerabilities arise during agent reasoning, action execution, and external interaction, and finally reviews representative defense mechanisms.

Result: Identified threats include skill poisoning, cognitive manipulation, multi-agent cascading failures, and supply-chain vulnerabilities. A layered framework categorizing these threats is presented, alongside a review of current defense mechanisms.

Conclusion: Several unresolved issues related to the reliability and trustworthiness of OpenClaw ecosystems remain, indicating open problems for future research.

Abstract: The rapid evolution of large language model (LLM)-driven autonomous agents has given rise to OpenClaw, a new class of open-source agent frameworks that operate as continuously running, skill-augmented systems with persistent memory, multi-channel interaction, and high degrees of autonomy. Such capabilities enable OpenClaw agents to autonomously execute complex, multi-step tasks and interact seamlessly with external applications, but simultaneously introduce a substantially enlarged attack surface. In particular, the combination of high-privilege operations and persistent memory exposes OpenClaw agents to various emerging threats, including skill poisoning, cognitive manipulation, multi-agent cascading failures, and supply-chain vulnerabilities. In this survey, we present a comprehensive study of the security landscape of OpenClaw agents. We first examine the general architecture and key characteristics that distinguish OpenClaw agents from traditional AI agent systems. We categorize existing security and privacy threats into a layered framework and analyze how vulnerabilities arise during agent reasoning, action execution, and external interaction. Representative defense mechanisms are also reviewed to draw the current defense landscape. Finally, several unresolved issues related to the reliability and trustworthiness of OpenClaw ecosystems are discussed.

</details>


### [193] [A Signal-Language Foundation Model for Broad-Spectrum Cardiovascular Assessment from Routine Electrocardiography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25446)
*Ziqing Yu,Yuhui Tao,Jiayu Huo,Lei Pan,Zilong Xiao,Juecheng Chen,Xiao Li,Jianxuan Li,You Zhou,Zhixing Li,Cong Wang,Beijian Zhang,Chen Chen,Hongyang Lu,Konstantinos Patlatzoglou,Daniel B. Kramer,Jonathan W. Waks,Yangang Su,Fu Siong Ng,Shuo Wang,Yixiu Liang,Junbo Ge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ECGCLIP is a signal-language contrastive learning framework aligning ECG waveforms with expert diagnostic reports, pre-trained on 2.8M ECG studies from 1.3M patients. Evaluated on ~1.5M ECGs across 89 tasks (45 diagnoses, 39 echo targets, 5 rare diseases), it outperformed baselines, improved detection of rare conditions, and achieved strong performance with only 10% of training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional AI ECG models are limited to common arrhythmias and generalize poorly across populations or clinically subtle/rare diseases, motivating the need for a more comprehensive and generalizable ECG interpretation framework.

Method: ECGCLIP employs contrastive language-image pre-training (CLIP) to align ECG waveforms with paired expert diagnostic reports, pre-trained on 2,837,962 ECG studies from 1,324,856 patients.

Result: ECGCLIP consistently outperformed random initialization and Merl-R18 baselines across 89 tasks. It achieved strong internal PRAUC for AF (0.900) and STEMI (0.383), improved rare disease detection (e.g., Ebstein anomaly 0.253, cardiac amyloidosis 0.201), matched full-dataset performance with only 10% training data, and showed clinically meaningful representations via saliency analysis.

Conclusion: Large-scale ECG-report contrastive pre-training can expand routine ECG interpretation beyond common arrhythmias toward broad cardiovascular assessment, echocardiographic target prediction, and opportunistic screening of rare conditions.

Abstract: Electrocardiography (ECG) is central to cardiovascular care, but conventional AI models are often restricted to common arrhythmias and may generalize poorly across populations or clinically subtle diseases. We developed ECG Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (ECGCLIP), a signal-language contrastive learning framework that aligns ECG waveforms with expert diagnostic reports. ECGCLIP was pre-trained on 2,837,962 ECG studies from 1,324,856 patients and evaluated on a held-out internal test set plus nine independent external cohorts comprising about 1.5 million ECGs. Evaluation covered 89 downstream tasks, including 45 ECG diagnoses, 39 echocardiographic targets, and 5 rare cardiac diseases, using PRAUC as the primary metric. ECGCLIP consistently improved performance over random initialization and Merl-R18 baselines. On the internal test set, ECGCLIP-R34 achieved strong performance for atrial fibrillation (PRAUC 0.900) and ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction (PRAUC 0.383), with robust generalization across all external cohorts. It also improved low-prevalence and diagnostically elusive diseases, including Ebstein anomaly, constrictive pericarditis, dextrocardia, and cardiac amyloidosis, with internal PRAUC values of 0.253, 0.175, 0.121, and 0.201, respectively. ECGCLIP was data efficient, matching or exceeding full-dataset baseline performance with only 10% of training data. Feature visualization and saliency analysis suggested clinically meaningful representations aligned with established electrocardiographic criteria. These findings indicate that large-scale ECG-report contrastive pre-training can expand routine ECG interpretation beyond common arrhythmias toward broad cardiovascular assessment and opportunistic screening of echocardiographic and rare conditions.

</details>


### [194] [ATWL: A Formal Language for Representing, Comparing, and Reusing Visual Analytics Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25489)
*Natalia Andrienko,Gennady Andrienko,Jürgen Bernard,Michael Sedlmair*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ATWL is a declarative language for formally representing visual analytics workflows using eight artifact types and standardized transform intents. Workflows are extracted via LLM-assisted supervised interaction, enabling cross-workflow analysis revealing structural regularities. Controlled experiments show ATWL representations outperform prose in providing iteration structure, typed data flow, adaptation provenance, and context scalability for LLM-based reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VA workflows are complex and typically described in unstructured prose, hindering systematic comparison, reuse of proven strategies, and training of novices. A formal representation is needed to capture workflow structure and analytical intent.

Method: Design of the Artifact-Transform Workflow Language (ATWL) based on a modular ontology of eight artifact types and transforms with standardized intents. Workflows are extracted from research papers via supervised interaction with LLM agents (human role reduced to review and refinement). A library of 17 ATWL workflows was constructed; cross-workflow analysis identified structural regularities. A controlled experiment compared LLM problem-solving using original papers vs. ATWL representations.

Result: Cross-workflow analysis reveals recurrent meta-structure, recurring motifs, reusable building blocks, diverse iterative strategies, and cross-domain equivalences invisible in prose. In the controlled experiment, ATWL representations systematically added explicit iteration structure, typed data flow, fragment-level adaptation provenance, and compactness supporting scaling beyond prose libraries' context limits.

Conclusion: ATWL enables a transition from narrative descriptions to formally represented, comparable, and reusable analytical knowledge in visual analytics.

Abstract: Visual analytics (VA) workflows are inherently complex, involving data transformation, feature engineering, visual representation, and human interpretation. They are typically described in unstructured prose, hindering systematic comparison, reuse of proven strategies, and training of novices. We present Artifact-Transform Workflow Language (ATWL), a domain-agnostic, declarative language that formally represents VA workflows by capturing their structure and underlying analytical intent. ATWL is built upon a modular ontology of eight artifact types (entities, features, arrangements, visualisations, patterns, models, knowledge, specifications) and transforms characterised by standardised intents (e.g., define-unit, characterise, contextualise, abstract). To show that formalisation effort need not impede adoption, we extract workflows from research papers through supervised interaction with LLM agents, reducing the human role to review and refinement. Using this process, we constructed a library of seventeen ATWL workflows from published VA papers. Cross-workflow analysis reveals structural regularities -- a recurrent meta-structure, recurring motifs, reusable building blocks, diverse iterative strategies, and cross-domain equivalences -- that remain invisible in prose. We further evaluate practical utility through a controlled experiment in which the same LLM addressed two analytical problems with the library supplied either as original papers or as ATWL representations. Both forms enabled useful recommendations, but the formal representation systematically added explicit iteration structure, typed data flow, fragment-level adaptation provenance, and compactness supporting scaling beyond what prose libraries can fit in an LLM's context. ATWL enables a transition from narrative descriptions to formally represented, comparable, and reusable analytical knowledge.

</details>


### [195] [Credit Assignment with Resets in Language Model Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25507)
*Ankur Samanta,Akshayaa Magesh,Ayush Jain,Youliang Yu,Daniel Jiang,Kavosh Asadi,Daniel Jiang,Kaveh Hassani,Paul Sajda,Jalaj Bhandari,Yonathan Efroni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Two reset-based policy optimization methods (RRPO and SRPO) improve credit assignment in reinforcement learning for language model reasoning by locating and correcting specific faulty reasoning steps, with SRPO outperforming standard GRPO across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards assigns a single outcome reward uniformly across all tokens, failing to identify which reasoning steps contributed to success or failure. This limits learning efficiency as entire trajectories are updated uniformly rather than targeting faulty steps.

Method: Proposes RRPO (resetting from random intermediate reasoning states) and SRPO (model self-localizes erroneous steps in incorrect trajectories and resets there). Analyzed within Conservative Policy Iteration (CPI) framework, showing that extending CPI with a credit-assignment oracle yields provable improvements.

Result: SRPO consistently outperforms standard GRPO and RRPO across models and reasoning benchmarks by sampling multiple suffix continuations at self-localized reset points and learning from their rewards, requiring no external supervision.

Conclusion: Reset-based credit assignment, especially self-localized resets (SRPO), enables more precise and efficient policy improvement for multi-step reasoning in language models compared to uniform reward assignment.

Abstract: Contemporary reinforcement learning with verifiable reward methods post-train language models on multi-step reasoning by assigning a single outcome reward uniformly across all tokens in a trajectory. Such uniform assignment ignores which steps contributed to success or failure. Improving credit assignment can address this limitation by enabling targeted refinement of faulty reasoning steps, rather than updating entire trajectories uniformly. Resets are one such simple mechanism, enabling more precise credit assignment by returning to an intermediate state and resampling counterfactual continuations, so that outcome differences can be attributed to decisions made at that point. We propose two such methods: Random-Reset Policy Optimization (RRPO), where reset states are drawn randomly from reasoning steps, and Self-Reset Policy Optimization (SRPO), where the model self-localizes the erroneous step in an incorrect trajectory and resets there. We analyze these methods within the Conservative Policy Iteration (CPI) framework. Extending CPI with a credit-assignment oracle that targets improvable states yields provable improvements over random resets. Across models and reasoning benchmarks, SRPO consistently outperforms standard GRPO and RRPO by sampling multiple suffix continuations at a self-localized reset and learning from their rewards, using only the model itself with no external supervision.

</details>


### [196] [What Gets Cited: Competitive GEO in AI Answer Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25517)
*Rahul Vishwakarma,Shushant Kumar,Ratnesh Jamidar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies competitive Generative Engine Optimization (GEO) by building a controlled RAG testbed with two candidate sources. Across 252,000 trials and 18 content factors, topical relevance and list position are the biggest drivers of being cited first, with price information and recency also helping; formatting-only edits have little impact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI answer engines cite only a few sources from retrieved pages, making visibility depend on being cited rather than just ranked. The authors aim to understand what makes one source more likely to be cited first when two candidates compete.

Method: A controlled two-document retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) testbed that injects exactly two candidate sources into the model context and measures which source is referenced by the first citation marker. 252,000 trials across six LLMs, paired comparisons under one factorial program over 18 content factors, with brand anonymization and counterbalanced source order to separate content effects from position bias. Mixed-effects models used for analysis.

Result: Topical relevance and list position are the biggest drivers of being cited first. Explicit price information and a recent timestamp consistently help. Completeness and trust cues add smaller gains. Formatting-only edits have little impact.

Conclusion: The study provides a reproducible evaluation protocol and a prioritized GEO checklist for practitioners, with positive qualitative feedback from an early internal pilot at Sprinklr.

Abstract: AI answer engines generate answers from retrieved pages but cite only a few sources. This makes visibility depend not just on ranking, but on being cited. We study competitive Generative Engine Optimization (GEO): when two retrieved candidates compete, what makes one more likely to be cited first? We build a controlled two-document retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) testbed that injects exactly two candidate sources into the model context and measures which source is referenced by the first citation marker in the output. Across six LLMs we execute 252,000 trials, repeated paired comparisons under one factorial program over 18 content factors. In each trial the two sources differ in exactly one factor; we use brand anonymization and counterbalanced source order to separate content effects from position bias. Mixed-effects models show that topical relevance and list position are the biggest drivers of being cited first. Including explicit price information and a recent timestamp also helps consistently. Completeness and trust cues add smaller gains, while formatting-only edits have little impact. We release a reproducible evaluation protocol and a prioritized GEO checklist for practitioners, and we exercised it in an early internal pilot at Sprinklr, where teams reported positive qualitative feedback on workflow usability.

</details>


### [197] [StructBreak: Structural Cognitive Overload-Induced Safety Failures in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25534)
*Yang Luo,Xinran Liu,Tiantian Ji,Zhiyi Yin,Lingyun Peng,Shuyu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StructBreak is a framework to quantify Structural Cognitive Overload (SCO) in MLLMs, revealing a black-box attack that achieves up to 97% Attack Success Rate (ASR) by exploiting structural reasoning brittleness, and demonstrating that current safety alignment fails under complex multimodal reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs excel at structural reasoning but exhibit logical brittleness in structural consistency (termed Structural Cognitive Overload, SCO), which prior work has overlooked by focusing only on typographic/pixel-level perturbations.

Method: Proposes StructBreak, an automated end-to-end framework to quantify SCO, operating as a black-box attack with no internal model access. Establishes a benchmark spanning ten threat scenarios with model-level interpretation via attention dynamics, latent space topology, and geometric analysis.

Result: Achieves 92% average ASR (up to 97% on Gemini 2.5) across six leading MLLMs; SCO acts as a structural channel to circumvent safety filters; current alignment paradigms are insufficient for complex multimodal reasoning.

Conclusion: StructBreak reveals SCO as a novel attack vector that bypasses safety mechanisms, highlighting fundamental inadequacies in existing alignment approaches for MLLMs under complex reasoning demands.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) excel at structural reasoning yet suffer from a sharp logical brittleness in structural consistency. We term this phenomenon Structural Cognitive Overload (SCO), a byproduct of the contention between deep reasoning and safety alignment. However, prior work has predominantly targeted typographic and pixel-level perturbations, leaving the study of SCO largely unexplored. To this end, we propose StructBreak, an automated end-to-end framework designed to quantify SCO. By leveraging StructBreak, we uncover a novel higher-order cognitive overload attack paradigm; notably, this attack operates under a practical black-box setting, requiring no internal model access. Consequently, we utilize this framework to establish a comprehensive benchmark spanning ten diverse threat scenarios. Empirical evaluations on six leading MLLMs reveal that SCO readily triggers toxic generation, yielding a 92% average ASR (up to 97% on Gemini 2.5). To elucidate the mechanism of SCO, we further conduct model-level interpretations spanning attention dynamics, latent space topology, and geometric analysis. Our findings reveal that StructBreak acts as a novel structural channel to circumvent safety filters. Furthermore, the limited efficacy of inherent safety mechanisms underscores that current alignment paradigms are insufficient for the era of complex multimodal reasoning.

</details>


### [198] [Personalize-then-Store: Benchmarking and Learning Personalized Memory for Long-horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25535)
*Yeonjun In,Wonjoong Kim,Sangwu Park,Kanghoon Yoon,Chanyoung Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Existing LLM memory systems use universal, static policies that mismatch user-specific needs. The paper introduces PerMemBench (the first benchmark for personalized memory systems) and proposes session-level storage gating, finding personalization improves retention but accurate gating remains challenging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM memory systems apply one-size-fits-all policies, failing to recognize that memory storage needs differ across users, wasting limited budget on transient interactions while losing critical long-horizon context.

Method: Introduces PerMemBench benchmark with multi-year, multi-domain interaction histories and diverse user personas. Proposes session-level storage gating framework that selectively bypasses memory operations for transient sessions.

Result: Personalization yields substantial retention gains under perfect gating, but accurate gating remains an open critical challenge.

Conclusion: Personalized memory policies show promise for improving LLM memory systems, but reliable session gating mechanisms need further development.

Abstract: Existing large language model (LLM) based memory systems apply universal, static policies that overlook a fundamental reality: the contexts that are worth storing in memory are different across users. This misalignment wastes limited memory budget on transient interactions while failing to preserve critical context for long horizon tasks. To address this gap, we investigate an underexplored question: can LLM based memory systems learn personalized memory policies? We introduce PerMemBench, the first benchmark for evaluating personalized memory systems, featuring multi year, multi domain interaction histories across diverse user personas. We further present the first empirical study of memory personalization, proposing session level storage gating, a lightweight framework that selectively bypasses memory operations for transient sessions. Our study confirms that personalization yields substantial retention gains under perfect gating, yet reveals that accurate gating remains an open and critical challenge.

</details>


### [199] [ADMFormer: An Adaptive-Decomposition Transformer with Time-Varying Masked Spatial Attention for Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25543)
*Ruiwen Gu,Qitai Tan,Yahao Liu,Xiao-Ping Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ADMFormer is a traffic forecasting model using an Adaptive-Decomposition Transformer with time-varying masked spatial attention that decouples traffic signals into periodic regularities and residual fluctuations, processes them via a dual-branch temporal module, and sparsifies spatial interactions dynamically, achieving state-of-the-art results on four datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traffic forecasting is challenging due to heterogeneous temporal patterns (stable periodic regularities co-existing with event-driven fluctuations) that existing methods treat uniformly, and dynamic/sparse spatial dependencies where dense all-pairs attention introduces redundant interactions and amplifies noise.

Method: (1) A time-node adaptive gating mechanism decouples traffic signals into dominant regularities and residual fluctuations. (2) A dual-branch temporal module captures global periodic dependencies and high-frequency irregular variations separately. (3) A time-varying masked spatial attention sparsifies spatial interactions based on real-time traffic states.

Result: Extensive experiments on four real-world datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance.

Conclusion: ADMFormer effectively addresses heterogeneous temporal patterns and dynamic sparse spatial dependencies in traffic forecasting by adaptive decomposition and time-varying masked attention, outperforming existing methods.

Abstract: Accurate traffic forecasting is essential for intelligent transportation systems, supporting a wide range of real-world applications. However, it remains challenging due to two key factors:~(1) Traffic series contain heterogeneous temporal patterns, where stable periodic regularities coexist with event-driven fluctuations. Existing methods often treat them within a unified representation, limiting their ability to capture fine-grained temporal dynamics.~(2)Spatial dependencies among nodes are inherently dynamic and sparse, while dense all-pairs attention often introduces redundant interactions and amplifies noise. To address these issues, we propose ADMFormer, an Adaptive-Decomposition Transformer with Time-Varying Masked Spatial Attention. Specifically, ADMFormer first employs a time-node adaptive gating mechanism to decouple traffic signals into dominant regularities and residual fluctuations that vary across time and nodes. A dual-branch temporal module is then designed to separately capture global periodic dependencies and high-frequency irregular variations from these two decomposed components. Furthermore, ADMFormer introduces a time-varying masked spatial attention that sparsifies spatial interactions based on real-time traffic states, thereby effectively preserving dynamic and informative dependencies. Extensive experiments on four real-world datasets demonstrate that ADMFormer achieves state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [200] [PHGNet: Prototype-Guided Hypergraph Construction for Heterogeneous Spatiotemporal Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25554)
*Ruiwen Gu,Yahao Liu,Zhenyu Liu,Qitai Tan,Xiao-Ping Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PHGNet is a prototype-guided hypergraph framework for traffic forecasting that captures high-order spatiotemporal dependencies by assigning pattern-similar nodes to hyperedges, using global-local node representations for consistent feature extraction, and leveraging iterative residual refinement with Temporal Query Attention for accurate parallel decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing traffic forecasting methods are limited to pairwise spatial dependency modeling, failing to capture dynamic high-order interactions among nodes with similar traffic patterns, which is crucial for handling spatial heterogeneity in traffic systems.

Method: PHGNet proposes: (1) a prototype learning mechanism to adaptively assign pattern-similar nodes to hyperedges, capturing dynamic high-order interactions; (2) a global-local node representation module to extract time-consistent features for reliable hypergraph construction; (3) iterative residual refinement and Temporal Query Attention for accurate forecasting with efficient parallel decoding.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that PHGNet achieves superior predictive performance compared to state-of-the-art methods.

Conclusion: The prototype-guided hypergraph approach effectively addresses the limitation of pairwise modeling in traffic forecasting by capturing dynamic high-order spatiotemporal dependencies, leading to improved accuracy.

Abstract: As a core task in intelligent transportation systems, traffic forecasting plays a critical role in urban traffic management. Accurate traffic forecasting relies on modeling complex spatiotemporal dependencies, which is inherently challenging due to spatial heterogeneity in traffic systems.Despite significant progress, most existing methods are still limited to pairwise spatial dependency modeling, making it difficult to capture dynamic high-order interactions among nodes with similar traffic patterns. To address this issue, we propose PHGNet, a novel spatiotemporal forecasting framework based on prototype-guided hypergraph construction. At the core of PHGNet, a prototype learning mechanism is designed to adaptively assign pattern-similar nodes to hyperedges, thereby capturing high-order interactions with time-varying structures. To improve the reliability of dynamic hypergraph construction, we further develop a global-local node representation module to extract time-consistent features. For forecasting, iterative residual refinement and Temporal Query Attention are introduced to improve forecasting accuracy while supporting efficient parallel decoding. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that PHGNet achieves superior predictive performance compared with state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [201] [Beyond Query Memorization: Large Language Model Routing with Query Decomposition and Historical Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25558)
*Bo Lv,Jingbo Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DecoR is a novel LLM routing framework that reformulates model selection as a query-matching process using historical logs, with a capability deconstruction method to disentangle surface form from task requirements, achieving superior accuracy and lower inference costs on both in-distribution and OOD data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM routing methods rely on direct query-to-model mapping based on surface-level features, which leads to memorization traps and poor generalization on out-of-distribution (OOD) data. The goal is to optimize the trade-off between predictive performance and computational cost.

Method: DecoR reframes routing as a matching process that identifies similar queries from historical logs; introduces a query capability deconstruction method to decouple linguistic surface forms from task-intrinsic requirements, directing matching toward capability dimensions; and constructs CodaSet, a benchmark for assessing routing generalization.

Result: Experimental results show DecoR maintains superior accuracy while substantially lowering inference costs across both in-distribution and OOD settings.

Conclusion: DecoR effectively mitigates the memorization trap in LLM routing, achieves better generalization on OOD data, and provides a more robust and cost-effective routing solution.

Abstract: Optimizing the trade-off among predictive performance and computational cost is a central focus in the deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs). Current routing methods primarily rely on direct mapping from queries to models based on surface-level features, making them susceptible to the memorization trap and leading to poor generalizability on out-of-distribution (OOD) data. In this paper, we propose DecoR, a novel routing framework that recasts the routing task as a matching process of sifting similar queries from historical logs, effectively mitigating the memorization trap. To enhance matching accuracy, we introduce a query capability deconstruction method that decouples linguistic surface forms from task-intrinsic requirements, directing matching toward capability dimensions to ground decisions in essential task attributes. Furthermore, we develop CodaSet, a comprehensive benchmark for assessing routing generalization, where experimental results demonstrate that DecoR maintains superior accuracy while substantially lowering inference costs across both in-distribution and OOD settings. All the codes and data are available at https://github.com/lvbotenbest/DecoR.

</details>


### [202] [Uncertainty Reasoning with Large Language Models for Explainable Disease Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25566)
*Xiaoyang Fan,Yufan Cai,Zhe Hou,Jin Song Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A neuro-symbolic reasoning framework that aligns LLMs with formal fuzzy logic to enable explainable, verifiable, and human-aligned clinical diagnosis through two-stage inductive generalization and inference verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs lack verifiability and interpretability essential for trustworthy medical AI, despite excelling at extracting information from natural language patient narratives.

Method: The framework embeds patient descriptions and clinical guidelines into a neural knowledge base, extracts structured medical entities and fuzzy symptom patterns via LLMs, decodes them into a symbolic knowledge base with fuzzy logic and declarative rules, then performs two-stage reasoning: (1) inductive symbolic generalization and (2) inference verification via a logic programming engine. Symptoms are treated as fuzzy predicates with probabilistic weights.

Result: The framework achieves performance comparable to state-of-the-art LLMs on public benchmarks while additionally providing interpretable reasoning paths and formally verifiable diagnostic conclusions.

Conclusion: By combining logic-based transparency, LLM adaptability, and probabilistic robustness, the framework enables human-aligned healthcare inference with strong generalization and verifiable step-by-step reasoning chains, supporting iterative refinement through formal rules.

Abstract: Clinical decision-making requires reasoning over incomplete, imprecise, and linguistically expressed patient narratives. While large language models (LLMs) excel at extracting latent information from natural language, they lack the verifiability and interpretability essential for trustworthy medical AI. We propose a neuro-symbolic reasoning framework that aligns LLMs with formal logic to enable explainable and formally verifiable medical diagnosis. Patient descriptions and clinical guidelines are embedded into a neural knowledge base, where LLMs extract structured medical entities, temporal relations, and fuzzy symptom patterns, which are decoded into a symbolic knowledge base expressed in fuzzy logic and declarative rules. We perform two-stage reasoning: (1) inductive symbolic generalization to capture diagnostic patterns from encoded narratives, and (2) inference verification via a logic programming engine to derive and validate diagnoses consistent with clinical standards. Each symptom is treated as a fuzzy predicate with probabilistic weights, and inference paths are auditable, adjustable, and compatible with physician feedback. Unlike purely statistical methods, our system supports iterative refinement: misalignment between LLM-generated diagnoses and ground truth can be traced, explained, and corrected through formal rules. By combining logic-based transparency, LLM adaptability, and probabilistic robustness, the framework enables human-aligned healthcare inference with strong generalization and verifiable, step-by-step reasoning chains. We validate our framework on public benchmarks, demonstrating effective reconciliation of symbolic reasoning and LLMs with real-world clinical narratives. Results show performance comparable to state-of-the-art LLMs, while additionally providing interpretable reasoning paths and formally verifiable diagnostic conclusions.

</details>


### [203] [Detecting Unfaithful Chain-of-Thought via Circuit-Guided Internal-External Discrepancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25603)
*Xu Shen,Zhen Tan,Song Wang,Pingjun Hong,Rui Miao,Xin Wang,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CIE-Scorer detects chain-of-thought unfaithfulness in LLMs by combining internal circuit tracing (sentence-level) with external reasoning traces, using Fused Gromov-Wasserstein distance to measure discrepancy between internal and external reasoning graphs, achieving SOTA performance on FaithCoT-Bench with reduced computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CoT unfaithfulness detectors rely on external signals (textual plausibility, answer consistency) and overlook internal model computation evidence. Full circuit tracing for long CoTs is costly and hard to scale.

Method: CIE-Scorer traces compact sentence-level circuits from informative reasoning tokens, constructs internal and external reasoning graphs, and measures their discrepancy using Fused Gromov-Wasserstein distance for instance-level CoT unfaithfulness detection.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on four datasets from FaithCoT-Bench while reducing circuit construction cost.

Conclusion: Combining mechanistic interpretability signals with external reasoning traces is effective for CoT unfaithfulness detection, and sentence-level circuit tracing offers a scalable alternative to full circuit construction.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning improves the problem-solving ability of large language models (LLMs), but generated reasoning traces may not faithfully reflect the model's actual decision process. Existing CoT unfaithfulness detectors mainly rely on external signals from generated rationales, such as textual plausibility or answer consistency, while overlooking evidence from the model's internal computation. Although recent circuit tracing methods provide a way to obtain model-internal evidence by tracing how information flows through model components during reasoning, constructing full reasoning circuits for long CoTs is costly and difficult to scale. To address these challenges, we propose Circuit-guided Internal-External Discrepancy Scorer (CIE-Scorer), a framework for instance-level CoT unfaithfulness detection. The key idea is that faithful reasoning traces should align with the model's computational process, whereas unfaithful traces may diverge from it. CIE-Scorer efficiently traces compact sentence-level circuits from informative reasoning tokens, constructs internal and external reasoning graphs, and measures their discrepancy using Fused Gromov--Wasserstein distance. Experiments on four datasets from FaithCoT-Bench show that CIE-Scorer achieves state-of-the-art performance while reducing the cost of circuit construction, demonstrating the effectiveness of combining mechanistic interpretability signals with external reasoning traces for CoT unfaithfulness detection.

</details>


### [204] [Back to Parsimonious Latents: Learning Task-Centric World Models from Visual Foundations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25620)
*Minghao Fu,Fan Feng,Nicklas Hansen,Biwei Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TC-WM turns frozen foundation-model embeddings into compact, task-sufficient world representations for reward-free offline control by linear projection, contrastive alignment, and embedding reconstruction, achieving superior planning and control across diverse environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world models either learn representations from pixels with limited semantic structure or inherit frozen foundation-model embeddings that contain excessive task-irrelevant detail, resulting in state spaces poorly suited for planning and control — especially challenging in reward-free offline settings without reward supervision or online interaction.

Method: TC-WM (1) linearly projects high-dimensional visual embeddings from pretrained foundation models into a compact latent dynamic space, (2) aligns a subspace with the agent's physical state via contrastive learning, and (3) reconstructs embeddings to retain useful visual structure. The pretrained embedding space serves as a semantic scaffold rather than the final state space.

Result: Theoretically, TC-WM identifies underlying task-centric latent factors up to a simple transformation. Empirically, it achieves better world-modeling quality and more precise control than state-of-the-art approaches in test-time planning across Robomimic and D4RL environments.

Conclusion: TC-WM effectively combines the generality of foundation features with the controllability of task-centric dynamics, providing a principled framework for building task-sufficient world representations in reward-free offline settings.

Abstract: World models enable agents to predict future dynamics conditioned on actions, making the choice of latent representation central to planning and control. Such representations are often either learned directly from pixels with limited semantic structure or inherited from frozen visual foundation models with excessive task-irrelevant detail, yielding state spaces that are poorly matched to downstream planning and control. This is especially challenging in reward-free offline settings, where the model must learn from fixed trajectories without reward supervision or online interaction. To address this, we propose TC-WM, a framework for turning foundation-model embeddings into compact, task-sufficient world representations. The key design is to treat the pretrained embedding space as a semantic scaffold rather than as the final state space: TC-WM linearly projects high-dimensional visual embeddings into a compact latent as the dynamic space, aligns a subspace with the agent's physical state via contrastive learning, and reconstructs embeddings to preserve useful visual structure. This combines the generality of foundation features with the controllability of task-centric dynamics. Theoretically, we show that TC-WM suffices to identify the underlying task-centric latent factors up to a simple transformation. Empirically, TC-WM enables test-time planning across diverse environments (e.g., Robomimic and D4RL), achieving better world-modeling quality and more precise control than state-of-the-art approaches.

</details>


### [205] [CUA-Gym: Scaling Verifiable Training Environments and Tasks for Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25624)
*Bowen Wang,Dunjie Lu,Junli Wang,Tianyi Bai,Shixuan Liu,Zhipeng Zhang,Haiquan Wang,Hao Hu,Tianbao Xie,Shuai Bai,Dayiheng Liu,Que Shen,Junyang Lin,Tao Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CUA-Gym is a scalable pipeline that co-generates task instructions, environment states, and verifiable reward functions for computer-use agent RLVR training, producing 32,112 tuples across 110 environments. Models trained with it (CUA-Gym-A3B and A17B) achieve 62.1% and 72.6% on OSWorld-Verified, outperforming prior open-source CUAs at comparable scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR has driven breakthroughs in math, tool-use, and software engineering, but its extension to computer-use agents (CUAs) is bottlenecked by the scarcity of scalable training data with deterministic rewards. Hand-curated benchmarks have high reward fidelity but limited coverage, while LLM-as-judge datasets scale broadly but lack reliable verification.

Method: A Generator agent constructs initial and golden environment states; a Discriminator agent writes the reward function from the task specification. An orchestrator drives iterative rounds, and final filtering combines LLM majority voting with agent rollouts. They also synthesize CUA-Gym-Hub, a suite of high-fidelity mock web applications grounded in real-world software-use distributions. Training uses GSPO (Generalized Supervised Policy Optimization).

Result: CUA-Gym-A3B achieves 62.1% and CUA-Gym-A17B achieves 72.6% on OSWorld-Verified, outperforming prior open-source CUAs at comparable scales. Performance scales smoothly with both data volume and environment diversity. Checkpoints also improve on the held-out WebArena benchmark, showing transfer beyond training environments.

Conclusion: The CUA-Gym pipeline effectively addresses the scarcity of verifiable training data for CUA RLVR, and the resulting models demonstrate strong performance and transferability. The full pipeline, dataset, environments, and models will be open-sourced.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has driven breakthroughs in domains such as math, tool-use, and software engineering, yet its extension to computer-use agents (CUAs) has been bottlenecked by the scarcity of scalable training data with deterministic rewards. Constructing such data for CUAs requires consistent task instruction, executable environment, and verifiable reward. However, hand-curated benchmarks achieve high reward fidelity but cover few applications and LLM-as-judge-based datasets scale broadly but lack reliable verification. We present CUA-Gym, a scalable pipeline that co-generates task instructions, environment states, and reward functions. Concretely, a Generator agent constructs the initial and golden environment states, and a separate Discriminator agent writes the reward function from the task specification. An orchestrator agent drives the two through iterative rounds upon execution.
