Generated tuples then pass a final filter combining LLM majority voting and agent rollouts, ensuring quality beyond the per-task adversarial loop. To address the scarcity of training environments, we further synthesize CUA-Gym-Hub, a broad suite of high-fidelity mock web applications grounded in real-world software-use distributions, expanding the scale of CUA RLVR data by magnitude. Using this pipeline, we construct CUA-Gym, a dataset of 32,112 verified RLVR training tuples grounded in 110 environments. Trained with GSPO on CUA-Gym, our CUA-Gym-A3B and CUA-Gym-A17B achieve 62.1% and 72.6% on OSWorld-Verified, outperforming prior open-source CUAs at comparable scales, with performance scaling smoothly in both data volume and environment diversity. The same checkpoints also improve on the held-out WebArena benchmark, indicating transfer beyond the training environments. We will open-source the full synthesis pipeline, dataset, CUA-Gym-Hub environments, and models.

</details>


### [206] [Insuring Every Action: An Authority Frontier Framework for Runtime Actuarial Control of Autonomous AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25632)
*Hao-Hsuan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Actuarial Action Interface (AAI) is a deterministic runtime contract that prices side-effect-bearing actions of autonomous AI agents against reserve capital budgets, using a quote-bind-commit protocol and a seven-class action taxonomy. Evaluated across four agentic environments and a live Postgres panel with three Azure-hosted models, the Authority Frontier reveals a consistent low-reserve refusal and intermediate-release pattern, with reserve capital requirements varying by 22x across domains. The framework provides a benchmark-ready evaluation method for runtime actuarial control of agent side effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous AI agents increasingly issue side-effect-bearing actions (database mutations, refunds, payments, external commitments) that carry financial and operational risks, yet existing systems lack a principled mechanism to price, budget, and gate these actions at runtime.

Method: The paper proposes the Actuarial Action Interface (AAI), a deterministic runtime contract using time-consistent risk mapping to price actions against a safe default, gating execution by reserve capital. It introduces the Authority Frontier evaluation primitive, a quote-bind-commit protocol with toll-bounded capability tokens, a seven-class action taxonomy, replay determinism with alpha-spending, and cross-domain normalization via full reserve demand C_full and Capital@k metrics.

Result: Evaluated across four environments (database mutation, customer-service refund, tau-bench retail and airline) and a live Postgres panel with three Azure-hosted models. The Authority Frontier shows a common low-reserve refusal and intermediate-release pattern, with saturation only at full reserve demand. Required reserve capital varies by 22x (Capital@50 from 289 to 6457). The live panel shows the contract prevents realized loss across all three models at low budget, while model identity acts as an actuarial underwriting variable.

Conclusion: AAI provides a benchmark-ready evaluation framework for runtime actuarial control of autonomous-agent side effects, surfacing each domain's actuarial geometry without forcing uniform shape, and demonstrating that model identity is a significant underwriting variable.

Abstract: Autonomous AI agents increasingly issue side-effect-bearing actions: database mutations, refunds, payments, external commitments. We propose the Actuarial Action Interface (AAI), a deterministic runtime contract that prices each such action against a contractually fixed safe default under a time-consistent risk mapping, and gates execution against a per-boundary reserve capital budget. We then develop the Authority Frontier, an evaluation primitive measuring how much autonomous authority the runtime releases at each level of reserve capital. The framework provides (i) a deterministic quote-bind-commit protocol with toll-bounded capability tokens; (ii) a universal seven-class action taxonomy mapping heterogeneous tool calls to comparable authority units; (iii) replay determinism and pathwise reserve coverage under alpha-spending; (iv) cross-domain normalization via full reserve demand C_full and capital metrics Capital@k. We instantiate AAI across four agentic environments (database mutation, customer-service refund, and the public tau-bench retail and airline tool-use traces) and report a live Postgres panel in which three Azure-hosted models propose actions through the same contract. The frontier exhibits a common low-reserve refusal and intermediate-release pattern across domains, with saturation only where the budget grid reaches full reserve demand; required reserve capital varies by 22x (Capital@50 from 289 to 6457). The framework does not force domains into the same shape; it surfaces each domain's actuarial geometry. In the live panel the contract prevents realized loss across all three models at low budget while differing in underwriting persistence under denial: model identity is an actuarial underwriting variable. The contribution is a benchmark-ready evaluation framework for runtime actuarial control of autonomous-agent side effects.

</details>


### [207] [AgentHijack: Benchmarking Computer Use Agent Robustness to Common Environment Corruptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25707)
*Jingwei Sun,Jianing Zhu,Yuanyi Li,Tongliang Liu,Xia HU,Bo Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentHijack is a benchmark evaluating how MLLM-based computer-use agents perform under 9 common environmental corruptions (e.g., pop-ups, resolution changes). Experiments show significant performance degradation from minor corruptions, highlighting agent fragility. The authors propose AgentHijack-Agent, a framework with enhanced grounding and an onlooker module for environment checking, demonstrating improved robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world execution environments for autonomous computer-use agents are imperfect—pop-ups, resolution changes, and competing applications frequently interfere with agent perception and control. There is a need to systematically evaluate agent robustness under these common corruptions.

Method: AgentHijack introduces 9 configurable common corruptions simulating realistic imperfect scenarios. The proposed AgentHijack-Agent framework integrates an action generator with enhanced grounding capabilities and an onlooker module responsible for behavior summarization and environment checking.

Result: Even minor corruption instances cause substantial performance degradation in MLLM-based agents, emphasizing their fragility. The proposed AgentHijack-Agent framework demonstrates effectiveness through extensive experiments.

Conclusion: The findings underscore the necessity of robustness evaluation for computer-use agents. The AgentHijack benchmark and AgentHijack-Agent framework provide tools to assess and improve robustness against common environmental corruptions.

Abstract: Autonomous computer use agents that powered by multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are emerging as capable assistants for completing complex digital workflows. However, real-world execution environments are far from ideal: pop-ups, resolution changes, and competing applications frequently interfere with agent perception and control. We introduce AgentHijack, a benchmark designed to evaluate the robustness of computer-use agents under common corruptions, where the uncertainties in dynamic environment disrupt the execution flow without direct adversarial intent. Specifically, AgentHijack introduces 9 configurable common corruptions to replicate realistic imperfect scenarios. We evaluate a variety of desktop tasks that utilize MLLM-based agents and discover that even minor instances of corruption can result in substantial performance degradation, which emphasizes the fragility of agents and underscores the necessity of robustness evaluation. Afterward, we propose AgentHijack-Agent, a framework that integrates an action generator with enhanced grounding capabilities and an onlooker responsible for behavior summarization and environment checking. Extensive experiments validate its effectiveness. Our code, environment, baseline models and data are publicly available at: https://AgentHijack.github.io.

</details>


### [208] [FLOATBench: A Dataset and Benchmark for Floating Offshore Wind Turbine Tower Fatigue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25717)
*João Alves Ribeiro,Bruno Alves Ribeiro,Francisco Pimenta,Sérgio M. O. Tavares,Faez Ahmed*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FLOATBench is the first public tabular benchmark for fatigue-damage prediction of 22 MW floating offshore wind turbines, containing 582,120 labels from 19,404 high-fidelity OpenFAST simulations across three tower geometries, with regime-aware evaluation protocols for surrogate modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offshore wind resource in deep waters requires floating offshore wind turbines (FOWTs), but as designs scale to 22 MW class, tower fatigue prediction becomes critical and the field lacks a shared surrogate benchmark with consistent simulations, splits, and metrics for comparison.

Method: The authors built FLOATBench using 19,404 OpenFAST simulations across three 22 MW FOWT tower geometries (6,468 per tower: 1,078 aligned wind/wave operating points × six turbulence seeds), labeled at 30 cross-sections per tower. They designed a regime-aware alpha-shape partition of the joint wind/wave operating envelope and three evaluation protocols: random validation (E1), within-tower regime-aware (E2), and cross-tower transfer (E3).

Result: A public dataset with 582,120 per-section fatigue-damage labels. The regime-aware protocol reveals rank shifts between global and extrapolation performance that random-split leaderboards cannot detect.

Conclusion: FLOATBench is the first FOWT fatigue benchmark for tabular surrogate modeling, offering a reproducible evaluation protocol that generalizes to engineering surrogates defined over physical operating envelopes.

Abstract: Most of the world's offshore wind resource lies in waters too deep for fixed-bottom foundations, making floating offshore wind turbines (FOWTs) essential for deep-water deployment. As the industry scales toward $22$ MW class designs, tower fatigue becomes increasingly critical because larger structures amplify the coupled aero-hydro-servo-elastic loads induced by continuous wind and wave excitation. Accurate fatigue-damage prediction is therefore central to certification, design optimization, and cost reduction. Yet the field lacks a shared surrogate benchmark: studies report different simulations, splits, and metrics, making methods difficult to compare. We present FLOATBench, a public tabular benchmark with $582{,}120$ per-section fatigue-damage labels across three $22$ MW FOWT tower geometries, derived from $19{,}404$ high-fidelity OpenFAST simulations across the three towers ($6{,}468$ per tower: $1{,}078$ aligned wind/wave operating points $\times$ six turbulence seeds), labeled at $30$ cross-sections per tower. FLOATBench includes a regime-aware alpha-shape partition of the joint wind/wave operating envelope, stratifying test points into in-train, interpolation, and extrapolation regimes. It is paired with a reproducible evaluation harness covering three protocol levels: random validation (E1), within-tower regime-aware evaluation (E2), and cross-tower transfer (E3). The regime-aware protocol reveals rank shifts between global and extrapolation performance that random-split leaderboards cannot detect. To the authors' knowledge, FLOATBench is the first FOWT fatigue benchmark for tabular surrogate modeling, and offers an evaluation protocol that generalizes to engineering surrogates defined over physical operating envelopes. Dataset and code available at: https://github.com/Joao97ribeiro/FLOATBench.

</details>


### [209] [Learning to Search and Searching to Learn for Generalization in Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25720)
*Michael Aichmüller,Yannik Hesse,Hector Geffner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a self-improving WA* learning framework with a Relational Graph Neural Network value heuristic that iteratively guides search and improves via Q-learning, achieving strong zero-shot generalization on combinatorial planning tasks where DRL fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Combinatorial generalization is a central challenge in DRL. Classical planning provides a controlled setting to study this problem without perceptual learning, but standard RL exploration via real-time search is ineffective in sparse-reward domains, and learning-based methods often rely on expert demonstrations or goal-state random walks.

Method: A self-improving WA* learning framework combining a Relational Graph Neural Network (RGNN) as a value heuristic with Q-learning: the heuristic guides best-first search, and search results iteratively update the heuristic via Q-learning, creating a closed-loop improvement cycle.

Result: The approach yields heuristics that function as general policies, solving new instances even without search where DRL fails. Strong zero-shot generalization is demonstrated: heuristics trained on Blocksworld instances with <30 blocks solve instances with 488 blocks without search. Tested on Sokoban, PushWorld, The Witness, and IPC 2023 benchmarks.

Conclusion: The self-improving WA*+RGNN framework enables combinatorial generalization in planning domains, producing heuristics that can generalize to much larger unseen instances, outperforming standard DRL approaches.

Abstract: Combinatorial generalization remains a central challenge in Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL). Classical planning provides a simple yet challenging setting to study this problem through explicit relational descriptions, without requiring learning from perception. In sparse-reward domains, standard RL exploration via real-time search is ineffective, and learning-based planning methods often rely on expert demonstrations, hindsight relabeling, or random walks from the goal state. In contrast, planners rely on best-first search methods such as $\mathrm{A}^\star$ to solve problems from scratch. We propose a self-improving $\mathrm{WA}^\star$ learning framework in combination with a value heuristic represented by a Relational Graph Neural Network: the heuristic guides search, and the resulting search data updates the heuristic via $Q$-learning. This loop yields heuristics that can function as general policies and solve new instances even without search, where DRL otherwise fails, as we show on puzzles such as Sokoban, PushWorld, The Witness, and the 2023 International Planning Competition benchmarks. Notably, we demonstrate strong zero-shot generalization: For example, heuristics trained on Blocksworld instances with fewer than 30 blocks successfully solve instances with 488 blocks without search.

</details>


### [210] [A Deep Dive into Axiomatic Design -- Part I: Problem Formulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25735)
*Aydin Homay*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper clarifies the critical yet often misunderstood problem formulation step in axiomatic design—specifically, defining a minimum set of independent first-level functional requirements (FRs). It explains what first-level FRs are, why they should be invariant across designers given the same needs and constraints, and identifies common pitfalls leading to design failure. Grounded in Suh's three books, it offers practical guidance and briefly discusses implications of large language models for this step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Problem formulation (translating needs/constraints into first-level FRs) is the most critical step in axiomatic design but is frequently misunderstood and underestimated, leading to design failures.

Method: Conceptual analysis grounded in Nam P. Suh's three books (The Principles of Design, Axiomatic Design: Advances and Applications, and Complexity Theory); clarifies definitions, invariance properties, and common pitfalls with practical guidance.

Result: Clarification of what first-level FRs are/are not; demonstration that they should not vary across designers given identical needs and constraints; identification of recurring pitfalls; and a brief assessment of LLM capabilities for first-level problem formulation.

Conclusion: Proper problem formulation is essential for design success; LLMs can assist but have inherent limitations at the first-level FR formulation stage.

Abstract: Problem formulation translating customer needs and constraints into a minimum set of independent first-level functional requirements, is arguably the most critical step in every design framework, including axiomatic design yet it is frequently misunderstood or underestimated in practice. This paper focuses exclusively on problem formulation in axiomatic design it clarifies what first-level FRs are (and are not), explains why they should not legitimately vary across designers given the same needs and constraints, and highlights intrinsic difficulties and recurring pitfalls that lead to design failure. The discussion is grounded primarily in Nam P.Suh's three books. The Principles of Design, Axiomatic Design Advances and Applications, and Complexity Theory, and it offers practical guidance to help designers formulate well-posed first-level FRs. Finally, the paper briefly revisits problem formulation in the era of large language models and discusses what such tools can (and cannot) contribute at the first level.

</details>


### [211] [Agent-Centric Social Trajectory Prediction: A Free Energy Principle Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25748)
*Yanping Wu,Ji Zhang,Hao Chen,Edmond S. L. Ho,Chongfeng Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FEP-Diff is an agent-centric trajectory prediction framework grounded in the Free Energy Principle that extracts ego-motion and social cues from local observations, infers multimodal latent beliefs via free-energy optimization with social consistency constraints, and generates diverse future trajectories using a residual diffusion model conditioned on belief representations. It outperforms SOTA methods on five public benchmarks under restricted observability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing trajectory prediction methods rely on global state assumptions, suffer from insufficient belief inference under partial observability, and lack cognitive behavioral constraints, compromising deployment feasibility and physical plausibility in real-world settings.

Method: A dual-branch spatiotemporal encoder extracts ego-motion dynamics and social interaction cues from local observations; a goal-conditioned belief learner infers multimodal latent belief distributions optimized via a free-energy objective with social consistency constraints on the local neighborhood graph; a residual diffusion trajectory generator conditioned on learned belief representations via token-level proxy conditioning produces precise and diverse future predictions.

Result: FEP-Diff consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods on five public benchmarks under restricted observability.

Conclusion: FEP-Diff achieves cognitively plausible predictions under realistic partial observability constraints by grounding trajectory prediction in the Free Energy Principle, enabling more physically plausible and deployable predictions in real-world settings.

Abstract: Trajectory prediction methods have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in capturing complex motion patterns. However, existing methods rely on global state assumptions, suffer from insufficient belief inference under partial observability, and lack cognitive behavioral constraints in prediction. These limitations severely compromise both deployment feasibility and physical plausibility in real-world settings. In this work, we propose FEP-Diff, an agent-centric trajectory prediction framework grounded in the Free Energy Principle, aimed at achieving cognitively plausible predictions under realistic constraints. Specifically, a dual-branch spatiotemporal encoder extracts ego-motion dynamics and social interaction cues from local observations. Building upon this, a goal-conditioned belief learner infers multimodal latent belief distributions optimized via a free-energy objective, with a social consistency constraint on the local neighborhood graph to promote cognitive alignment among neighboring agents. Finally, a residual diffusion trajectory generator is conditioned on the learned belief representations with token-level proxy conditioning, producing precise and diverse future predictions. Extensive experiments on five public benchmarks demonstrate that FEP-Diff consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods under restricted observability. Code: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/FEP-Diff-8876.

</details>


### [212] [When Can We Trust Early Warnings? Leakage-Excluded Early Outcome Prediction from LMS Interaction Logs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25794)
*Ngoc Luyen Le,Marie-Hélène Abel,Bertrand Laforge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies temporal leakage in LMS-based early-warning models and introduces LEAP, a protocol to enforce cutoff-first truncation. Using OULAD, they show that leakage, especially from assessment data, inflates early performance, and that Random Forest works best at earliest cutoffs while Gradient Boosting dominates later.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reported "early" performance in LMS-based early-warning models is often inflated by temporal leakage—using information unavailable at prediction time. The paper aims to formalize and mitigate this problem.

Method: The authors formalize cutoff-based early outcome prediction under temporal constraints and introduce LEAP (Leakage-Excluded Early-Availability Protocol), which enforces cutoff-first truncation before joins/aggregation and audits feature provenance. They instantiate LEAP on OULAD across weekly cutoffs using ROC-AUC, PR-AUC, Brier score, and F1@0.5 with multiple standard learning methods.

Result: Performance improves as the observation window expands, with a notable gain around week 3. Random Forest performs best at earliest cutoffs; Gradient Boosting dominates thereafter. Leakage ablations show temporal violations, especially through assessment information, inflate apparent "early" performance.

Conclusion: Temporal leakage significantly inflates early-warning model performance, and LEAP provides a principled protocol to control for it. Assessment data is a primary source of leakage. Proper cutoff-first truncation is essential for realistic evaluation.

Abstract: Early-warning models built from Learning Management System (LMS) logs aim to predict end-of-course outcomes early enough to enable timely learner support. However, reported "early" performance is often inflated by temporal leakage. This occurs when the pipeline uses information that would not yet be available at the time of prediction. We formalize cutoff-based early outcome prediction under a temporal availability constraint and introduce LEAP (Leakage-Excluded Early-Availability Protocol), which enforces cutoff-first truncation prior to joins and aggregation and audits feature provenance to prevent post-cutoff evidence from entering the benchmark. We instantiate LEAP on the public Open University Learning Analytics Dataset (OULAD) as a multi-step protocol for leakage-controlled evaluation across weekly cutoffs. Using several standard learning methods, we evaluate performance using ROC-AUC, PR-AUC, Brier score, and F1@0.5. Results show improving performance as the observation window expands, with a marked gain around week~3; Random Forest performs best at the earliest cutoffs, while Gradient Boosting dominates thereafter. Leakage ablations further show that temporal violations, especially through assessment information, can inflate apparent "early" performance.

</details>


### [213] [Behind EvoMap: Characterizing a Self-Evolving Agent-to-Agent Collaboration Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25815)
*Qiming Ye,Peixain Zhang,Yupeng He,Zifan Peng,Gareth Tyson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study of EvoMap, a major A2A collaboration network with 1.5M assets and 128K agents, reveals fundamental flaws: 98% of assets are never reused, rewards are concentrated among a few agents, the scoring system is vulnerable to manipulation via unverified self-reported metadata, and over 84% of approved assets use vacuous tests (e.g., console.log) due to lack of independent validation. The authors conclude that scalable A2A collaboration requires mechanisms balancing open participation with verifiable execution and trustworthy evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how decentralized A2A collaboration networks operate in practice, as their real-world dynamics remain largely unexplored despite their potential for enabling autonomous AI agent cooperation.

Method: Large-scale empirical study of EvoMap, analyzing 1.5M assets and 128K agents; examining the credit economy incentive structure, the GDI scoring/ranking algorithm, and the validation/verification mechanisms.

Result: (1) Credit economy incentivizes mass production over quality: 98% of assets never reused, rewards highly concentrated. (2) GDI scoring system is manipulable via unverified self-reported metadata (e.g., claimed lines of code). (3) 84%+ of approved assets use vacuous tests (e.g., console.log) due to lack of independent verification.

Conclusion: Future A2A collaboration networks cannot rely on unverified self-reporting alone; scalable collaboration requires balancing open participation with verifiable execution and trustworthy evaluation.

Abstract: Agent-to-Agent (A2A) networks enable autonomous AI agents to collaborate by sharing reusable problem-solving instructions. However, how these decentralized ecosystems operate in practice remains largely unexplored. We present the first large-scale empirical study of EvoMap, a prominent A2A collaboration network. By analyzing over 1.5M assets and 128K agents, we show how design choices that prioritize scalable growth introduce trade-offs in reusability, evolution, and auditability. First, EvoMap's credit economy rewards agents for publishing valuable assets. Although this design encourages participation at scale, rewards are tied primarily to publication rather than adoption. This leads agents to mass-produce assets to accumulate credits. As a result, 98% of assets are never reused, while rewards become highly concentrated among a small fraction of agents. Second, EvoMap employs an algorithm (referred to as GDI) to score and rank the quality of these shared assets. We demonstrate that this scoring system is flawed: rather than measuring objective performance, an asset's rank is heavily dictated by unverified, self-reported metadata (e.g., claimed lines of code modified). This allows agents to trivially manipulate their asset's scores. Finally, EvoMap relies on agents to provide local execution logs as evidence that uploaded assets function correctly. Because these validations are not independently verified, over 84% of approved assets bypass quality checks using vacuous tests (e.g., console.log). Our findings show that future A2A collaboration networks cannot rely on unverified self-reporting alone. Scalable collaboration requires mechanisms that balance open participation with verifiable execution and trustworthy evaluation.

</details>


### [214] [MuCRASP: Multimodal Chain-of-thought Reasoning aware Structured Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25842)
*Aritra Dutta,Somak Aditya*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MuCRASP is a structured pruning framework for vision-language models that preserves chain-of-thought reasoning by targeting pivot tokens and accounting for cross-modal activation differences, outperforming prior methods at compression rates up to 50%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing structured pruning methods fail to preserve chain-of-thought reasoning accuracy in VLMs due to being CoT-agnostic and ignoring activation-distribution differences across visual and textual modalities.

Method: MuCRASP prunes reasoning-critical components (pivot tokens) while preserving cross-modal alignment and accounting for layer-wise sensitivity under a global parameter budget.

Result: At 30% pruning on Qwen2.5-VL-7B, MuCRASP achieves LLM-as-a-Judge score 8.87 vs 7.32 for strongest baseline on physical reasoning; maintains high reasoning consistency up to 50% pruning.

Conclusion: MuCRASP consistently preserves reasoning quality under increasing compression and significantly outperforms prior pruning approaches with lower perplexity degradation.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) increasingly rely on chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning to solve complex multimodal tasks, but their large parameter sizes make deployment expensive. Structured pruning offers a natural solution; however, existing methods fail to preserve CoT reasoning accuracy in VLMs. We identify two key reasons: (1) CoT consistency depends on sparse transition points (pivot tokens) in the generation trajectory, while existing pruning methods are CoT-agnostic; and (2) pruning methods designed for unimodal LLMs do not account for activation-distribution differences across visual and textual modalities. Motivated by these observations, we propose MuCRASP, a structured pruning framework that targets reasoning-critical components while preserving cross-modal alignment and accounting for layer-wise sensitivity under a global parameter budget. Experiments on four VLMs across three reasoning benchmarks show that MuCRASP consistently preserves reasoning quality under increasing compression. At 30% pruning on Qwen2.5-VL-7B, MuCRASP achieves an LLM-as-a-Judge score of 8.87 versus 7.32 for the strongest baseline on physical reasoning tasks. Furthermore, MuCRASP maintains high reasoning consistency up to 50% pruning, significantly outperforming prior pruning approaches while exhibiting lower perplexity degradation.

</details>


### [215] [From Accounting to Coordination: A Virtual Water-Aware Electricity-Computation-Water Nexus Framework for Data Center Dispatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25854)
*Haiyang You,Chengwei Lou,Jin Zhao,Yue Zhou,Lu Zhang,Jin Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel operational ECW nexus framework internalizes virtual water impacts into power system dispatch using differentiable optimization layers and fixed-point coordination, achieving 3-5% reductions in freshwater withdrawals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing static statistical accounting methods fail to capture how dispatch optimization and workload relocation dynamically affect water withdrawals, remaining decoupled from the optimization process and incapable of guiding workload relocation or power dispatch to mitigate water stress.

Method: Develops an ECW nexus framework that: (1) internalizes virtual water impacts as a differentiable optimization layer embedded within a deep learning architecture, (2) enables end-to-end learning of coordination policies while preserving operational feasibility, (3) uses fixed-point coordination to enforce consistency between virtual water attribution and physical generation-side withdrawals.

Result: Case studies on IEEE 30-bus and 118-bus systems demonstrate reliable convergence, exact power-water consistency, and approximately 3-5% reductions in generation-related freshwater withdrawals under water-constrained conditions.

Conclusion: The proposed dynamic ECW nexus framework outperforms static statistical approaches by enabling coordinated workload relocation and power dispatch to effectively mitigate water stress in data center operations.

Abstract: The expansion of data centers (DCs) drives a sustained increase in electricity demand and associated water withdrawals at generation sites. These withdrawals occur at generation sites and are virtually allocated to demand based on network power flows. Consequently, the actual water footprint of a specific load varies dynamically with generation dispatch and network conditions. Existing approaches typically rely on static statistical accounting to quantify these water footprints. However, such static methods fail to capture how dispatch optimization and workload relocation dynamically affect water withdrawals. As a result, static statistical accounting approaches remain decoupled from the optimization process, rendering them incapable of guiding workload relocation or power dispatch to mitigate water stress. To address this limitation, this paper develops an operational electricity-computation-water (ECW) nexus framework that internalizes virtual water impacts directly into power system dispatch. The framework represents dispatch optimization as a differentiable optimization layer embedded within a deep learning architecture, enabling efficient end-to-end learning of coordination policies while preserving operational feasibility. Combined with fixed-point coordination, the framework enforces consistency between virtual water attribution and physical generation-side withdrawals. Case studies on the IEEE 30-bus and 118-bus test systems demonstrate reliable convergence, exact power-water consistency, and reductions of approximately 3-5% in generation-related freshwater withdrawals under water-constrained conditions.

</details>


### [216] [$D^2$-Monitor: Dynamic Safety Monitoring for Diffusion LLMs via Hesitation-Aware Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25893)
*Aoxi Liu,Yupeng Chen,James Oldfield,Guanzhe Hong,Junchi Yu,Baoyuan Wu,Philip Torr,Adel Bibi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces D^2-Monitor, a bi-level safety monitor for diffusion LLMs that uses lightweight probes as always-on monitors and activates heavier probes only when safety hesitation (repeated proximity to decision boundaries) exceeds a threshold, achieving SOTA safety monitoring with ≤0.85M parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety monitoring for diffusion LLMs is largely unexplored despite their emergence as alternatives to autoregressive LLMs. Unlike AR-LLMs, D-LLMs generate text through multi-step denoising that exposes intermediate hidden representations containing safety-relevant information unavailable in standard single-step monitoring.

Method: The authors analyze trajectory-level signals to identify when lightweight probes struggle, finding safety hesitation (intermediate hidden states repeatedly near the decision boundary) as the most informative signal. They propose D^2-Monitor, a bi-level monitor: a lightweight probe jointly estimates hesitation and performs base classification; when hesitation exceeds a threshold, a heavier probe is activated for dynamic resource allocation.

Result: Evaluated on 3 datasets (WildguardMix, ToxicChat, OpenAI-Moderation) across 4 D-LLMs, D^2-Monitor achieves SOTA performance with ≤0.85M parameters and exhibits the best trade-off between effectiveness and efficiency relative to 8 baselines.

Conclusion: Safety hesitation robustly predicts probe failure in D-LLMs, and the proposed bi-level dynamic routing mechanism efficiently allocates monitoring resources at test time for optimal safety-accuracy-efficiency balance.

Abstract: Despite the emergence of diffusion large language models (D-LLMs) as an alternative to autoregressive large language models (AR-LLMs), safety monitoring for D-LLMs remains largely unexplored. Unlike AR-LLMs, D-LLMs generate text through a multi-step denoising process, exposing intermediate hidden representations that may contain safety-relevant information unavailable in standard single-step monitoring setups. Motivated by the suitability of lightweight probes for always-on monitoring, we analyze which trajectory-level signals best indicate when such probes are likely to struggle. We find that the most informative signal is safety hesitation: intermediate hidden states repeatedly falling within a small margin of the probe's decision boundary. The number of such hesitation steps in D-LLM's trajectory predicts probe failure effectively, providing a proxy of sample difficulty. Building on this analysis, we propose $D^2$-Monitor, a bi-level safety monitor for D-LLMs. $D^2$-Monitor adopts a lightweight probe as an always-on monitor to jointly estimate hesitation and perform base classification. When the hesitation level exceeds a threshold, a more expressive but computationally heavier probe is activated. This dynamic routing mechanism allocates monitoring resources efficiently at test time. Evaluated on 3 datasets (WildguardMix, ToxicChat, OpenAI-Moderation) across 4 D-LLMs, $D^2$-Monitor achieves state-of-the-art performance with a compact parameter footprint ($\leq$ 0.85M parameters), and exhibits the best trade-off between effectiveness and efficiency relative to 8 baselines.

</details>


### [217] [Explore Before You Solve: The Speed--Depth Trade-off in Epistemic Agents for ARC-AGI-3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25931)
*Liew Keong Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analysis of 25 public ARC-AGI-3 games reveals they are solvable via non-intelligent strategies (trivial heuristics), invalidating them as measures of exploration. The authors propose AERA, a three-phase agent, and formalize a Speed-Depth trade-off framework. Only the private 55-game evaluation remains a genuine intelligence test.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current interactive reasoning benchmarks (ARC-AGI-3 public set) may not genuinely require intelligent exploration; the authors aim to expose this vulnerability and propose a principled agent that balances exploration speed vs. information depth.

Method: Systematic vulnerability analysis of all 25 public games; proposal of AERA (EXPLORE / VERIFY / PLAN) agent using Qwen2.5-0.5B; formalization via Speed-Depth trade-off framework with convexity assumption yielding quadratic RHAE penalty.

Result: All 25 public games are reachable via non-intelligent strategies (10 single blind step, 5 after one probing action, etc.). AERA achieves RHAE=0.2116 (4/25 solved) on public games; random/no-explore baselines score 0.0000. Full private evaluation achieves RHAE=0.30.

Conclusion: The public ARC-AGI-3 evaluation set cannot discriminate intelligent exploration from trivial heuristics; the private 55-game set is the only valid intelligence test. AERA demonstrates that model capability and exploration strategy interact non-trivially.

Abstract: We systematically investigate all 25 public ARC-AGI-3 games and find that every one is reachable through non-intelligent strategies: 10 in a single blind step, 5 after one probing action, 1 via repeated ACTION1 presses, 1 via diverse exploration, and 8 via single repeated actions with sufficient budget (50-200 steps). A library-level null-coordinate vulnerability additionally bypasses 18 games in 1 step. This benchmark critique implies the public evaluation set cannot discriminate intelligent exploration from trivial heuristics - the private 55-game evaluation is the only genuine intelligence test. Against this backdrop, we present AERA (Adaptive Epistemic Reasoning Agent), a three-phase (EXPLORE / VERIFY / PLAN) agent achieving RHAE=0.2116 (4/25 solved) on these 25 games with Qwen2.5-0.5B, while random and no-explore baselines score 0.0000. We formalise AERA through a Speed--Depth trade-off framework: under a convexity assumption (proved for a class of environments in the Appendix), RHAE's quadratic form emerges as a second-order penalty for deviating from the Pareto frontier between action efficiency and information gain. Contributions: (i) a benchmark validity analysis showing that current interactive reasoning benchmarks fail to measure the exploration they claim to require, and (ii) the EXPLORE-before-PLAN framework and model-capability x exploration interaction. The linked code track entry achieves RHAE=0.30 on the full 55-game private evaluation. Code: CC0.

</details>


### [218] [LECTOR: Joint Optimization of Scientific Reasoning Graphs and Introduction Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25964)
*Jiabei Xiao,Yizhou Wang,Chen Tang,Pengze Li,Wanli Ouyang,Shixiang Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LECTOR is a Logic-Expression Co-Reinforcement Learning framework for generating scientific paper Introductions that are logically sound and faithfully grounded in the paper's evidence, using a logic-reasoning graph and co-rewarding mechanism to improve citation quality, graph fidelity, and overall consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic scientific paper Introduction writing is challenging because it requires not only linguistic fluency but also logical soundness and verifiable faithfulness. Existing AI-assisted methods treat it as text generation rather than reasoning and structuring, leading to issues like hallucinated citations.

Method: LECTOR first formulates the Content-Conditional Introduction Generation (CCIG) task requiring grounding in core evidence. It constructs a logic-reasoning graph from the paper's main body as a verifiable blueprint, then uses a Logic-Expression Co-Reinforcement Learning framework with a Co-Rewarding mechanism to jointly optimize structural fidelity and narrative quality.

Result: Experiments on a dataset from Nature Communications papers show improvements: Graph Quality (+26.7%), Citation Quality (+8.6%), and Paper Consistency (+3.3%).

Conclusion: LECTOR effectively addresses hallucination and logical coherence issues in Introduction generation by grounding writing in a verifiable logic-reasoning graph and optimizing both logic fidelity and narrative quality through co-reinforcement learning.

Abstract: AI Scientists have shown promising progress across multiple stages of the research pipeline, among which automatic scientific paper writing remains a formidable challenge. The Introduction writing is especially challenging, which demands not only linguistic fluency, but logical soundness and verifiable faithfulness. Most AI-assisted methods treat the task as text generation instead of reasoning and structuring, leading to severe drawbacks, e.g., hallucinating citations. To address this, we first formulate the Content-Conditional Introduction Generation (CCIG) task, which requires grounding the Introduction in the paper's core evidence. We then propose LECTOR, a novel Logic-Expression Co-Reinforcement Learning framework that can strictly follow the scientist's logic, add high-quality citations and keep structured expressions. LECTOR first constructs a logic-reasoning graph from the paper's main body to serve as a verifiable logical blueprint. Subsequently, it employs a Logic-Expression Co-Rewarding mechanism to jointly optimize for both the graph's structural fidelity and the final narrative's quality. We conduct a dataset from Nature Communications papers to assess our method. Extensive experiments show consistent improvements in both logic fidelity and Introduction generation quality metrics, e.g., Graph Quality (+26.7%), Citation Quality (+8.6%), and Paper Consistency (+3.3%). Code and data are available at https://github.com/Xiao-Youth/LECTOR.

</details>


### [219] [Neural Scalable Symbolic Search Framework for Complex Logical Queries with Multiple Free Variables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25985)
*Weizhi Fei,Hang Yin,Zihao Wang,Shukai Zhao,Wei Zhang,Yangqiu Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NS3 is a budgeted neural symbolic search framework that approximates joint ranking for EFO_k queries over incomplete KGs by merging variables into pruned hypernodes and progressively reducing query complexity, improving joint ranking performance while maintaining marginal accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current CQA methods rely on marginal rankings over individual variables, which serve as poor proxies for true joint rankings of answer tuples. Answering EFO_k queries with k free variables requires ranking tuples in E^k, which becomes intractable as k grows due to exponential enumeration.

Method: NS3 builds on neural symbolic search for EFO_1 queries and introduces: (i) answering marginalized sub-queries to obtain candidate sets, (ii) merging multiple free variables into hypernodes with domain pruning controlled by a dynamic budget B, and (iii) progressively reducing an EFO_k query to an EFO_{k-1} query over a budgeted reduced domain.

Result: Across three standard KG datasets, NS3 substantially improves joint ranking performance while retaining strong marginal accuracy. The paper also releases a joint-ranking benchmark extending existing EFO_1 datasets to k=3.

Conclusion: NS3 effectively addresses the tractability challenge of multi-variable query answering in KGs by providing a scalable approximation to joint ranking without full enumeration of E^k, with code publicly available.

Abstract: Complex Query Answering (CQA) is a fundamental knowledge representation and reasoning task over incomplete knowledge graphs (KGs). Answering existential first-order queries with $k$ free variables (i.e., $\text{EFO}_k$ queries) is a crucial yet challenging problem, as it requires ranking answer tuples in $\mathcal{E}^k$, where $\mathcal{E}$ denotes the entity set of a KG. This quickly becomes intractable as $k$ grows. Consequently, existing benchmarks and methods rely on marginal rankings over individual variables; however, marginal rankings are a poor proxy for the true joint ranking of tuples. Building on neural symbolic search for $\text{EFO}_1$ queries, we propose Neural Scalable Symbolic Search (NS3), a budgeted framework that approximates joint ranking without enumerating $\mathcal{E}^k$. NS3 (i) answers marginalized sub-queries to obtain necessary candidate sets, (ii) merges multiple free variables into hypernodes whose domains are pruned and controlled by a dynamic budget $B$, and (iii) progressively reduces an $\text{EFO}_k$ query to an $\text{EFO}_{k-1}$ query over a budgeted reduced domain. Across three standard KG datasets, NS3 substantially improves joint ranking performance while retaining strong marginal accuracy. We further release a joint-ranking benchmark that extends existing $\text{EFO}_1$ datasets to $k=3$, enabling systematic evaluation of multi-variable queries. Our code is provided in https://github.com/HKUST-KnowComp/NS3_KDD2026.

</details>


### [220] [CausaLab: A Scalable Environment for Interactive Causal Discovery Toward AI Scientists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26029)
*Junlin Yang,Dylan Zhang,Xiangchen Song,Qirun Dai,Xiao Liu,Yuen Chen,Aniket Vashishtha,Jing Shi,Chenhao Tan,Hao Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CausaLab is a scalable environment for evaluating interactive causal discovery by LLM agents, separating predictive success from causal understanding. Experiments show LLMs can achieve high prediction accuracy but struggle with correct causal mechanism recovery, though mixed observation-intervention strategies improve structural fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior evaluations of LLM-based causal reasoning conflate predictive success with genuine causal understanding. There is a need for a benchmark that explicitly separates whether an agent can solve a task from whether it has correctly inferred the underlying causal mechanism.

Method: CausaLab places agents in a synthetic laboratory environment where they receive prior measurement records, intervene on a manipulator crystal, and predict resonance frequencies. The hidden DGP is a randomly sampled SCM. A domain-specific language records agents' evolving SCM hypotheses, enabling trajectory inspection and comparison with ground truth.

Result: In the purely observational 6-node setting, GPT-5.2-high achieves 92% task accuracy but only 0.471 all-edge F₁. Mixed observation-intervention strategies improve structural fidelity to 80% on both metrics. Pure intervention strategies perform poorly on both metrics. Premature stopping is identified as a major weakness.

Conclusion: CausaLab reveals a persistent gap between task performance and causal mechanism recovery in LLM agents. Verification of hypothesis-data consistency can mitigate premature stopping, but current LLMs remain limited as experimental causal reasoners.

Abstract: We introduce CausaLab, a scalable environment for evaluating interactive causal discovery by LLM agents. Unlike prior evaluations, CausaLab evaluates both whether an agent can solve a problem using causal evidence and whether its answer is supported by a correct hypothesis about the underlying causal mechanism. Each episode places an agent in a synthetic laboratory: it receives prior measurement records, intervenes on a manipulator crystal, and predicts the resonance frequency of a held-out reactor crystal governed by the same mechanism. The hidden data-generating process is a randomly sampled structural causal model (SCM), so success requires recovering both a causal graph and structural equations rather than recalling prior knowledge. CausaLab also includes a domain-specific language that records the agent's evolving SCM hypothesis, making trajectories inspectable and comparable with ground truth. Experiments show a persistent gap between prediction and mechanism recovery: in the purely observational 6-node setting, GPT-5.2-high reaches 92% task accuracy but only 0.471 all-edge $F_1$. This observation further motivates our exploration of different interaction strategies: Mixed observation--intervention strategies improve structural fidelity: in the mixed 6-node setting, GPT-5.2-high achieves 80% on both task accuracy and all-edge $F_1$. Yet even strong agents struggle to design informative interventions, as pure intervention strategies perform poorly on both task accuracy and all-edge $F_1$. We identify premature stopping as a major weakness of agents, and show that asking the model to verify the consistency between its hypothesis and past data can help mitigate this issue. CausaLab therefore separates predictive success from causal understanding and exposes current LLM agents' limits as experimental causal reasoners.

</details>


### [221] [CITYREP: A Unified Benchmark for Urban Representations Across Cities, Tasks, and Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26036)
*Junyuan Liu,Xinglei Wang,Zichao Zeng,Jiazhuang Feng,Quan Qin,Ilya Ilyankou,Guangsheng Dong,Tao Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CityRep is a unified benchmark for evaluating urban representation learning across 8 cities, 8 tasks, and 11 models using spatially structured splits to mitigate spatial leakage and enable fair, generalizable comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of urban representation learning are limited to one or two cities/tasks and rely on random splits that introduce spatial leakage, inflating performance and hindering cross-location generalization and fair comparison.

Method: CityRep introduces (1) a spatial unit-agnostic evaluation framework with a standardized alignment module, (2) a unified protocol using block-based spatial splits to prevent spatial leakage, and (3) a multi-city, multi-task benchmark suite spanning 8 cities and 8 tasks (regression, classification, distribution prediction). 11 urban representation models are evaluated.

Result: Performance is highly sensitive to the split protocol: random splits inflate scores and alter model rankings. Substantial variability across cities and tasks is observed, highlighting the need for generalization-aware evaluation.

Conclusion: CityRep provides a reproducible benchmark with datasets, pipelines, and diagnostic tools to promote fair comparison and support progress toward urban foundation models.

Abstract: Urban representation learning encodes complex urban environments into general-purpose embeddings for diverse downstream tasks and emerging urban foundation models. However, current evaluations are limited, typically focusing on one or two cities and tasks and relying on random splits that introduce spatial leakage, leading to inflated performance and weak support for cross-location generalization and fair comparison. To address this, we propose CityRep, a unified benchmark that evaluates urban representations across data modalities, cities, and tasks using spatially structured splits. CityRep consists of three key components: (1) a spatial unit-agnostic evaluation framework that supports heterogeneous urban representations through a standardized alignment module; (2) a unified evaluation protocol using block-based spatial splits to mitigate spatial leakage and enable rigorous model comparison; and (3) an extensible multi-city, multi-task benchmark suite spanning 8 cities and 8 tasks across regression, classification, and distribution prediction. We evaluate 11 representative urban representation models. Results show that performance is highly sensitive to the split protocol, with random splits inflating scores and altering model rankings. We also observe substantial variability across cities and tasks, underscoring the need for generalization-aware evaluation. CityRep is released as a reproducible benchmark with datasets, evaluation pipelines, and diagnostic tools to facilitate fair comparison and support future research in urban representation learning towards urban foundation models.

</details>


### [222] [L2IR: Revealing Latent Intent in Graph Fraud Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26040)
*Jinsheng Guo,Zhenhao Weng,Yibo Liu,Yan Qiao,Meng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: L2IR is an LLM-driven framework that uncovers latent intent from user behaviors and connections to improve graph fraud detection under camouflaged conditions, achieving up to 8.27% AUPRC improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fraudsters disguise themselves by forging connections with benign users, diluting fraud signals during GNN aggregation. Existing LLM-based methods overlook the intent behind suspicious connections, and limited labeled data further hampers robust detection under heavy camouflage.

Method: L2IR extracts intent-aware representations from raw behavioral traces and reasons about the true purpose behind individual connections using LLMs. It distinguishes supportive links from misleading ones and incorporates adaptive self-training to improve robustness under limited supervision.

Result: L2IR surpasses strong baselines on two real-world camouflaged datasets and improves AUPRC by up to 8.27% when used as a plug-in enhancement for GNN-based detectors.

Conclusion: Revealing latent intent via LLMs effectively mitigates camouflage interference in graph fraud detection, and the framework can serve as a general plug-in to boost existing GNN detectors.

Abstract: Graph fraud detection has long depended on Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to propagate and aggregate information across relational data. A critical obstacle in practice, however, is that fraudsters frequently disguise themselves by forging numerous connections with benign users, causing fraud signals to be progressively diluted during neighborhood aggregation and undermining detection reliability. While recent efforts have used Large Language Models (LLMs) to provide rich semantic cues for fraud detection, the underlying intent behind suspicious connections remains insufficiently explored. Compounding this issue, the scarcity of annotated fraud samples makes it difficult to train detectors that remain robust under heavy camouflage. To address these gaps, we propose L2IR, an LLM-driven Latent Intent Revealing framework for graph fraud detection. By uncovering latent intent from both user behaviors and suspicious connections, L2IR extracts intent-aware representations from raw behavioral traces and reasons about the true purpose behind individual connections, effectively distinguishing supportive links from misleading ones. It further incorporates adaptive self-training to enhance robustness under limited supervision. Evaluations on two real-world datasets characterized by pervasive camouflage demonstrate that L2IR surpasses strong baselines and can function as a plug-in enhancement for a range of GNN-based detectors, improving AUPRC by up to 8.27%.

</details>


### [223] [Retrying vs Resampling in AI Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26047)
*James Lucassen,Adam Kaufman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies retrying and resampling techniques for AI coding scaffolds from an adversarial control perspective, finding that retrying can be exploited by untrusted models to create sneakier attacks, while resampling (drawing multiple samples per step) improves safety from 61% to 71% at minimal audit cost. Two key findings contradict prior work: using maximum suspicion scores outperforms minimum, and executing the least suspicious sample yields minimal safety gain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to analyze AI coding scaffold techniques (retrying and resampling) from an adversarial AI control perspective, treating the model as potentially adversarial, to understand their true safety implications and disentangle previously bundled design choices.

Method: The study uses BashArena with Claude Opus 4.6 as the untrusted model and MiMo-V2-Flash as the trusted monitor. They compare retrying (blocking risky actions) vs resampling (drawing multiple samples from the same context), evaluating different audit strategies including maximum vs minimum suspicion scores and selective resampling only on suspicious steps.

Result: Resampling raises safety from 61% to 71% at a 0.3% audit budget with no usefulness cost. Selective resampling recovers 6.2 percentage points of gain while drawing only 10% as many extra samples. Retrying reduces honest suspicion scores but allows models to exploit monitor rationale for sneakier attacks. Maximum-based auditing outperforms minimum-based (contradicting Ctrl-Z), and executing least-suspicious samples yields only +3.9 pp gain (overlapping zero).

Conclusion: Resampling is a safer technique than retrying for AI coding scaffolds as it doesn't leak exploitable information to untrusted models. The authors' findings contradict prior work on key design choices (max vs min auditing, defer-to-resample efficacy), highlighting the need for careful evaluation of these techniques under adversarial assumptions.

Abstract: AI coding scaffolds like Claude Code and Codex use \textit{retrying}: blocking actions flagged as risky and continuing the trajectory. We study retrying from an AI control perspective, which treats the model as potentially adversarial. We find that while retrying reduces honest suspicion scores, the untrusted model can exploit monitor rationale to construct sneakier attacks, negating safety gains. We also study \textit{resampling}: drawing multiple samples from the same context, which does not leak exploitable information. We disentangle design choices that previous work on resampling had bundled together. In BashArena, with Claude Opus 4.6 as the untrusted model and MiMo-V2-Flash as the trusted monitor, drawing five samples per step and auditing on the maximum suspicion score raises safety from 61\% to 71\% at a 0.3\% audit budget, at no cost to usefulness. Selectively resampling only the steps that look suspicious on the first draw recovers 6.2 percentage points of the gain while drawing only 10\% as many extra samples. Two of our findings in this setting contradict earlier work on resampling. The first is that auditing based on the maximum across resampled suspicion scores outperforms using the minimum, which is the opposite of what Ctrl-Z found. The second is that executing the least suspicious sample, which is the central mechanism in earlier defer-to-resample protocols, gives only a small empirical safety gain in our setting (+3.9 pp, with the confidence interval overlapping zero).

</details>


### [224] [VeriTrace: Evolving Mental Models for Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26081)
*Haolang Zhao,Yunbo Long,Lukas Beckenbauer,Alexandra Brintrup*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VeriTrace is a cognitive-graph framework that implements three explicit regulatory loops (interpretive update, deviation feedback, schema revision) to regulate how deep research agents evolve their mental models, improving over baselines by 4.22 pp on DRB Insight and achieving state-of-the-art open-source results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep research agents suffer from contaminated intermediate representations and error propagation due to lacking explicit regulation of their evolving mental models, where unregulated information mixing forces reliance on larger model scales as a substitute for proper reasoning structure.

Method: VeriTrace realizes three explicit regulatory loops—interpretive update, deviation feedback, and schema revision—within a cognitive-graph framework to continuously align the agent's task understanding with reality through explicit feedback mechanisms.

Result: With Qwen3.5-27B backbones, VeriTrace outperforms the strongest matched baseline by 4.22 pp on DRB Insight (1.49 pp Overall) and 5.9 pp Overall win rate on DeepConsult. With Config-DeepSeek, it achieves the strongest reproducible open-source result on DRB.

Conclusion: Explicit regulation of intermediate representations through structured feedback loops improves deep research agent performance and reduces reliance on model scale to compensate for reasoning deficiencies.

Abstract: Deep research agents face vast, interdependent, and pervasively uncertain information. Existing systems explore what evolving intermediate representations should look like, but leave their evolution to the LLM's implicit reasoning. Without explicit regulation, the intermediate layer is easily contaminated by mixed-quality information and propagates errors along its dependencies, so model scale often ends up substituting for absent regulation. We argue that an agent's mental model should instead evolve through explicit feedback that continuously aligns task understanding with reality, and identify three regulatory loops: interpretive update, deviation feedback, and schema revision. We realise this in VeriTrace, a cognitive-graph framework that explicitly implements the three loops. Using matched Qwen3.5-27B backbones, VeriTrace improves over the strongest matched baseline by 4.22 pp on DeepResearch Bench (DRB) Insight (1.49 pp Overall) and by 5.9 pp Overall win rate on DeepConsult. With Config-DeepSeek, it achieves the strongest reproducible open-source result on DRB.

</details>


### [225] [Claw-Anything: Benchmarking Always-On Personal Assistants with Broader Access to User's Digital World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26086)
*Yusong Lin,Xinyuan Liang,Haiyang Wang,Qipeng Gu,Siqi Cheng,Jiangui Chen,Shuzhe Wu,Feiyang Pan,Lue Fan,Sanyuan Zhao,Dandan Tu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Claw-Anything is a benchmark for always-on LLM agents that expands context to include long-horizon histories, interdependent services, and multi-device GUI/CLI interaction with realistic noise. GPT-5.5 scores only 34.5% pass@1, revealing a large capability gap, while a released automated data pipeline yields 2,000 training environments and 23.7% improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agents and benchmarks operate over narrow slices of user data, lacking the broad, always-on personal assistant context needed for context-sensitive reasoning and proactive assistance.

Method: Introduces Claw-Anything benchmark with three dimensions: long-horizon activity histories, interdependent backend services, and integrated GUI/CLI interaction across devices. Simulates months of user activity via multi-round event injection with realistic noise (irrelevant events, conflicting signals). Also provides an automated data-generation pipeline producing 2,000 training environments.

Result: GPT-5.5 achieves only 34.5% pass@1 on the benchmark, significantly lower than prior benchmarks. The automated data pipeline improves the base model by 23.7%.

Conclusion: Current LLM agents (e.g., GPT-5.5 at 34.5% pass@1) fall substantially short of the capabilities needed for always-on personal assistance. The proposed automated data pipeline yields 2,000 training environments and improves the base model by 23.7%.

Abstract: Large language model agents are increasingly envisioned as always-on personal assistants with access to anything relevant in the user's digital world. Yet current systems operate over only narrow slices of that world, limiting context-sensitive reasoning and effective assistance. Existing benchmarks similarly provide only partial user state and therefore fail to capture performance in such a broad, always-on setting. To address this gap, we introduce Claw-Anything, a benchmark that expands agent context along three dimensions: long-horizon activity histories, interdependent backend services, and integrated GUI and CLI interaction across multiple devices. To instantiate this setting, we simulate months of user activity through multi-round event injection, producing complex world states and realistic noise, including irrelevant events and conflicting signals. Agents must reason over rich contextual environments while remaining robust to such noise. This expanded scope also enables the evaluation of proactive assistance, requiring agents to anticipate user needs and deliver timely recommendations. Experiments show that GPT-5.5 achieves only 34.5% pass@1, substantially below prior benchmarks, underscoring a gap between current agent capabilities and the demands of always-on personal assistance. Alongside the benchmark, we release an automated data-generation pipeline that yields 2,000 training environments and improves the base model by 23.7%, demonstrating its utility of scalable data infrastructure.

</details>


### [226] [From Model Scaling to System Scaling: Scaling the Harness in Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26112)
*Shangding Gu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that the next major bottleneck in agentic AI is system scaling (the "harness") rather than model scaling alone, introducing the concept of treating the structured execution layer around foundation models as a first-class design object. The authors identify three core bottlenecks—context governance, trustworthy memory, and dynamic skill routing—and propose a research agenda for harness-level evaluation. They present CheetahClaws as a Python-native reference harness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of AI agents is model-centric, reducing agents to final-task success while treating memory, retrieval, tool use, orchestration, verification, and governance as secondary implementation details. This framing is inadequate because agent performance emerges from the interaction among multiple system components, not just the foundation model.

Method: The paper identifies three core bottlenecks: context governance, trustworthy memory, and dynamic skill routing, along with orchestration and governance mechanisms. It proposes a research agenda for harness-level benchmarks measuring trajectory quality, memory hygiene, context efficiency, communication fidelity, verification cost, and safe evolution. The authors develop CheetahClaws, a Python-native reference harness, and compare it with Claude Code and OpenClaw.

Result: Development of CheetahClaws as a reference harness and a comparative analysis with existing harnesses (Claude Code, OpenClaw). The paper establishes a framework for understanding and evaluating the agent harness as a first-class design object.

Conclusion: Future progress in agentic AI will depend as much on system design (scaling the harness) as on stronger foundation models. The harness—including memory substrate, context constructor, skill-routing layer, orchestration loop, and verification-and-governance layer—must be treated as a first-class object of design, evaluation, and optimization.

Abstract: This paper studies the next major bottleneck in agentic AI as system scaling, not only model scaling: the design of auditable, persistent, modular, and verifiable architectures around foundation models. We refer to this shift as scaling the harness: treating the structured execution layer around a foundation model as a first-class object of design, evaluation, and optimization. Although recent large language models enable agents to use tools, retrieve information, maintain memory, and execute long-horizon workflows, evaluation remains largely model-centric, often reducing agents to final-task success while treating memory, retrieval, tool use, orchestration, verification, and governance as secondary implementation details. This framing is increasingly inadequate because agent performance emerges from the interaction among the foundation model, memory substrate, context constructor, skill-routing layer, orchestration loop, and verification-and-governance layer. Together, these components form the agent harness, which translates model capability into long-horizon agent behavior. We study scaling the harness through three core bottlenecks: context governance, trustworthy memory, and dynamic skill routing, together with the orchestration and governance mechanisms that coordinate and constrain them. We further outline a research agenda for harness-level benchmarks that go beyond one-shot task success to measure trajectory quality, memory hygiene, context efficiency, communication fidelity, verification cost, and safe evolution over time. To make the discussion concrete, we develop CheetahClaws: https://github.com/SafeRL-Lab/cheetahclaws, a Python-native reference harness, and compare it with Claude Code and OpenClaw. Our main claim is that future progress in agentic AI will depend as much on system design as on stronger foundation models.

</details>
